Weak Distributive Laws between Monads of Continuous Valuations and of Non-Deterministic Choice
Jean Goubault-Larrecq

Abstract
We show that there is weak distributive law of the Smyth hyperspace monad 𝒬 𝖵 {\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}} (resp., the Hoare hyperspace monad ℋ 𝖵 {\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}} , resp. the monad 𝒫 ​ ℓ 𝖵 q \mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}} of quasi-lenses, resp. the monad 𝒫 ​ ℓ 𝒱 \mathcal{P}\ell_{\mathcal{V}} of lenses) over the continuous valuation monad 𝐕 {\mathbf{V}} , as well as over the subprobability valuation monad 𝐕 ≤ 1 {\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1} and the probability valuation monad 𝐕 1 {\mathbf{V}}_{1} , on the whole category 𝐓𝐨𝐩 \mathbf{Top} of topological spaces (resp., on certain full subcategories such as the category of locally compact spaces or of stably compact spaces). We show that the resulting weak composite monad is the author’s monad of superlinear previsions (resp., sublinear previsions, resp. forks), possibly subnormalized or normalized depending on whether we consider 𝐕 ≤ 1 {\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1} or 𝐕 1 {\mathbf{V}}_{1} instead of 𝐕 {\mathbf{V}} . As a special case, we obtain a weak distributive law of the monad 𝒫 ​ ℓ 𝖵 q ≅ 𝒫 ​ ℓ 𝒱 \mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}\cong\mathcal{P}\ell_{\mathcal{V}} over the monad of (sub)probability Radon measures 𝐑 ∙ \mathbf{R}_{\bullet} on the category of stably compact spaces, which specializes further to a weak distributive laws of the Vietoris monad over 𝐑 ∙ \mathbf{R}_{\bullet} . The associated weak composite monad is the monad of (sub)normalized forks.



Journal:
Topology and its Applications

2020 MSC
18C15
, 54B20 , 28A33 , 46E27

For the purpose of Open Access, a CC-BY public copyright licence has
been applied by the authors to the present document and will be
applied to all subsequent versions up to the Author Accepted
Manuscript arising from this submission.

Une montagne en mal d’enfant

Jetait une clameur si haute

Que chacun, au bruit accourant,

Crut qu’elle accoucherait, sans faute,

D’une cité plus grosse que Paris;

Elle accoucha d’une souris.

Quand je songe à cette fable,

Dont le récit est menteur

Et le sens est véritable,

Je me figure un auteur

Qui dit: Je chanterai la guerre

Que firent les Titans au Maître du tonnerre. »

C’est promettre beaucoup: mais qu’en sort-il souvent?

Du vent.

La montagne qui accouche
,
Jean de la Fontaine, Fables, livre V, fable 10, 1668.

1
Introduction

Jon Beck introduced the notion of distributive laws

\lambda\colon TS\to ST
of a monad
S
over a monad
T
, and showed
that any such distributive law allowed for the construction of a
composite monad
ST

[
6
]
. The theory is much richer
than what the latter may suggest, and there is a one-to-one
correspondence between distributive laws as above, extensions of
T

to the Kleisli category of
S
, and liftings of
S
to the category of

T
-algebras.

It was once dreamed that one could perhaps use this construction in
order to define new monads, combining various side-effects in the
semantics of programming languages. But distributive laws are rare,
and, as a case in point, there is no distributive law of the monad of
non-deterministic choice over the monad of discrete probability
measure on
\mathbf{Set}

[
45
]
. The same argument shows that
there is no distributive law of any of the monads of non-deterministic
choice (the Smyth powerdomain monad
{\mathcal{Q}}
, the Hoare powerdomain
monad
{\mathcal{H}}
, the Plotkin powerdomain monad
\mathcal{P}\ell
) over the
monad
{\mathbf{V}}
of continuous valuations (or
{\mathbf{V}}_{1}
[probability
valuations], or
{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1}
[subprobability valuations],
implementing probabilistic choice).

Gabriella Böhm was the first to study weakenings of the notion of
distributive law between monads
[
8
]
, in a general

2
-categorical context. Given two monads
S
and
T
, a distributive
law
\lambda\colon TS\to ST
is a natural transformation satisfying
four equations expressing compatibility with the units and
multiplications of each of the two monads. Weak distributive laws are
obtained by dropping one or the other of those equations. Richard
Garner then observed that one of these kinds of weak distributive
laws, where compatibility with the unit of the
T
monad is ignored,
could be applied to algebraic-effect type monads

[
12
]
. This is the kind that we are interested in
here.

The theory of weak distributive law parallels Beck’s theory of
distributive laws, and notably, weak distributive laws of
S
over
T

are in one-to-one correspondence with weak extensions of
T
to the
Kleisli category of
S
, and weak liftings of
S
to the category of

T
-algebras.

It was suggested by Alexandre Goy
[
24
, Chapter 7]
that,
while there is no distributive law of the Vietoris monad (which sends
every space
X
to its space of non-empty compact subsets with the
Vietoris topology) over the monad
\mathbf{R}_{1}
of Radon probability
measures on the category
\mathbf{KHaus}
of compact Hausdorff spaces,
there should be a weak distributive law relating the two. We will
confirm that it exists, as a special case of a weak distributive law
between the Plotkin hyperspace monad
\mathcal{P}\ell_{\mathcal{V}}
over

{\mathbf{V}}_{1}\cong\mathbf{R}_{1}
, in the final section of this paper
(Section
12
). (A toy version of this result was
established by Goy and Petrişan
[
25
]
, on the category

\mathbf{Set}
, with the powerset monad instead of the Vietoris monad and
the finite distribution monad instead of
\mathbf{R}_{1}
.)

We will show that the resulting weak composite monad is the author’s
monad of normalized forks, introduced thirteen years earlier

[
14
]
on the category of dcpos. We take the opportunity to
make the relatively obvious generalization to topological spaces of
that monad here.

Before we reach that final section on the Vietoris and Radon monads,
we will explore essentially all possible combinations of monads of
non-deterministic choice on
\mathbf{Top}
on the one hand, and of monads
of probabilistic choice on the other hand. The latter include the
monads
{\mathbf{V}}
of continuous valuations,
{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1}
of
subprobability valuations,
{\mathbf{V}}_{1}
of probability valuations.
Continuous valuations are a close cousin to measures, and we will soon
explain how they relate to each other. The former include the Smyth
hyperspace monad
{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}
(demonic non-determinism) the Hoare hyperspace
monad
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}
(angelic non-determinism), and two variants of the
Plotkinn hyperspace monad (erratic non-determinism), the monad

\mathcal{P}\ell_{\mathcal{V}}
of lenses and the monad
\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}
of quasi-lenses. We show
that all of those have weak distributivity laws over
{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}
,
where
\bullet
is nothing, “
\leq 1
”, or “
1
”. In the case of

{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}
, this works on the whole category
\mathbf{Top}
of topological
spaces. For
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}
, we have to restrict to certain full subcategories,
such as the subcategory of locally compact spaces. For
\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}
and

\mathcal{P}\ell_{\mathcal{V}}
, we need to restrict even further, typically to stably
compact spaces—a category that still contains
\mathbf{KHaus}
as a
proper subcategory.

In each case, we make the resulting weak composite monad explicit.
And, in each case, that is a well-known monad: the monad of
superlinear (resp., subnormalized or normalized, depending on

\bullet
) previsions in the demonic case, the monad of sublinear
(resp., subnormalized or normalized) previsions in the angelic case,
and the monad of (resp., subnormalized or normalized) forks in the
erratic case. Those monads first appeared in
[
14
]
on the
category of dcpos, and we will rely on the (pretty obvious)
generalization to
\mathbf{Top}
here. “Tout ça pour ça!”, as we
say in French; in a more elegant form—and written in La Fontaine’s
admirable style—see the quote before this introduction. At least
this study should confirm that Garner definitely hit upon a, perhaps
the, right way of combining monads.

Outline.
Section
2
sets some technical
preliminaries. We will introduce other notions and results along the
way. The rest of the paper is structured in three parts, for each
version of non-determinism (demonic, angelic, erratic) we consider.
Each part has exactly the same structure, except for the last one,
which has an extra, final Section
12
on Radon
measures and the Vietoris monad. Explicitly, that structure consists
of one section on the monad we will eventually discover as the weak
composite monad (superlinear previsions in
Section
3
, sublinear previsions in
Section
6
, forks in
Section
9
), one on the weak distributive law
(Section
4
,
Section
7
,
Section
10
), and one on the resulting weak
composite monad (Section
5
,
Section
8
,
Section
11
).

2
Preliminaries

For background on topology, we refer the reader to

[
16
]
. We write
\text{int}({A})
for the interior of
A
,

cl(A)
(or
cl_{X}(A)
) for the closure of
A
(in a space
X
), and

{\mathcal{O}}X
for the lattice of open subsets of
X
. The specialization
preordering
\leq
of a topological space
X
is defined on points

x,y\in X
by
x\leq y
if and only if every open neighborhood of

x
contains
y
, if and only if
x
lies in the closure of
\{y\}
.
We also write
x\in A\mapsto f(x)
for the function that maps every
element
x
of a set
A
to the value
f(x)
; logicians and computer
scientists would use the notation
\lambda x\in A.f(x)
, but this
would interfere with the standard use of the letter
\lambda
for
(weak) distributive laws.

A
directed
family of elements in a poset is a non-empty family
of elements
D
such that any two elements of
D
have an upper bound
in
D
. A
filtered
family is defined similarly, with lower
bounds replacing upper bounds. We use
\sup\nolimits^{\scriptstyle\uparrow}
for suprema of directed
families,
\bigcup\nolimits^{\scriptstyle\uparrow}
for unions of directed families of sets,
\inf\nolimits^{\scriptstyle\downarrow}
for
filtered infima,
\bigcap\nolimits^{\scriptstyle\downarrow}
for intersections of filtered families of
sets.

An
irreducible
closed subset
C
of
X
is a non-empty closed
subset such that, for any two closed subsets
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
of
X

such that
C\subseteq C_{1}\cup C_{2}
,
C
is included in
C_{1}
or in

C_{2}
already; equivalently, if
C
intersects two open sets, it must
intersect their intersection. A space
X
is
sober
if and only
if it is
T_{0}
and every irreducible closed subset is of the form

\dc x
for some point
x\in X
. Every Hausdorff space, for example,
is sober. The notation
\dc x
stands for the
downward closure

of
x
in
X
, namely the set of points
y
below
x
. Symmetrically,

\upc x
stands for the
upward closure
of
x
, namely the set
of points
y
above
x
. This notation extends to
\upc A
, for any
subset
A
, denoting
\bigcup_{x\in A}\upc x
.

A
compact
subset
A
of a space
X
is one such that one can
extract a finite subcover from any of its open covers. No separation
property is assumed. A subset
A
of
X

saturated
if and only
if it is equal to the intersection of its open neighborhoods, or
equivalently if and only if it is upwards-closed in the specialization
preordering of
X
.

A space
X
is
locally compact
if and only if every point has a
base of compact neighborhoods, or equivalently of compact saturated
neighborhoods, since for any compact subset
K
of
X
, the upward
closure
\upc K
of
K
with respect to the specialization preordering
of
X
is compact saturated. Please beware that, in non-Hausdorff
spaces, a compact space may fail to be locally compact.

A space is
coherent
if and only if the intersection of any two
compact saturated subsets is compact (and necessarily saturated).
That, too, is a property that may fail in non-Hausdorff spaces.

A
stably locally compact
space is a coherent, locally compact,
sober space, and a
stably compact
is a compact, stably locally
compact space. We note that every compact Hausdorff space is stably
compact, as well as any coherent continuous dcpo with a least element.
(We will not define dcpos; see
[
16
]
,
[
1
]
,
or
[
13
]
.)

Let
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
be the set of extended non-negative real numbers

\mathbb{R}_{+}\cup\{\infty\}
, with its usual ordering. When needed, we will
consider it with its Scott topology, whose open sets are the intervals

]t,\infty]
,
t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
, plus
\emptyset
and
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
itself. In
general, the
Scott topology
on a poset has as open sets the
upward closed subsets
U
(i.e.,
U=\upc U
) such that every
directed family
D
whose supremum exists and is in
U
intersects

U
.

A
continuous valuation
on a space
X
is a map

\nu\colon{\mathcal{O}}X\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
that is
strict

(
\nu(\emptyset)=0
),
modular
(for all
U,V\in{\mathcal{O}}X
,

\nu(U)+\nu(V)=\nu(U\cup V)+\nu(U\cap V)
) and
Scott-continuous. We say that
\nu
is a
probability
valuation if and only if

\nu(X)=1
, and a
subprobability
valuation if and only if

\nu(X)\leq 1
.

Let
{\mathbf{V}}X
denote the space of continuous valuations on a space
X
,
with the following
weak topology
. That is defined by subbasic
open sets
[U>r]\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\{\nu\in{\mathbf{V}}X\mid\nu(U)>r\}
, where

U\in{\mathcal{O}}X
and
r\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
. We define its subspace
{\mathbf{V}}_{1}X
of
probability valuations and
{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1}X
(subprobability)
similarly. In general, we will write
{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X
, where

\bullet
is nothing, “
\leq 1
”, or “
1
”.

The specialization ordering of each is the
stochastic ordering

\leq
given by
\nu\leq\nu^{\prime}
if and only if
\nu(U)\leq\nu^{\prime}(U)

for every
U\in{\mathcal{O}}X
; indeed,
\nu\leq\nu^{\prime}
if and only if for
every
U\in{\mathcal{O}}X
, for every
r\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
,
\nu\in[U>r]

implies
\nu^{\prime}\in[U>r]
.

Continuous valuations are an alternative to measures that have become
popular in domain theory
[
30
,
29
]
. The first
results that connected continuous valuations and measures are due to
Saheb-Djahromi
[
40
]
and Lawson

[
36
]
. The current state of the art on similar
results is the following. In one direction, every measure on the
Borel
\sigma
-algebra of
X
induces a continuous valuation on
X
by
restriction to the open sets, if
X
is hereditarily Lindelöf
(namely, if every directed family of open sets contains a cofinal
monotone sequence). This is an easy observation, and one half of
Adamski’s theorem
[
2
, Theorem 3.1]
, which states
that a space is hereditary Lindelöf if and only if every measure on
its Borel
\sigma
-algebra restricts to a continuous valuation on its
open sets. In the other direction, every continuous valuation on a
space
X
extends to a measure on the Borel sets provided that
X
is
an LCS-complete space
[
10
, Theorem 1]
. An

LCS-complete
space is a space homeomorphic to a
G_{\delta}

subset of a locally compact sober space—
G_{\delta}
meaning a
countable intersection of open sets.

There is a monad
({\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet},\eta,\mu)
on
\mathbf{Top}
. For every
continuous map
f\colon X\to Y
,
{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}f
sends every

\nu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X
to the
pushforward
continuous
valuation
f[\nu]
, defined by
f[\nu](V)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\nu(f^{-1}(V))

for every
V\in{\mathcal{O}}Y
. The unit
\eta
is defined by

\eta_{X}(x)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\delta_{x}
for every
x\in X
, for every space
X
;

\delta_{x}
is the
Dirac valuation
at
x
, and maps every open
subset
U
of
X
to
1
if
x\in U
, to
0
otherwise. For every
continuous map
f\colon X\to{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}Y
, its
extension

f^{\dagger}\colon{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X\to{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}Y
is defined by

f^{\dagger}(\nu)(V)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\int_{x\in X}f(x)(V)\,d\nu
for every

\nu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X
and for every
V\in{\mathcal{O}}Y
, and then

\mu_{X}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\mathrm{id}_{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}^{\dagger}
. Integration is
best defined by a Choquet formula
[
44
, Section 4]
.
Explicitly, let
\mathcal{L}X
be the set of all lower semicontinuous
functions
h
from
X
to
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, that is, the set of all continuous
maps from
X
to
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
equipped with its Scott topology. For every

h\in\mathcal{L}X
,
\int_{x\in X}h(x)\,d\nu
is defined as the
indefinite Riemann integral

\int_{0}^{\infty}\nu(h^{-1}(]t,\infty]))\,dt
. The following

change of variable
formula follows immediately: for every
continuous map
f\colon X\to Y
, for every
h\in\mathcal{L}Y
, for
every
\nu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X
,

\int_{y\in Y}h(y)\,df[\nu]=\int_{x\in X}h(f(x))\,d\nu
.
We also note that, for every
h\in\mathcal{L}X
, for every
\xi\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}
,

\displaystyle\int_{x\in X}h(x)\,d\mu_{X}(\xi)

\displaystyle=\int_{\nu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}\left(\int_{x\in X}h(x)\,d\nu\right)\,d\xi

See
[
35
, Hilfssatz 6.1]
for a proof.

The Choquet formula makes sense when
\nu
is not just a continuous
valuation, but any monotone map from
{\mathcal{O}}X
to
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, and we
will use that observation later on.

It will be useful to note that
\eta_{X}^{-1}([U>r])=U
if
r<1

and is empty otherwise, that

{({\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}f)}^{-1}([V>r])=[f^{-1}(V)>r]
. The formula
for
{(\mu_{X})}^{-1}([U>r])
is more involved. For every

h\in\mathcal{L}X
, for every
r\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
, let

[h>r]\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\{\nu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X\mid\int_{x\in X}h(x)\,d\nu>r\}
. This is also an open subset of
{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X
, and
such sets form an alternate base of the weak topology, when
h
varies
over
\mathcal{L}X
and
r
over
\mathbb{R}_{+}
, see

[
31
, Theorem 3.3]
where this was proved for spaces of
probability and subprobability valuations; the proof is similar for
arbitrary continuous valuations. We may even restrict
r
to be
non-zero, since
[h>0]=\bigcup_{r>0}[h>r]
, and in fact to be
equal to
1
since
[h>r]=[h/r>1]
when
r>0
. The point is
that

{(\mu_{X})}^{-1}([U>r])=[(\nu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X\mapsto\nu(U))>r]
, and more generally:

\displaystyle{(\mu_{X})}^{-1}([h>r])

\displaystyle=\left[\left(\nu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X\mapsto\int_{x\in X}h(x)\,d\nu\right)>r\right].

(1)

We will also need to note that

{({\mathbf{V}}f)}^{-1}([h>r])=[h\circ f>r]
, a consequence of the
change of variable formula, and that

\eta_{X}^{-1}([h>r])=h^{-1}(]r,\infty])
.

Part I
A weak distributive law between
{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}
and
{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}

For every topological space
X
, let
{\mathcal{Q}}X
be the set of
non-empty compact saturated subsets of
X
. The
upper Vietoris

topology on that set has basic open subsets
\Box U
consisting of
those non-empty compact saturated subsets of
X
that are included in

U
, where
U
ranges over the open subsets of
X
. We write
{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X

for the resulting topological space. Its specialization ordering is
reverse inclusion
\supseteq
.

The
{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}
constructions was studied by a number of people, starting
with Mike Smyth
[
42
]
, and later by Andrea Schalk

[
41
, Section 7]
who studied not only this, but also the
variant with the Scott topology, and a localic counterpart. See also

[
1
, Sections 6.2.2, 6.2.3]
or

[
13
, Section IV-8]
, where the accent is rather on
the Scott topology of
\supseteq
. In this setting,
{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}
is a model
of
demonic
non-determinism.

There is a monad
({\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}},\eta^{\mathcal{Q}},\mu^{\mathcal{Q}})
on
\mathbf{Top}
. For
every continuous map
f\colon X\to Y
,
{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}f
maps every

Q\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X
to
\upc f[Q]
, where
f[Q]
denotes

\{f(x)\mid x\in Q\}
. The unit
\eta^{\mathcal{Q}}
is defined by

\eta^{\mathcal{Q}}_{X}(x)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\upc x
for every
x\in X
. For every
continuous map
f\colon X\to{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}Y
, there is an extension

f^{\sharp}\colon{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X\to{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}Y
, defined by

f^{\sharp}(Q)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\bigcup_{x\in Q}f(x)
. The multiplication

\mu^{\mathcal{Q}}
is defined by

\mu^{\mathcal{Q}}_{X}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\mathrm{id}_{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}^{\sharp}
, namely

\mu^{\mathcal{Q}}_{X}(\mathcal{Q})\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\bigcup\mathcal{Q}

[
41
, Proposition 7.21]
.

We note the equalities
{\eta^{\mathcal{Q}}}^{-1}(\Box U)=U
,

{({\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}f)}^{-1}(\Box V)=\Box{f^{-1}(V)}
, and

{\mu^{\mathcal{Q}}}^{-1}(\Box U)=\Box{\Box U}
.

3
The monad of superlinear previsions

We will show that there is a weak distributive law of
{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}
of

{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}
, and we will build the resulting weak composite monad.
That monad has been well-known for some time, and is the monad of
superlinear previsions of
[
14
]
, except that this paper
studied it on the category of dcpos and Scott-continuous maps instead
of
\mathbf{Top}
; the study of superlinear previsions on
\mathbf{Top}
was
initiated in
[
17
]
, but it was not stated that they formed
a monad there, although, as we will see below, this is pretty
elementary.

At any rate, we will need to learn quite a number of facts about
superlinear previsions in our endeavor to build the desired weak
distributive law and its resulting weak composite monad.

We recall that
\mathcal{L}X
is the space of lower semicontinuous maps
from
X
to
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
. We equip it with the Scott topology of the
pointwise ordering. A
prevision
on
X
is a Scott-continuous
functional
F\colon\mathcal{L}X\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
such that
F(ah)=aF(h)
for
all
a\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
and
h\in\mathcal{L}X
. It is
linear
(resp.,

superlinear
) if and only if
F(h+h^{\prime})=F(h)+F(h^{\prime})
(resp.,

\geq
) for all
h,h^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}X
. It is
subnormalized

(resp.,
normalized
) iff
F(\mathbf{1}+h)\leq\mathbf{1}+F(h)
(resp.,

=
) for every
h\in\mathcal{L}X
, where
\mathbf{1}
is the constant function
with value
1
. We write
\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}X
for the space of superlinear
previsions on
X
,
\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\leq 1}X
for its subspace of
subnormalized superlinear previsions, and
\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{1}X
for its
subspace of normalized superlinear previsions; synthetically, we use
the notation
\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X
. Similarly, we denote by

\mathbb{P}_{\mathtt{P}}^{\bullet}X
the corresponding spaces of linear
previsions. The topology on each of those spaces is generated by
subbasic open sets

[h>r]\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\{F\in\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X\text{ (resp.,
$\mathbb{P}_{\mathtt{P}}^{\bullet}X$)}\mid F(h)>r\}
,
h\in\mathcal{L}X
,

r\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
. Its specialization ordering is the pointwise ordering:

F\leq F^{\prime}
if and only if
F(h)\leq F(h^{\prime})
for every

h\in\mathcal{L}X
.

There is a
\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}
endofunctor on
\mathbf{Top}
, and its
action on morphisms
f\colon X\to Y
is given by

\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}f(F)=(h\in\mathcal{L}Y\mapsto F(h\circ f))
.
The
\mathbb{P}_{\mathtt{P}}^{\bullet}
endofunctor is defined similarly. Those
are both functor parts of monads. Those monads, as well as the
forthcoming monad of sublinear previsions, were made explicit in

[
14
, Proposition 2]
, on the category of dcpos (resp.,
pointed dcpos) and Scott-continuous maps, except that spaces of
previsions were given the Scott topology of the pointwise ordering.
The formulae for unit and multiplication is the same below. The proof
that those form monads is elementary, and the only change compared to

[
14
]
is how we show continuity.

Proposition 3.1

Let
\bullet
be nothing, “
\leq 1
”, or “
1
”. There is a
monad

(\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet},\allowbreak\eta^{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}},\allowbreak\mu^{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}})

(resp.,

(\mathbb{P}_{\mathtt{P}}^{\bullet},\allowbreak\eta^{\mathtt{P}},\allowbreak\mu^{\mathtt{P}})
) on
\mathbf{Top}
, whose unit and multiplication are
defined at every topological space
X
by:

•

for every
x\in X
,
\eta^{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}_{X}(x)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}(h\in\mathcal{L}X\mapsto h(x))
,

•

for every

\mathcal{F}\in\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}
(resp.,
in
\mathbb{P}_{\mathtt{P}}^{\bullet}{\mathbb{P}_{\mathtt{P}}^{\bullet}X}
),

\mu^{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}_{X}(\mathcal{F})\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}(h\in\mathcal{L}X\mapsto\mathcal{F}(F\mapsto F(h)))
, where
F
ranges over
\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X
,
resp.
\mathbb{P}_{\mathtt{P}}^{\bullet}X
.

Proof 1

Unit. The functional
(h\in\mathcal{L}X\mapsto h(x))
is a linear
prevision, hence also a superlinear prevision, as one checks easily.
It is also normalized, hence subnormalized. The map
\eta^{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}_{X}
is
continuous, since the inverse image of
[h>r]
is

h^{-1}(]r,\infty])
.

Multiplication. For every
h\in\mathcal{L}X
, the map

F\mapsto F(h)
is lower semicontinuous, since the inverse image
of
]r,\infty]
is
[h>r]
. Hence
\mathcal{F}(F\mapsto F(h))

makes sense. The map

(h\in\mathcal{L}X\mapsto\mathcal{F}(F\mapsto F(h)))
is
Scott-continuous, because
\mathcal{F}
is. Additionally, when

\mathcal{F}
is superlinear (resp., linear), and
F
ranges over

\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X
, resp.
\mathbb{P}_{\mathtt{P}}^{\bullet}X
, it is clear
that
(h\in\mathcal{L}X\mapsto\mathcal{F}(F\mapsto F(h)))
is also
superlinear (resp., linear). Hence
\mu^{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}_{X}
is a superlinear
(resp., linear) prevision. When
\bullet
is “
\leq 1
”, namely
if
\mathcal{F}
is subnormalized and
F
ranges over

\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\leq 1}X
, then for every
h\in\mathcal{L}X
,

\mu^{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}_{X}(\mathbf{1}+h)=\mathcal{F}(F\mapsto F(\mathbf{1}+h))\leq\mathcal{F}(F\mapsto 1+F(h))\leq 1+\mathcal{F}(F\mapsto F(h))=1+\mu^{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}_{X}(h)
, so
\mu^{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}_{X}(\mathcal{F})
is
subnormalized; similarly, if
\mathcal{F}
is normalized, then

\mu^{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}_{X}(\mathcal{F})
is normalized. (And yes, the dependency on

\bullet
should be made explicit in the notation
\mu^{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}_{X}
, but
we feel that the notation is heavy enough.) Finally,
\mu^{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}_{X}
is
continuous since the inverse image of
[h>r]
is

[(F\mapsto F(h))>r]
.

The monad laws are easily verified. In fact, they are verified
exactly as for the continuation monad, which is defined with the
same formulae. ∎

There is a homeomorphism between
\mathbb{P}_{\mathtt{P}}^{\bullet}X
and

{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X
, for every space
X
: in one direction,

\nu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X
is mapped to

(h\in\mathcal{L}X\mapsto\int_{x\in X}h(x)\,d\nu)
, and in the
other direction any
G\in\mathbb{P}_{\mathtt{P}}^{\bullet}X
is mapped to

(U\in{\mathcal{O}}X\mapsto G(\chi_{U}))
, where
\chi_{U}
is the
characteristic map of
U
. We note in particular that

\int_{x\in X}\chi_{U}(x)\,d\nu=\nu(U)
. This was first proved
by Kirch
[
35
, Satz 8.1]
, under the additional assumption
that
X
is core-compact; Tix later observed that this assumption was
unnecessary
[
44
, Satz 4.16]
.

4
The weak distributive law

We will show that there is a weak distributive law of
{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}
over

{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}
on the whole category of topological spaces.
This will be the collection of maps
{\lambda^{\sharp}_{X}}
given as follows.

Proposition 4.1
(Corollary 12.6 of
[
23
]
)

For every topological space
X
, there is a continuous map

{\lambda^{\sharp}_{X}}
from
{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}
to
{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}
,
which maps every
\mu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}
to the collection of
continuous valuations
\nu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X
such that

\nu(U)\geq\mu(\Box U)
for every
U\in{\mathcal{O}}X
.

We will also need to know how this is proved.

There is a map

r_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}\colon{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}({\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X)\to\mathbb{P}_{{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}}^{\bullet}X
, defined
by

r_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}(Q)(h)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\min_{\nu\in Q}\allowbreak\int_{x\in X}h(x)\,d\nu
, and a map

s_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}\colon\mathbb{P}_{{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}}^{\bullet}X\to{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}({\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X)

defined by

s_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}(F)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\{\nu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X\mid\forall h\in\mathcal{L}X,\int_{x\in X}h(x)\,d\nu\geq F(h)\}
. Both are
continuous, and they form a retraction:

r_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}\circ s_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}=\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{P}_{{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}}^{\bullet}X}
. This
is the contents of Proposition 3.22 of
[
17
]
, only
replacing the space
\mathbb{P}_{\mathtt{P}}^{\bullet}X
used there by

{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X
, since the two are homeomorphic. Additionally,

r_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}
and
s_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}
are natural in
X

[
23
, Lemma
11.2]
.

This retraction even cuts down to a homeomorphism between

{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{cvx}({\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X)
and
\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X

[
17
, Theorem 4.15]
, where the former denotes the subspace
of
{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}({\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X)
consisting of convex non-empty compact
saturated subsets of
{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X
. (A subset
A
of the latter is

convex
if and only if for all
G_{1},G_{2}\in A
, for every

r\in[0,1]
,
rG_{1}+(1-r)G_{2}\in A
.)

Then, there is a continuous map

\Phi\colon{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}\to\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X
defined by

\Phi(\mu)(h)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\int_{Q\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}\min_{x\in Q}h(x)\,d\mu

for every
h\in\mathcal{L}X

[
23
, Lemma 12.5]
, and
the proof of Corollary 12.6 of
[
23
]
builds

{\lambda^{\sharp}_{X}}
as
s_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}\circ\Phi
.

Fact 4.2

{\lambda^{\sharp}_{X}}=s_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}\circ\Phi
.

Remark 4.3

In other words, for every
\mu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}
,

\lambda^{\sharp}_{X}(\mu)
is also the set
\{\nu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X\mid\forall h\in\mathcal{L}X,\int_{x\in X}h(x)\,d\nu\geq\int_{Q\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}\min_{x\in Q}h(x)\,d\mu\}
.

In order to illustrate what
{\lambda^{\sharp}_{X}}
does, let us look
at the special case where

\mu\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\delta_{Q_{i}}
, where
n\geq 1
,

Q_{i}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\upc E_{i}
, and where each set
E_{i}
is non-empty and
finite. For every
h\in\mathcal{L}X
,

\Phi(\mu)(h)=\int_{Q\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}\min_{x\in Q}h(x)\,d\mu=\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\min_{x_{i}\in Q_{i}}h(x_{i})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\min_{x_{i}\in E_{i}}h(x_{i})=\min_{x_{1}\in E_{1},\cdots,x_{n}\in E_{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}h(x_{i})=\min_{x_{1}\in E_{1},\cdots,x_{n}\in E_{n}}\allowbreak\int_{x\in X}h(x)\,d\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\delta_{x_{i}}
,
so
\Phi=r_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}(\upc\mathcal{E})
where
\mathcal{E}
is the finite
set

\{\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\delta_{x_{i}}\mid x_{1}\in E_{1},\cdots,x_{n}\in E_{n}\}
. By
[
17
, Proposition 4.20]
,

s_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}\circ r_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}
maps every compact saturated subset
Q

of
{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X
to its compact saturated convex hull, namely to

\upc conv(Q)
, where
conv
denotes convex hull. Hence

{\lambda^{\sharp}_{X}}(\mu)=\upc conv(\{\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\delta_{x_{i}}\mid x_{1}\in E_{1},\cdots,x_{n}\in E_{n}\})
.

All those maps are continuous. For
\Phi
, we have the following.

Fact 4.4

For all
h\in\mathcal{L}X
and
r\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
,

\Phi^{-1}([h>r])=[h^{*}>r]
where
h^{*}(Q)
is defined as

\min_{x\in Q}h(x)
for every
Q\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X
. The map
h^{*}
is in

\mathcal{L}{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}
, since for every
t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
,

{h^{*}}^{-1}(]t,\infty])=\Box{h^{-1}(]t,\infty])}
.

Fact 4.5

For every
h\in\mathcal{L}X
,
{(r_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}})}^{-1}([h>1])=\Box{[h>1]}
.

The proof that
s_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}
is continuous is complex

[
17
, Lemma 3.20]
. We produce a simpler proof in
Lemma
4.6
below, which additionally describes the
shape of inverse images of (sub)basic open sets by that map. We note
that a base of open subsets of
{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}({\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X)
is given by sets
of the form

\Box{\bigcup_{i\in I}\bigcap_{j=1}^{n_{i}}[U_{ij}>r_{ij}]}
.
Since
\Box
commutes with directed unions and

\bigcup_{i\in I}\mathcal{U}_{i}=\sup\nolimits^{\scriptstyle\uparrow}_{J\in\mathbb{P}_{\mathrm{fin}}(I)}\bigcup_{i\in J}\mathcal{U}_{i}
, since finite unions and finite intersections
distribute, and since
\Box
commutes with finite intersections, a
simpler base is given by open subsets of the form

\Box{\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}[U_{i}>r_{i}]}
, where each
U_{i}
is open in
X

and
r_{i}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
. More generally, we consider open subsets of the
form
\Box{\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}[h_{i}>r_{i}]}
where each
h_{i}
is in

\mathcal{L}X
and
r_{i}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\smallsetminus\{0\}
. We may even restrict them
to
n\geq 1
, since
\Box\emptyset
is empty. Since

[h_{i}>r_{i}]=[h_{i}/r_{i}>1]
, we also restrict
r_{i}
to be equal to

1
. Let

\Delta_{n}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\{(t_{1},\cdots,t_{n})\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{n}\mid\sum_{i=1}^{n}t_{i}=1\}
. We write
\vec{a}
for the elements of
\Delta_{n}
;
and
a_{i}
for component number
i
of
\vec{a}
.

Lemma 4.6

For every
n\geq 1
, for all
h_{1},\cdots,h_{n}\in\mathcal{L}X
,

\displaystyle{(s_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet})}^{-1}(\Box{(\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}[h_{i}>1])})

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{\vec{a}\in\Delta_{n}}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}h_{i}>1\right].

Proof 2

Let

F\in\bigcup_{\vec{a}\in\Delta_{n}}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}h_{i}>1\right]
. Then
F(\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}h_{i})>1
for some

\vec{a}\in\Delta_{n}
. For every
\nu\in s_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}(F)
, we
then have

\int_{x\in X}\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}h_{i}(x)\,d\nu\geq F(\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}h_{i})>1
. It follows that
\int_{i=1}^{n}h_{i}(x)\,d\nu>1

for some
i\in\{1,\cdots,n\}
: otherwise

\int_{i=1}^{n}h_{i}(x)\,d\nu\leq 1
for every
i
, so

\int_{x\in X}\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}h_{i}(x)\,d\nu\leq 1
. Since

\nu
is arbitrary in
s_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}(F)
,

s_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}(F)\subseteq\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}[h_{i}>1]
, so

F\in{(s_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet})}^{-1}(\Box{(\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}[h_{i}>1])})
.

Conversely, let

F\in{(s_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet})}^{-1}(\Box{(\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}[h_{i}>r_{i}])})
, and let
us define
f\colon s_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}(F)\times\Delta_{n}\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
by

f(\nu,\vec{a})\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\int_{x\in X}\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}h_{i}(x)\,d\nu
. The space
s_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}(F)
, seen as a subspace of

{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X
, is non-empty and compact. The map

f(\_,\vec{a})
is lower semicontinuous for every

\vec{a}\in\Delta_{n}
, since

f(\_,\vec{a})^{-1}(]t,\infty])=s_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}(F)\cap[\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}h_{i}>t]
for every
t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
. The Minimax
Theorem 3.3 of
[
18
]
then says that:
(*)

\sup_{\vec{a}\in\Delta_{n}}\inf_{\nu\in s_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}(F)}f(\nu,\vec{a})=\min_{\nu\in s_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}(F)}\sup_{\vec{a}\in\Delta_{n}}f(\nu,\vec{a})
(and the
\min
on the right is
attained), provided we can show that
f
is closely convex in its
first argument and closely concave in its second argument. Whatever
the latter mean, Remark 3.4 of
[
18
]
tells us that
maps that preserve pairwise linear combinations of their first
arguments (with coefficients
a
and
1-a
,
a\in[0,1]
) are
convex hence closely convex in their first argument, and similarly
with “concave” instead of “convex”; and it is clear that
f
is
linear in both its arguments.

We recall that
F=r_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}(s_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}(F))
, so

F(h)=\min_{\nu\in s_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}(F)}\int_{x\in X}h(x)\,d\nu
for every
h\in\mathcal{L}X
. Since

F\in{(s_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet})}^{-1}(\Box{(\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}[h_{i}>r_{i}])})
, for every

\nu\in s_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}(F)
, there is an
i\in\{1,\cdots,n\}

such that
\int_{x\in X}h_{i}(x)\,d\nu>1
. In particular, there
is an
\vec{a}\in\Delta_{n}
such that

\int_{x\in X}\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}h_{i}(x)\,d\nu>1
. Therefore

\min_{\nu\in s_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}(F)}\sup_{\vec{a}\in\Delta_{n}}f(\nu,\vec{a})>1
. By
(*)
,

\sup_{\vec{a}\in\Delta_{n}}\inf_{\nu\in s_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}(F)}f(\nu,\vec{a})>1
, so there is an
\vec{a}\in\Delta_{n}
such that

\inf_{\nu\in s_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}(F)}\int_{x\in X}\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}h_{i}(x)\,d\nu>1
, namely such that

r_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}(s_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}(F))(\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}h_{i})>1
, or
equivalently such that
F(\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}h_{i})>1
. We conclude
that

F\in\bigcup_{\vec{a}\in\Delta_{n}}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}h_{i}>1\right]
. ∎

Lemma 4.7

For every
n\geq 1
, for all
h_{1},\cdots,h_{n}\in\mathcal{L}X
,

\displaystyle{\lambda^{\sharp}_{X}}^{-1}(\Box{(\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}[h_{i}>1])})

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{\vec{a}\in\Delta_{n}}\left[(\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}h_{i})^{*}>1\right].

When
n=1
, there is just one element in
\Delta_{n}
, and that is

(1)
. We obtain the following as a special case.

Corollary 4.8

For every
h\in\mathcal{L}X
,

\displaystyle{(s_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet})}^{-1}(\Box{[h>1]})

\displaystyle=[h>1]

\displaystyle{\lambda^{\sharp}_{X}}^{-1}(\Box{[h>1]})

\displaystyle=[h^{*}>1].

Lemma 4.9

\lambda^{\sharp}
is a natural transformation.

Proof 3

Since
s_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}
is natural, it suffices to show that
\Phi
is
natural. Let
f\colon X\to Y
be any continuous map. We need to
show that

\Phi\circ{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}f}=\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}f\circ\Phi
.
Let
\mu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}
and
h\in\mathcal{L}Y
. Then

(\Phi\circ{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}f})(\mu)(h)=\Phi({\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}f[\mu])(h)=\int_{Q\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}Y}\min_{y\in Q}h(y)\,d{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}f[\mu]
. By
the change of variable formula, this is equal to

\int_{Q\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}\min_{y\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}f(Q)}h(y)\,d\mu
. Now

\min_{y\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}f(Q)}h(y)=\min_{y\in\upc f[Q]}h(y)=\min_{y\in f[Q]}h(y)
(because
h
, being lower semicontinuous,
is monotonic)
=\min_{x\in Q}h(f(x))
. We therefore obtain
that

(\Phi\circ{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}f})(\mu)(h)=\int_{Q\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}\min_{x\in Q}h(f(x))\,d\mu
. But

(\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}f\circ\Phi)(\mu)(h)=\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}f(\Phi(\mu))(h)=\Phi(\mu)(h\circ f)=\int_{Q\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}\min_{x\in Q}h(f(x))\,d\mu
. ∎

Lemma 4.10

{\lambda^{\sharp}_{X}}\circ{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{\eta^{\mathcal{Q}}_{X}}=\eta^{\mathcal{Q}}_{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}
.

Proof 4

For every
\nu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X
, the elements of

({\lambda^{\sharp}_{X}}\circ{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{\eta^{\mathcal{Q}}_{X}})(\nu)
are those

\nu^{\prime}\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X
such that

\nu^{\prime}(U)\geq{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{\eta^{\mathcal{Q}}_{X}}(\nu)(\Box U)
for
every
U\in{\mathcal{O}}X
. Now

{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{\eta^{\mathcal{Q}}_{X}}(\nu)(\Box U)=\nu({(\eta^{\mathcal{Q}}_{X})}^{-1}(\Box U))=\nu(U)
, so

({\lambda^{\sharp}_{X}}\circ{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{\eta^{\mathcal{Q}}_{X}})(\nu)
is simply the
collection of
\nu^{\prime}\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X
such that
\nu^{\prime}\geq\nu
,
namely
\eta^{\mathcal{Q}}_{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}(\nu)
. ∎

Lemma
4.10
is the first of the weak distributivity
laws. The proofs of the others are more complex. We will use the
following several times:

Trick A.

In order to show that two continuous maps

f,g\colon Y\to Z
are equal, where
Z
is a
T_{0}
space, check
that
f^{-1}(V)=g^{-1}(V)
for every element
V
of a subbase of
the topology of
Z
.

Indeed, if the condition holds, then

f^{-1}(V)=g^{-1}(V)
for every open subset
V
of
Z
.
Hence, for every point
y\in Y
,
f(y)
and
g(y)
will have the
same open neighborhoods and therefore be equal, since
Z
is
T_{0}
.

We also note that any space of the form
{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}Y
,

\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}Y
, or
{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}Y
is
T_{0}
, since their specialization
preorderings are
\leq
,
\leq
, and
\subseteq
respectively, and
they are antisymmetric.

In general, the maps
f
and
g
that are to be shown equal are
compositions
f_{1}\circ\cdots\circ f_{m}
and

g_{1}\circ\cdots\circ g_{n}
, and we will use Trick A by writing lists
of the following kind:

V{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\Longrightarrow}\limits^{{f_{1}}}}}V_{1}{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\Longrightarrow}\limits^{{f_{2}}}}}\cdots{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\Longrightarrow}\limits^{{f_{m}}}}}V_{m}
and

V{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\Longrightarrow}\limits^{{g_{1}}}}}V^{\prime}_{1}{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\Longrightarrow}\limits^{{g_{2}}}}}\cdots{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\Longrightarrow}\limits^{{g_{n}}}}}V^{\prime}_{n}
, meaning
that
V_{1}=f_{1}^{-1}(V)
, etc., and finally checking that
V_{m}=V^{\prime}_{n}
.

For example, an alternate proof of Lemma
4.10
along
these lines is:

\displaystyle\Box{(\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}[h_{i}>1])}

\displaystyle{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\Longrightarrow}\limits^{{{\lambda^{\sharp}_{X}}}}}}\bigcup_{\vec{a}\in\Delta_{n}}[(\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}h_{i})^{*}>1]

by Lemma
4.7

\displaystyle{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\Longrightarrow}\limits^{{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{\eta^{\mathcal{Q}}_{X}}}}}}\bigcup_{\vec{a}\in\Delta_{n}}[(\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}h_{i})^{*}\circ\eta^{\mathcal{Q}}_{X}>1]

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{\vec{a}\in\Delta_{n}}[\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}h_{i}>1],

since for every
h\in\mathcal{L}X
,
h^{*}\circ\eta^{\mathcal{Q}}_{X}=h
, and:

\displaystyle\Box{(\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}[h_{i}>1])}

\displaystyle{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\Longrightarrow}\limits^{{\eta^{\mathcal{Q}}_{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}}}}}\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}[h_{i}>1].

Now

\bigcup_{\vec{a}\in\Delta_{n}}[\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}h_{i}>1]=\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}[h_{i}>1]
, as open subsets of
{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X
: for
every
\nu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X
, if
\int_{x\in X}h_{i}(x)\,d\nu>1

for some
i
, then

\int_{x\in X}\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}h_{i}(x)\,d\nu>1
for the tuple

\vec{a}
whose
i
th component is
1
and whose other components are

0
. Conversely, if

\int_{x\in X}\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}h_{i}(x)\,d\nu>1
, then

\int_{x\in X}h_{i}(x)\,d\nu>1
for some
i
, otherwise

\int_{x\in X}h_{i}(x)\,d\nu\leq 1
for every
i
, so

\int_{x\in X}\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}h_{i}(x)\,d\nu=\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\int_{x\in X}h_{i}(x)\,d\nu\leq 1
.

We will also use the following.

Trick B.

In order to show that
f=g
, where
f
and
g
are continuous
maps from a space
Y
to a space of the form
{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}Z}
,
show that
f(y)
and
g(y)
are convex for every
y\in Y
, and
then show that
r_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}\circ f=r_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}\circ g
.

Indeed, we recall that
r_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}
is a homeomorphism of
{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{cvx}({\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}Z)
onto
\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}Z
.

Lemma 4.11

{\lambda^{\sharp}_{X}}\circ{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{\mu^{\mathcal{Q}}_{X}}=\mu^{\mathcal{Q}}_{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}\circ{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\lambda^{\sharp}_{X}}\circ\lambda^{\sharp}_{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}
.

Proof 5

Since
{\lambda^{\sharp}_{X}}=s_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}\circ\Phi
and the image
of
s_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}
consists of convex subsets, the left-hand side
takes its values in convex sets. For the right-hand side, for every

\xi\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}}
, let

Q^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}(\mu^{\mathcal{Q}}_{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}\circ{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\lambda^{\sharp}_{X}}\circ\lambda^{\sharp}_{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X})(\xi)
. We want to show that
Q^{\prime}
is
convex. The elements
\nu
of
Q^{\prime}
are those of

\bigcup({\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\lambda^{\sharp}_{X}}\circ\lambda^{\sharp}_{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X})(\xi)
, namely those that are in some element
\mathcal{Q}
of

({\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\lambda^{\sharp}_{X}}\circ\lambda^{\sharp}_{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X})(\xi)=\upc{\lambda^{\sharp}_{X}}[\lambda^{\sharp}_{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}(\xi)]=\{\mathcal{Q}\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}\mid\exists\mu\in\lambda^{\sharp}_{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}(\xi),\mathcal{Q}\subseteq{\lambda^{\sharp}_{X}}(\mu)\}
. (Remember that
the specialization ordering on
{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}
is reverse
inclusion
\supseteq
.) Simplifying this, and since
\upc\mu
is
always non-empty, compact and saturated, hence can take the place of

\mathcal{Q}
, the elements
\nu
of
Q^{\prime}
are those such that there
is a
\mu\in\lambda^{\sharp}_{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}(\xi)
such that

\nu\in{\lambda^{\sharp}_{X}}(\mu)
.

Let
a\in[0,1]
, and
\nu_{1},\nu_{2}\in Q^{\prime}
. Hence there is a

\mu_{1}\in\lambda^{\sharp}_{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}(\xi)
such that

\nu_{1}\in{\lambda^{\sharp}_{X}}(\mu_{1})
and there is a

\mu_{2}\in\lambda^{\sharp}_{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}(\xi)
such that

\nu_{2}\in{\lambda^{\sharp}_{X}}(\mu_{2})
. We note that

\lambda^{\sharp}_{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}(\xi)=(s_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}\circ\Phi)(\xi)
is
convex, so
a\mu_{1}+(1-a)\mu_{2}\in\lambda^{\sharp}_{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}(\xi)
.
Additionally,

a\nu_{1}+(1-a)\nu_{2}\in{\lambda^{\sharp}_{X}}(a\mu_{1}+(1-a)\mu_{2})
: for every

U\in{\mathcal{O}}X
,
\nu_{1}(U)\geq\mu_{1}(\Box U)
and

\nu_{2}(U)\geq\mu_{2}(\Box U)
by definition of
{\lambda^{\sharp}_{X}}
, so

(a\nu_{1}+(1-a)\nu_{2})(U)\geq(a\mu_{1}+(1-a)\mu_{2})(\Box U)
.
Therefore there is a
\mu\in\lambda^{\sharp}_{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}(\xi)
, namely

\mu\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}a\mu_{1}+(1-a)\mu_{2}
, such that

a\nu_{1}+(1-a)\nu_{2}\in{\lambda^{\sharp}_{X}}(\mu)
. Equivalently,

a\nu_{1}+(1-a)\nu_{2}
is in
Q^{\prime}
. Hence
Q^{\prime}
is convex, as
promised.

It follows that we can use Trick B, and so we prove the following
instead:

\displaystyle\Phi\circ{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{\mu^{\mathcal{Q}}_{X}}

\displaystyle=r_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}\circ\mu^{\mathcal{Q}}_{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}\circ{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\lambda^{\sharp}_{X}}\circ\lambda^{\sharp}_{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}.

Indeed,

r_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}\circ{\lambda^{\sharp}_{X}}=r_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}\circ s_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}\circ\Phi=\Phi
, which allows us to simplify the left-hand side.

We now use Trick A:

\displaystyle[h>1]

\displaystyle{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\Longrightarrow}\limits^{{\Phi}}}}[h^{*}>1]

\displaystyle{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\Longrightarrow}\limits^{{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{\mu^{\mathcal{Q}}_{X}}}}}}[h^{*}\circ\mu^{\mathcal{Q}}_{X}>1]=[h^{**}>1],

where for every
\mathcal{Q}\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}
,

h^{**}(\mathcal{Q})=\min_{Q\in\mathcal{Q}}h^{*}(Q)=\min_{Q\in\mathcal{Q}}\min_{x\in Q}h(x)
; and indeed, the latter is equal
to

\min_{x\in\bigcup\mathcal{Q}}h(x)=h^{*}(\mu^{\mathcal{Q}}_{X}(\mathcal{Q}))
.

\displaystyle[h>1]

\displaystyle{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\Longrightarrow}\limits^{{r_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}}}}}\Box{[h>1]}

by Fact
4.5

\displaystyle{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\Longrightarrow}\limits^{{\mu^{\mathcal{Q}}_{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}}}}}\Box{\Box{[h>1]}}

\displaystyle{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\Longrightarrow}\limits^{{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{{\lambda^{\sharp}_{X}}}}}}}\Box{{\lambda^{\sharp}_{X}}^{-1}(\Box{[h>1]})}=\Box{[h^{*}>1]}

by Corollary
4.8

\displaystyle{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\Longrightarrow}\limits^{{\lambda^{\sharp}_{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}}}}}[h^{**}>1]

\displaystyle\text{by Corollary~\ref{corl:lambda:inv:func:spec}}.

∎

Lemma 4.12

{\lambda^{\sharp}_{X}}\circ\mu_{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}={\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\mu_{X}}\circ\lambda^{\sharp}_{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}\circ{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{\lambda^{\sharp}_{X}}
.

Proof 6

The left-hand side takes its values in convex sets. We claim that
the right-hand side does, too. For every

\xi\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}}
, the elements of

A\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}({\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\mu_{X}}\circ\lambda^{\sharp}_{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}\circ{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{\lambda^{\sharp}_{X}})(\xi)
are the continuous
valuations
\nu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X
such that
\nu\geq\mu_{X}(\mu)

for some

\mu\in\lambda^{\sharp}_{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}({\lambda^{\sharp}_{X}}[\xi]))
. For all
a\in[0,1]
,
\nu_{1},\nu_{2}\in A
, there are

\mu_{1},\mu_{2}\in\lambda^{\sharp}_{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}({\lambda^{\sharp}_{X}}[\xi])
such that
\nu_{1}\geq\mu_{X}(\mu_{1})
and

\nu_{2}\geq\mu_{X}(\mu_{2})
. Then
\mu\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}a\mu_{1}+(1-a)\mu_{2}

is in
\lambda^{\sharp}_{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}({\lambda^{\sharp}_{X}}[\xi])
,
since
\lambda^{\sharp}_{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}
takes its values in convex
sets. In turn,

a\nu_{1}+(1-a)\nu_{2}\geq a\mu_{X}(\mu_{1})+(1-a)\mu_{X}(\mu_{2})=a(\mathcal{U}\in{\mathcal{O}}{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}\mapsto\int_{\nu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}\nu(\mathcal{U})\,d\mu_{1})+(1-a)(\mathcal{U}\in{\mathcal{O}}{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}\mapsto\int_{\nu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}\nu(\mathcal{U})\,d\mu_{2})=\mu_{X}(\mu)
, so

a\nu_{1}+(1-a)\nu_{2}\in A
, showing that
A
is convex.

We can therefore use Trick B, and since
r_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}\circ{\lambda^{\sharp}_{X}}=r_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}\circ s_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}\circ\Phi=\Phi
, it remains to show:

\displaystyle\Phi\circ\mu_{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}

\displaystyle=r_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}\circ{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\mu_{X}}\circ\lambda^{\sharp}_{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}\circ{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{\lambda^{\sharp}_{X}}.

To this end, we use Trick A:

\displaystyle[h>1]

\displaystyle{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\Longrightarrow}\limits^{{\Phi}}}}[h^{*}>1]

by Fact
4.4

\displaystyle{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\Longrightarrow}\limits^{{\mu_{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}}}}}\left[\left(\mu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}\mapsto\int_{Q\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}h^{*}(Q)\,d\mu\right)>1\right]

by (
1
)

while:

\displaystyle[h>1]

\displaystyle{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\Longrightarrow}\limits^{{r_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}}}}}\Box{[h>1]}

by Fact
4.5

\displaystyle{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\Longrightarrow}\limits^{{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\mu_{X}}}}}}\Box{\mu_{X}^{-1}([h>1])}=\Box{\left[\left(\nu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X\mapsto\int_{x\in X}h(x)\,d\nu\right)>1\right]}\mskip-80.0mu

by (
1
)

\displaystyle{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\Longrightarrow}\limits^{{\lambda^{\sharp}_{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}}}}}\left[\left(\nu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X\mapsto\int_{x\in X}h(x)\,d\nu\right)^{*}>1\right]

by Corollary
4.8

\displaystyle{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\Longrightarrow}\limits^{{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{\lambda^{\sharp}_{X}}}}}}\left[\left(\nu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X\mapsto\int_{x\in X}h(x)\,d\nu\right)^{*}\circ{\lambda^{\sharp}_{X}}>1\right].

In order to conclude, it suffices to show that:

\displaystyle\left(\nu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X\mapsto\int_{x\in X}h(x)\,d\nu\right)^{*}\circ{\lambda^{\sharp}_{X}}

\displaystyle=(\mu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}\mapsto\int_{Q\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}h^{*}(Q)\,d\mu).

The left-hand side maps every
\mu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}
to

\displaystyle\min_{\nu\in s_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}(\Phi(\mu))}\int_{x\in X}h(x)\,d\nu

\displaystyle=r_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}(s_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}(\Phi(\mu)))(h)

\displaystyle=\Phi(\mu)(h)=\int_{Q\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}h^{*}(Q),

and this completes the proof. ∎

Putting Lemma
4.9
, Lemma
4.10
,
Lemma
4.11
and Lemma
4.12

together, we obtain the following.

Theorem 4.13

Let
\bullet
be nothing, “
\leq 1
”, or “
1
”. Then

\lambda^{\sharp}
is a weak distributive law of
{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}
over

{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}
on the category
\mathbf{Top}
of topological spaces.

5
The associated weak composite monad

We will show that the associated weak composite monad (see

[
12
, Section 3.2]
, or
[
24
, page 46]
) is
isomorphic to the monad
\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}
of superlinear (resp., and
subnormalized, resp., and normalized) previsions, or equivalently to
the isomorphic monad
{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{cvx}{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}
.

There is an idempotent:

\textstyle{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\lambda^{\sharp}_{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\mu_{X}}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}}

(2)

and splitting it defines the action of the (functor part of the)
associated weak composite monad on each object
X
.
Splitting

an idempotent
e
means writing it as
\iota\circ\pi
, for a pair of
morphisms such that
\pi\circ\iota=\mathrm{id}
.

Lemma 5.1

A splitting of (
2
) is

\textstyle{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{r_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{s_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}}
.

Proof 7

Let
f
denote the idempotent (
2
). We recall that

\lambda^{\sharp}_{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}=s_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}\circ\Phi
. In
particular, any element in its range is convex. The image of any
convex element
Q
of a space
{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}}
under
{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}f
,
where
f
is any linear continuous map from

{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}Y\to{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}Z
, is convex; and
\mu_{X}
is
linear. Therefore
f
takes its values in

{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{cvx}{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}
.

Using Trick B, and simplifying using the fact that
r_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}\circ s_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}
is the identity, it remains to show that
r_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}
is
equal to
r_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}\circ f
, namely to:

\textstyle{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\lambda^{\sharp}_{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\mu_{X}}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{r_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}

Using Trick A,

\displaystyle[h>1]

\displaystyle{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\Longrightarrow}\limits^{{r_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}}}}}\Box{[h>1]}

by Fact
4.5

\displaystyle{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\Longrightarrow}\limits^{{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\mu_{X}}}}}}\Box{\mu_{X}^{-1}([h>1])}

\displaystyle\qquad=\Box{\left[\left(\nu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X\mapsto\int_{x\in X}h(x)\,d\nu\right)>1\right]}

by (
1
)

\displaystyle{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\Longrightarrow}\limits^{{\lambda^{\sharp}_{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}}}}}\left[\left(\nu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X\mapsto\int_{x\in X}h(x)\,d\nu\right)^{*}>1\right]

by Corollary
4.8

\displaystyle{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\Longrightarrow}\limits^{{\eta_{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}}}}}}\left\{Q\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}\mid\left(\nu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X\mapsto\int_{x\in X}h(x)\,d\nu\right)^{*}(Q)>1\right\}\mskip-360.0mu

\displaystyle\qquad=\left\{Q\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}\mid r_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}(Q)(h)>1\right\}=r_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{-1}([h>1]).\mskip-250.0mu

∎

We can always split an idempotent
e\colon Z\to Z
in
\mathbf{Top}
as
the composition of its corestriction to
e[Z]
with the inclusion map

e[Z]\to Z
. In Lemma
5.1
, we might have chosen
the homeomorphic space
{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{cvx}{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}
instead of

\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X
.

Generalizing (
2
), there is an idempotent:

\textstyle{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\lambda^{\sharp}_{X}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}\alpha}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}

(3)

for every
{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}
-algebra
\alpha
. Finding out what its
splitting is is harder in general, especially because we have no
complete characterization of
{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}
-algebras in
\mathbf{Top}
. We
will only need to compute this splitting for one additional

{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}
-algebra.

Lemma 5.2

For every topological space
X
, there is a
{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}
-algebra

\alpha_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}\colon\allowbreak{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}\to\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X
defined by

\alpha_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}(\xi)(h)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\int_{F\in\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}F(h)\,d\xi
for all

\xi\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}
and
h\in\mathcal{L}X
.

The idempotent (
3
) with

\alpha\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\alpha_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}
splits as:

\displaystyle\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 21.3347pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-21.3347pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 45.3347pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 45.3347pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{cvx}{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 119.69707pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 119.69707pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces,

where the arrow on the right is the inclusion map, and the arrow on
the left is the corestriction of (
3
), and maps
every
Q\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{cvx}{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}
to itself.

Proof 8

The map
F\mapsto F(h)
is lower semicontinuous, since the inverse
image of
]t,\infty]
is
[h>t]
, for all
h\in\mathcal{L}X
and

t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
. Hence the integral

\int_{F\in\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}F(h)\,d\xi
makes sense. The
map
{\alpha_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}}(\xi)
is Scott-continuous because each

F\in\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X
is and because integration with respect
to
\nu
is Scott-continuous; it is superlinear because
F
is and
integration with respect to
\xi
is linear. Additionally,

{(\alpha_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet})}^{-1}([h>r])=[(F\mapsto F(h))>r]
,
so
{\alpha_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}}
is continuous.

We claim that the map
\alpha_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}
is a

{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}
-algebra. We have

\alpha_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}(\eta_{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}(F))(h)=\int_{F^{\prime}\in\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}F^{\prime}(h)\,d\delta_{F}=F(h)
, so

\alpha_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}\circ\eta_{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}
is the
identity map. In order to show that

\alpha_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}\circ\mu_{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}=\alpha_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}\circ{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{\alpha_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}}
, we use
Trick A:

\displaystyle[h>1]

\displaystyle{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\Longrightarrow}\limits^{{\alpha_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}}}}}[(F\in\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X\mapsto F(h))>1]

\displaystyle{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\Longrightarrow}\limits^{{\mu_{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}}}}}[(\xi\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}\mapsto\int_{F\in\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}F(h)\,d\xi)>1]

by (
1
)

\displaystyle[h>1]

\displaystyle{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\Longrightarrow}\limits^{{\alpha_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}}}}}[(F\in\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X\mapsto F(h))>1]

\displaystyle{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\Longrightarrow}\limits^{{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{\alpha_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}}}}}}[(F\in\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X\mapsto F(h))\circ\alpha_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}>1]

\displaystyle=[(\xi\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}\mapsto\alpha_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}(\xi)(h))>1]

\displaystyle=[(\xi\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}\mapsto\int_{F\in\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}F(h)\,d\xi)>1].

In order to justify the announced splitting of
(
3
), we need to show that the image of
(
3
) is exactly
{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{cvx}{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}
.
For every
Q\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X
, the image of
Q
by (
3
)
is
\upc\alpha_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}[Q^{\prime}]
where

Q^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\lambda^{\sharp}_{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}(\delta_{Q})
is
convex, and
\alpha_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}
is linear (in its
\xi
argument),
so that image is convex.

In order to see that every element
Q
of

{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{cvx}{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}
is reached as the image of some
element by (
3
), we simply show that
Q
is its
own image by (
3
), or equivalently, that
(
3
) restricts to the identity on

{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{cvx}{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}
. We use Trick A:

\displaystyle\Box{\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}[h_{i}>1]}

\displaystyle{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\Longrightarrow}\limits^{{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\alpha_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}}}}}}\Box{\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}{(\alpha_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet})}^{-1}([h_{i}>1])}

\displaystyle=\Box{\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}[(F\mapsto F(h_{i}))>1]}

\displaystyle{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\Longrightarrow}\limits^{{\lambda^{\sharp}_{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}}}}}\bigcup_{\vec{a}\in\Delta_{n}}\left[\left(F\mapsto\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}F(h_{i})\right)^{*}>1\right]

by Lemma
4.7

\displaystyle{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\Longrightarrow}\limits^{{\eta_{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}}}}}}\bigcup_{\vec{a}\in\Delta_{n}}\left(\left(F\mapsto\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}F(h_{i})\right)^{*}\right)^{-1}(]1,\infty]),

and we claim that the convex elements
Q
of
{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}
that are in the latter open set are exactly those of
\Box{\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}[h_{i}>1]}
, which will show the claim.

We have

Q\in\bigcup_{\vec{a}\in\Delta_{n}}\left(\left(F\mapsto\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}F(h_{i})\right)^{*}\right)^{-1}(]1,\infty])
if
and only there is an
\vec{a}\in\Delta_{n}
such that

\min_{F\in Q}\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}F(h_{i})>1
. For every

Q\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{cvx}{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}
and for every

i\in\{1,\cdots,n\}
, let

f(F,\vec{a})\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}F(h_{i})
. This defines a
function from
Q\times\Delta_{n}
to
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
that is lower
semicontinuous in
Q
(since
f
is the composition of various sums
and products with the functions
F\mapsto F(h_{i})
, which are lower
semicontinuous), is such that

f(aF_{1}+(1-a)F_{2},i)=af(F_{1},i)+(1-a)f(F_{2},i)
for all

a\in[0,1]
and
F_{1},F_{2}\in Q
(we need
Q
to be convex simply
to make sure that
aF_{1}+(1-a)F_{2}
still lies in
Q
) hence is
convex (the same property, with
\geq
instead of
=
) hence closely
convex in its first argument (see Remark 3.4 of

[
18
]
). It is also concave hence closely concave in
its second argument. By the Minimax Theorem 3.3 of

[
18
]
, therefore,

\sup_{\vec{a}\in\Delta_{n}}\min_{F\in Q}f(F,\vec{a})=\min_{F\in Q}\sup_{\vec{a}\in\Delta_{n}}f(F,\vec{a})
. For every

Q\in\bigcup_{\vec{a}\in\Delta_{n}}\left(\left(F\mapsto\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}F(h_{i})\right)^{*}\right)^{-1}\allowbreak(]1,\infty])
, the left-hand side is strictly larger than
1
, so the
right-hand side is, too. Hence, for every
F\in Q
, there is an

\vec{a}\in\Delta_{n}
such that
\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}F(h_{i})>1
. It
follows easily that
F(h_{i})>1
for some
i\in\{1,\cdots,n\}
,
so
Q\in\Box{\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}[h_{i}>1]}
. Conversely, if

Q\in\Box{\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}[h_{i}>1]}
, then for every
F\in Q
,
there is an
i\in\{1,\cdots,n\}
such that
F(h_{i})>1
, so
there is an
\vec{a}\in\Delta_{n}
such that

\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}F(h_{i})>1
, and therefore

\sup_{\vec{a}\in\Delta_{n}}\min_{F\in Q}f(F,\vec{a})>1
,
showing that
Q
is in

\bigcup_{\vec{a}\in\Delta_{n}}\left(\left(F\mapsto\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}F(h_{i})\right)^{*}\right)^{-1}\allowbreak(]1,\infty])
.
∎

Following Böhm, Garner showed that, for all monads
S
and
T
on a
category where idempotent splits, every weak distributive law

\lambda\colon TS\to ST
yields a weak lifting
\overline{S}
of
S

to the category of
T
-algebras, built as follows.

A
T
-semialgebra is a morphism
\alpha\colon TX\to X
such that

\alpha\circ\mu^{T}_{X}=\alpha\circ T\alpha
. Given a weak
distributive law
\lambda
,

S\alpha\circ\lambda_{X}\circ\eta^{T}_{SX}\colon SX\to SX
is an
idempotent, and also a morphism of
T
-semialgebras. We can split it
in
\mathbf{Top}
, as:

\textstyle{SX\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{\alpha}}
\textstyle{\overline{S}X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{\alpha}}
\textstyle{SX}

since idempotents split in
\mathbf{Top}
. We recall that splitting means
that the idempotent is equal to
\iota_{\alpha}\circ\pi_{\alpha}
, and

\pi_{\alpha}\circ\iota_{\alpha}
is the identity. This actually splits
the idempotent in the category of
T
-semialgebras, as follows:

\textstyle{TSX\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\lambda_{X}}
\scriptstyle{T\pi_{\alpha}}
\textstyle{T\overline{S}X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T\iota_{\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lambda_{X}\circ T\iota_{\alpha}}
\textstyle{TSX\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\lambda_{X}}
\textstyle{STX\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{S\alpha}
\textstyle{STX\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{\alpha}\circ S\alpha}
\textstyle{STX\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{S\alpha}
\textstyle{SX\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{\alpha}}
\textstyle{\overline{S}X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{\alpha}}
\textstyle{SX}

Additionally, the middle vertical arrow
\overline{S}\alpha\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\pi_{\alpha}\circ S\alpha\circ\lambda_{X}\circ T\iota_{\alpha}
is not
just a
T
-semialgebra, but a
T
-algebra.

This defines the object part of a endofunctor
\overline{S}
on the
category of
T
-algebras.
On morphisms, the action of
\overline{S}
is given by: for all

T
-algebras
\alpha\colon TX\to X
and
\beta\colon TY\to Y
, for
every
T
-algebra morphism
f\colon\alpha\to\beta
,

\overline{S}f\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\pi_{\beta}\circ Sf\circ\iota_{\alpha}
.

\overline{S}
is part of a monad, whose unit

\eta^{\overline{S}}_{\alpha}
is
\pi_{\alpha}\circ\eta^{S}_{X}
, and whose
multiplication is

\mu^{\overline{S}}_{\alpha}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\pi_{\alpha}\circ\mu^{S}_{X}\circ S\iota_{\alpha}\circ\iota_{\overline{S}\alpha}
. All of that was
proved by Böhm and Garner.

Lemma 5.3

For
S\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}
,
T\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}
,

\lambda\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\lambda^{\sharp}
, the monad
\overline{S}
on the
category of
{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}
-algebras has the following properties:

1.

For every free
{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}
-algebra

\mu_{X}\colon{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}\to{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X
,

\overline{S}\mu_{X}\colon{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}\to\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X
is
\alpha_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}
(see
Lemma
5.2
).

2.

The unit
\eta^{\overline{S}}_{\mu_{X}}
evaluated at the free

{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}
-algebra

\mu_{X}\colon{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}\to{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X
is

r_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}\circ\eta^{\mathcal{Q}}_{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}
.

3.

The multiplication
\mu^{\overline{S}}_{\mu_{X}}
evaluated at
the free
{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}
-algebra

\mu_{X}\colon{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}\to{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X
is
the function that maps every
Q\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{cvx}{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}
to
(h\in\mathcal{L}X\mapsto\min_{F\in Q}F(h))\in\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X
; the inverse image of
[h>1]
by that
function is
\Box{[h>1]}\cap{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{cvx}{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}
,
for every
h\in\mathcal{L}X
.

4.

The counit
\epsilon^{T}
of the adjunction
F^{T}\dashv U^{T}

between
\mathbf{Top}
and the category of
T
-algebras
(
{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}
-algebras) is given at each algebra
\beta\colon{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X\to X
as
\beta
itself.

Proof 9

1. We know that
\overline{S}\mu_{X}
must be a
{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}
-algebra
on
\overline{S}{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}
, and the latter can be taken as

\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X
, by Lemma
5.1
. (All
splittings are up to isomorphism.) We know that

\overline{S}\mu_{X}=\pi_{\mu_{X}}\circ S\mu_{X}\circ\lambda_{X}\circ T\iota_{\mu_{X}}=r_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}\circ{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}\mu_{X}\circ{\lambda^{\sharp}_{X}}\circ{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{s_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}}
, which we elucidate using
Trick A:

\displaystyle[h>1]

\displaystyle{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\Longrightarrow}\limits^{{r_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}}}}}\Box{[h>1]}

by Fact
4.5

\displaystyle{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\Longrightarrow}\limits^{{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\mu_{X}}}}}}\Box{\mu_{X}^{-1}([h>1])}=\Box{\left[\left(\nu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X\mapsto\int_{x\in X}h(x)\,d\nu\right)>1\right]}\mskip-80.0mu

by (
1
)

\displaystyle{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\Longrightarrow}\limits^{{\lambda^{\sharp}_{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}}}}}\left[\left(\nu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X\mapsto\int_{x\in X}h(x)\,d\nu\right)^{*}>1\right]

by Corollary
4.8

\displaystyle{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\Longrightarrow}\limits^{{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{s_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}}}}}}\left[\left(\nu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X\mapsto\int_{x\in X}h(x)\,d\nu\right)^{*}\circ s_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}>1\right]

\displaystyle=[(F\in\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X\mapsto F(h))>1].

The last equality is justified as follows. For every

F\in\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X
,

\displaystyle\left(\left(\nu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X\mapsto\int_{x\in X}h(x)\,d\nu\right)^{*}\circ s_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}\right)(F)

\displaystyle=r_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}(s_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}(F))(h)=F(h).

Finally,
[(F\in\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X\mapsto F(h))>1]

\xi\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}

, namely
{(\alpha_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet})}^{-1}([h>1])
.

2.

\eta^{\overline{S}}_{\mu_{X}}=\pi_{\mu_{X}}\circ\eta^{S}_{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}=r_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}\circ\eta^{\mathcal{Q}}_{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}
.

3. In general,
\mu^{\overline{S}}_{\alpha}
is a morphism from

\overline{S}{\overline{S}\alpha}
to
\overline{S}\alpha
in the

{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}
-algebras. When

\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}

\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X
.
We have

. We may take

\iota_{\overline{S}\mu_{X}}=\iota_{\alpha_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}}
to be the

{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{cvx}{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}

5.2
—just
subspace inclusion. Then

5.1

\displaystyle[h>1]

\displaystyle=\Box{[h>1]}

by Corollary

Now this looks just like

, and we imitate it by letting

be defined by
