Finally, the weak composite monad of
S
with
T
is

U^{T}\overline{S}F^{T}
, with unit

U^{T}\eta^{\overline{S}}F^{T}\circ\eta^{T}
and multiplication

U^{T}\mu^{\overline{S}}F^{T}\circ U^{T}\overline{S}\epsilon^{T}\overline{S}F^{T}
, where
F^{T}\dashv U^{T}
is the adjunction between the base
category (
\mathbf{Top}
in our case) and the category of
T
-algebras, and

\epsilon^{T}
is its counit.

Theorem 5.4

The weak composite monad associated with

\lambda^{\sharp}\colon{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}}\to{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}}
on

\mathbf{Top}
is
(\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet},\eta^{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}},\mu^{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}})
.

Proof 10

We take
T\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}
,
S\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}
,

\lambda\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\lambda^{\sharp}
. The functor
U^{T}
takes every

{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}
-algebra
\alpha\colon{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X\to X
to
X

and every
{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}
-algebra morphism

f\colon(\alpha\colon{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X\to X)\to(\beta\colon{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}Y\to Y)
to the underlying continuous map

f\colon X\to Y
. The functor
F^{T}
takes every space
X
to

\mu_{X}\colon{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}\to{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X
, and
every continuous map
f\colon X\to Y
to
{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}f
, seen as
a morphism of
{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}
-algebras.

Then
U^{T}\overline{S}F^{T}
maps every space
X
to the splitting

\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X
of

{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\mu_{X}}\circ{\lambda^{\sharp}_{X}}\circ\eta_{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}
we have obtained in
Lemma
5.1
. We also obtain

\pi_{\mu_{X}}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}r_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}
,
\iota_{\mu_{X}}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}s_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}
.

Given any continuous map
f\colon X\to Y
,
U^{T}\overline{S}F^{T}(f)
is equal to (the map underlying the
{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}
-algebra)

\overline{S}({\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}f)
, namely
\pi_{\mu_{Y}}\circ{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}f}\circ\iota_{\mu_{X}}=r_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}\circ{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}f}\circ s_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}
. We claim that this is equal
to
\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}(f)
, namely that it maps every
F\in\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X
to
(h\in\mathcal{L}X\mapsto F(f\circ h))
.
We use Trick A:

\displaystyle[h>1]

\displaystyle{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\Longrightarrow}\limits^{{r_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}}}}}\Box{[h>1]}

by Fact
4.5

\displaystyle{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\Longrightarrow}\limits^{{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}f}}}}}\Box{({\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}f)^{-1}([h>1])}=\Box{[h\circ f>1]}

\displaystyle{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\Longrightarrow}\limits^{{s_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}}}}}[h\circ f>1]

by Corollary
4.8

\displaystyle={(\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}(f))}^{-1}([h>1]).

From Lemma
5.3
, item 2, the unit of
\overline{S}
at
the algebra
\mu_{X}
is

\eta^{\overline{S}}_{\mu_{X}}=r_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}\circ\eta^{\mathcal{Q}}_{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}
, which is the map

\nu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X\mapsto r_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}(\upc\nu)
, namely

\nu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X\mapsto(h\in\mathcal{L}X\mapsto\int_{x\in X}h(x)\,d\nu)
from
{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X
to
\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X
.

The unit of the weak composite monad is

U^{T}\eta^{\overline{S}}F^{T}\circ\eta^{T}
. At object
X
, this maps
every
x\in X
to

(h\in\mathcal{L}X\mapsto\int_{x^{\prime}\in X}h(x^{\prime})\,d\delta_{x})
,
namely to
(h\in\mathcal{L}X\mapsto h(x))
, and that is exactly the
unit of the
\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}
monad.

The multiplication is

U^{T}\mu^{\overline{S}}F^{T}\circ U^{T}\overline{S}\epsilon^{T}\overline{S}F^{T}
, and here is what this means, explicitly. At any
object
X
,
\overline{S}F^{T}X
is the
T
-algebra

\overline{S}\mu_{X}\colon T\overline{S}TX\to\overline{S}TX
.
Then
\epsilon^{T}
evaluated at this
T
-algebra is the bottom arrow
in the following diagram:

\textstyle{TT\overline{S}TX\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu_{\overline{S}TX}}
\scriptstyle{T\epsilon^{T}_{\overline{S}F^{T}X}}
\textstyle{T\overline{S}TX\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{S}\mu_{X}}
\textstyle{T\overline{S}TX\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\epsilon^{T}_{\overline{S}F^{T}X}}
\textstyle{\overline{S}TX}

from the
T
-algebra
\alpha\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\mu_{\overline{S}TX}
that is
the leftmost vertical arrow to the
T
-algebra

\beta\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\overline{S}\mu_{X}
on the right. We apply
\overline{S}
to the morphism
\epsilon^{T}_{\overline{S}F^{T}X}
(i.e., we now
apply
\overline{S}
to a morphism of
T
-algebras, not to a

T
-algebra, as we did before), and we obtain the composition:

\textstyle{\overline{S}T\overline{S}TX\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{\alpha}}
\textstyle{ST\overline{S}TX\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{S\epsilon^{T}_{\overline{S}F^{T}X}}
\textstyle{S\overline{S}TX\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{\beta}}
\textstyle{\overline{S}\overline{S}TX.}

(4)

Applying
U^{T}
to the latter, we obtain the same composition, this
time seen as a morphism in the base category, instead of as a
morphism in the category of
T
-algebras. We finally compose it
with
U^{T}\mu^{\overline{S}}_{F^{T}X}
, which is

\mu^{\overline{S}}_{\mu_{X}}\colon\overline{S}\overline{S}TX\to\overline{S}TX
.

By Lemma
5.3
, item 4,

\epsilon^{T}_{\overline{S}F^{T}X}\colon\alpha\to\beta
is
\beta

itself, where
\beta=\overline{S}\mu_{X}=\alpha_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}

(Lemma
5.3
, item 1) and

\alpha=\mu_{\overline{S}TX}=\mu_{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}
: we
have
\overline{S}TX=\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X
, using
Lemma
5.1
, and
\iota_{\alpha}=s_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}
.
Using Lemma
5.2
,

\pi_{\beta}\colon{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}\to{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{cvx}{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}
is the corestriction of
(
3
) (call it
f
), which maps every convex
element of
{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}
to itself.

Hence the multiplication of the weak composite monad, evaluated at

X
, is the composition of (
4
) with
\mu^{\overline{S}}_{\mu_{X}}
(given in Lemma
5.3
, item 3), namely:

\textstyle{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{s_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\alpha_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{cvx}{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu^{\overline{S}}_{\mu_{X}}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}

(5)

For every
\mathcal{F}\in\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}
,

{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\alpha_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}}(s_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{F}))=\upc\{\alpha_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}(\xi)\mid\xi\in s_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{F})\}=\upc\{(h\in\mathcal{L}X\mapsto\int_{F\in\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}F(h)\,d\xi)\mid\xi\in s_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{F})\}
. It is easy
to see that this is a convex set, because

s_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{F})
is convex and integration is linear in
the continuous valuation. Therefore
f
maps it to itself, and,
using Lemma
5.3
, item 3,
\mu^{\overline{S}}_{\mu_{X}}

maps it to:

\displaystyle(h\in\mathcal{L}X\mapsto\min_{F\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\alpha_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}}(s_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{F}))}F(h))

\displaystyle=(h\in\mathcal{L}X\mapsto\min_{\xi\in s_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{F}),F\geq\alpha_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}(\xi)}F(h))

\displaystyle=(h\in\mathcal{L}X\mapsto\min_{\xi\in s_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{F})}\alpha_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}(\xi)(h))

\displaystyle=(h\in\mathcal{L}X\mapsto\min_{\xi\in s_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{F})}\int_{F\in\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}F(h)\,d\xi)

\displaystyle=(h\in\mathcal{L}X\mapsto r_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}(s_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{F}))(F\in\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X\mapsto F(h)))

\displaystyle=(h\in\mathcal{L}X\mapsto\mathcal{F}(F\in\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X\mapsto F(h)),

and this is exactly what the multiplication of the

\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}
monad, evaluated at
X
and applied to
\mathcal{F}
, produces.
∎

Part II
A weak distributive law between
{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}
and
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}

Just as
{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}
is a model of demonic non-determinism, the Hoare
powerdomain is a model of
angelic
non-determinism, and is
obtained as follows. For every topological space
X
, let
{\mathcal{H}}X

be the set of non-empty closed subsets of
X
. The
lower
Vietoris
topology on that set has subbasic open subsets
\Diamond U
consisting of those non-empty closed subsets that intersect
U
,
for each open subset
U
of
X
. We write
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}X
for the resulting
topological space. Its specialization ordering is (ordinary)
inclusion
\subseteq
.

The
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}
construction was studied by Schalk

[
41
, Section 6]
, as well as a localic variant and a
variant with the Scott topology. See also
[
1
, Sections 6.2.2,
6.2.3]
or
[
13
, Section IV-8]
.

There is a monad
({\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}},\eta^{\mathcal{H}},\mu^{\mathcal{H}})
on
\mathbf{Top}
. For
every continuous map
f\colon X\to Y
,
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}f
maps every

C\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X
to
cl(f[C])
. The unit
\eta^{\mathcal{H}}
is defined by

\eta^{\mathcal{H}}_{X}(x)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\dc x
for every
x\in X
. For every
continuous map
f\colon X\to{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}Y
, there is an extension

f^{\flat}\colon{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}X\to{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}Y
, defined by

f^{\flat}(C)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}cl(\bigcup_{x\in C}f(x))
. The multiplication

\mu^{\mathcal{H}}
is defined by

\mu^{\mathcal{H}}_{X}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\mathrm{id}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}^{\flat}
, namely

\mu^{\mathcal{H}}_{X}(\mathcal{C})\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}cl(\bigcup\mathcal{C})
.

We note the equalities
{\eta^{\mathcal{H}}}^{-1}(\Diamond U)=U
,

{({\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}f)}^{-1}(\Diamond V)=\Diamond{f^{-1}(V)}
, and

{\mu^{\mathcal{H}}}^{-1}(\Diamond U)=\Diamond{\Diamond U}
.

The remainder of this part is more or less directly copied from
Part I, and is therefore pretty boring. For the most part, we replace

\Box
with
\Diamond
,
\supseteq
by
\subseteq
,
\lambda^{\sharp}_{X}

by
\lambda^{\flat}_{X}
, etc. But there are a few differences, if we
look closely enough. For example, the sets
\Diamond U
form a

subbase
, while the sets
\Box U
formed a
base
. Perhaps
more importantly, we will not reason on the whole category
\mathbf{Top}
,
but on a full subcategory
\mathbf{Top}^{\flat}
of spaces
X
satisfying
certain conditions. The reason is that there are maps
r_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}
and

s_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}
, similar to the maps
r_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}
and
s_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}
, but
they are only known to enjoy similar properties on spaces
X
that are

{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}_{\bullet}
-friendly (see
[
17
]
and its errata

[
21
]
)—we introduce
{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}_{\bullet}
-friendliness
below. Also, the formula(e) for inverse images of subbasic open sets
by
s_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}
will be trickier.

We import the following from
[
32
]
. A
cone

is a set with a scalar multiplication operation, by scalars from

\mathbb{R}_{+}
, and with an addition operation, satisfying the expected laws.
A
semitopological cone
is a cone with a topology that makes
both scalar multiplication and addition separately continuous, where

\mathbb{R}_{+}
is given the Scott topology. For example,
\mathcal{L}X
,
{\mathbf{V}}X
,

\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}X
,
\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}X
are semitopological cones, and

{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X
,
\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X
,
\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X
are
convex subspaces of the latter three. In a semitopological cone,
scalar multiplication is always jointly continuous, but addition may
fail to be. A
topological cone
is one where addition is
jointly continuous. A semitopological cone
C
is
locally
convex
if and only if for every
x\in C
, every open neighborhood
of
x
contains a convex open neighborhood of
x
. It is

locally convex-compact
if and only if for every
x\in C
,
every open neighborhood of
x
contains a convex compact saturated
neighborhood of
x
.

A space
X
is
{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}_{\bullet}
-friendly

[
21
, Definition 1]

if and only if:

—

\bullet
is nothing or “
\leq 1
”, and
\mathcal{L}X
is locally
convex;

—

or
\bullet
is “
1
”, and either:

1.

\mathcal{L}X
is locally convex and
X
is compact;

2.

or
\mathcal{L}X
is a locally convex, locally convex-compact,
sober topological cone;

3.

or
X
is LCS-complete.

Every locally compact space is core-compact, and every core-compact
space is
{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}_{\bullet}
-friendly, for any value of
\bullet

[
21
, Remark 2]
.
Every LCS-complete space is
{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}_{\bullet}
-friendly for any value of

\bullet

[
21
, Remark 3]
.
We state this formally here.

Fact 5.5

Let
\bullet
be nothing, “
\leq 1
”, or “
1
”. Every locally
compact space
X
(even every core-compact space), every
LCS-complete space
X
is
{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}_{\bullet}
-friendly.

The categorical machinery requires the subcategory
\mathbf{Top}^{\flat}
to
be closed under the
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}
and
{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}
functors, as well as
under retracts (so that
\mathbf{Top}^{\flat}
, just like
\mathbf{Top}
, splits
idempotents). For example, we may take
\mathbf{Top}^{\flat}
to be the full
subcategory of locally compact spaces. First, by
Fact
5.5
, such spaces are
{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}_{\bullet}
-friendly.
Second, for every locally compact space
X
,
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}X
is locally
compact, by
[
41
, Proposition 6.11]
, and
{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X

is locally compact if
\bullet
is nothing or “
\leq 1
” (see

[
23
, Theorem 12.2]
, or

[
22
, Theorem 4.1]
). When
\bullet
is “
1
”,

{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X
is locally compact and compact if
X
locally compact
and compact, and
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}X
is also locally compact, as we have seen, and
compact: the proof of
[
41
, Proposition 6.11]
shows in
particular that

\Diamond K\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\{C\in{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}X\mid C\cap K\neq\emptyset\}
is
compact for every compact subset
K
of
X
, and the claim follows by
taking
K
equal to
X
. Finally, any retract of a locally compact
(resp., compact) space is locally compact (resp., compact); this
follows from the arguments developed in the proof of
[
31
, Proposition
2.17]
. In brief, we work under the following
assumption.

Definition 5.6

Let
\mathbf{Top}^{\flat}
be any full subcategory of
\mathbf{Top}
consisting
of
{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}_{\bullet}
-friendly spaces, and closed under
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}
,

{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}
and under retracts—for example the category of
locally compact spaces if
\bullet
is nothing or “
\leq 1
”, or
the category of locally compact, compact spaces if
\bullet
is
“
1
”.

6
The monad of sublinear previsions

A
sublinear
prevision on a space
X
is a prevision
F
such
that
F(h+h^{\prime})\leq F(h)+F(h^{\prime})
for all
h,h^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}X
. We
write
\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}X
for the space of sublinear previsions on
X
,

\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\leq 1}X
for its subspace of subnormalized sublinear
previsions, and
\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{1}X
for its subspace of normalized
sublinear previsions, and we use the synthetic notation

\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X
for each. The topology is generated by sets
that we continue to write as
[h>r]
, with
h\in\mathcal{L}X
and

r\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
, and defined as

\{F\in\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X\mid F(h)>r\}
. Its specialization
ordering is the pointwise ordering:
F\leq F^{\prime}
if and only if

F(h)\leq F(h^{\prime})
for every
h\in\mathcal{L}X
.

There is a
\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}
endofunctor on
\mathbf{Top}
, which is
defined with the exact same formulae as for
\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}
or

\mathbb{P}_{\mathtt{P}}^{\bullet}
. Its action on morphisms
f\colon X\to Y

is given by

\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}f(F)=(h\in\mathcal{L}Y\mapsto F(h\circ f))
.

As with superlinear and linear previsions, sublinear previsions form a
monad on
\mathbf{Top}
, which is a natural counterpart of the monad of
sublinear previsions on the category of dcpos given in

[
14
, Proposition 2]
. The following, which is
Proposition
3.1
with “
{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}
” replaced by “
{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}
”,
is proved in exactly the same way.

Proposition 6.1

Let
\bullet
be nothing, “
\leq 1
”, or “
1
”. There is a
monad

(\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet},\allowbreak\eta^{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}},\allowbreak\mu^{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}})
on

\mathbf{Top}
, whose unit and multiplication are defined at every
topological space
X
by:

•

for every
x\in X
,
\eta^{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}_{X}(x)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}(h\in\mathcal{L}X\mapsto h(x))
,

•

for every

\mathcal{F}\in\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}
,

\mu^{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}_{X}(\mathcal{F})\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}(h\in\mathcal{L}X\mapsto\mathcal{F}(F\in\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X\mapsto F(h)))
.

For every
{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}_{\bullet}
-friendly space
X
, for example, for every
locally compact or LCS-complete space
X
(see Fact
5.5
),
there is a function

r_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}\colon{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}({\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X)\to\mathbb{P}_{{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}}^{\bullet}X
, defined
by

r_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}(C)(h)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\sup_{\nu\in C}\allowbreak\int_{x\in X}h(x)\,d\nu
, and a map

s_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}\colon\mathbb{P}_{{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}}^{\bullet}X\to{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}({\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X)

defined by

s_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}(F)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\{\nu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X\mid\forall h\in\mathcal{L}X,\int_{x\in X}h(x)\,d\nu\leq F(h)\}
. Both are
continuous, and

r_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}\circ s_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}=\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{P}_{{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}}^{\bullet}X}
. This
is the content of Corollary 3.12 of
[
17
]
, up to two
changes: first, we have replaced the space
\mathbb{P}_{\mathtt{P}}^{\bullet}X

used there by
{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X
, since the two are homeomorphic;
second, we need to assume
X
to be
{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}_{\bullet}
-friendly for this,
as indicated in the errata
[
21
, Corollary 3.12, repaired]
.

Additionally,
r_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}
and
s_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}
are natural in
X
on the
category of
{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}_{\bullet}
-spaces
[
23
, Lemma 13.2]
,
in particular on the category
\mathbf{Top}^{\flat}
.

Corollary 6.2

The monad
(\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet},\allowbreak\eta^{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}},\allowbreak\mu^{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}})
restricts to a monad on any subcategory
\mathbf{Top}^{\flat}
as
given in Definition
5.6
.

Proof 11

It suffices to verify that
\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X
is an object of

\mathbf{Top}^{\flat}
for every object
X
of
\mathbf{Top}^{\flat}
, and this
follows from the fact that it arises as a retract of
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}
through
r_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}
and
s_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}
. ∎

This retraction even cuts down to a homeomorphism between

{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{cvx}({\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X)
and
\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X
provided that

X
is
{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}_{\bullet}
-friendly (see Theorem 4.11 of

[
17
, Theorem 4.11]
and the errata

[
21
]
);
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{cvx}({\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X)
denotes the
subspace of
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}({\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X)
consisting of convex non-empty
closed subsets of
{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X
.

As with
s_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}
, we propose the following simpler proof that

s_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}
is continuous, exhibiting the shape of inverse images
of subbasic open subsets
\Diamond{\bigcap_{i=1}^{n}[h_{i}>1]}
of

{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}({\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X)
(
n\geq 1
,
h_{i}\in\mathcal{L}X
).

Lemma 6.3

For every
n\geq 1
, for every natural number
N>n
, let

\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{N}
denote the set of non-negative integer multiples of

\frac{1}{N}
, and
\Delta_{n}^{N}
be the finite set

\{(b_{1},\cdots,b_{n})\in\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{N}\mid 1-\frac{n}{N}<\sum_{i=1}^{n}b_{i}\leq 1\}
.

Let
\bullet
be nothing, “
\leq 1
” or “
1
”, and
X
be an

{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}_{\bullet}
-friendly space. For every

n\geq 1
, for all
h_{1},\cdots,h_{n}\in\mathcal{L}X
,

\displaystyle{(s_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet})}^{-1}(\Diamond{(\bigcap_{i=1}^{n}[h_{i}>1])})

\displaystyle=\bigcap_{\vec{a}\in\Delta_{n}}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}h_{i}>1\right]

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{N>n}\bigcap_{\vec{b}\in\Delta_{n}^{N}}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{n}b_{i}h_{i}>1\right].

The first formula produces an intersection of open sets

[\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}h_{i}>1]
over all
\vec{a}\in\Delta_{n}
. That is
an infinite intersection in general, and therefore it is not
immediately clear that this intersection is open. The next line is an
infinite union of finite intersections of open sets, and is therefore
open.

Proof 12

Letting (1), (2), (3) be the three values above, in order to prove

(1)=(2)=(3)
, we show
(1)\subseteq(3)\subseteq(2)\subseteq(1)
.

(1)\subseteq(3)
. Let

F\in{(s_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet})}^{-1}(\Diamond{(\bigcap_{i=1}^{n}[h_{i}>1])})
. There is a
\nu\in s_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}(F)
such that

\int_{x\in X}h_{i}(x)\,d\nu>1
for every

i\in\{1,\cdots,n\}
. We pick a natural number
N>n
so large
than
\frac{N}{N-n}<\min_{i=1}^{n}\int_{x\in X}h_{i}(x)\,d\nu
.
Then, for every
\vec{b}\in\Delta_{n}^{N}
,

F(\sum_{i=1}^{n}b_{i}h_{i})\geq\int_{x\in X}\sum_{i=1}^{n}b_{i}h_{i}(x)\,d\nu
(because
\nu\in s_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}(F)
)

=\sum_{i=1}^{n}b_{i}\int_{x\in X}h_{i}(x)\,d\nu>\sum_{i=1}^{n}b_{i}\frac{N}{N-n}>(1-\frac{n}{N})\frac{N}{N-n}=1
.

(3)\subseteq(2)
. Let
F\in\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X
be such that
there is a natural number
N>n
such that, for every

\vec{b}\in\Delta_{n}^{N}
,
F(\sum_{i=1}^{n}b_{i}h_{i})>1
. For every

\vec{a}\in\Delta_{n}
, we define
\vec{b}
by

b_{i}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\frac{1}{N}\lfloor Na_{i}\rfloor
for each

i\in\{1,\cdots,n\}
. Then
b_{i}\in\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{N}
, and

\sum_{i=1}^{n}b_{i}
lies between

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{Na_{i}-1}{N}=1-\frac{n}{N}
(strictly) and

\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}=1
, so
\vec{b}
is in
\Delta_{n}^{N}
. Also,

a_{i}\geq b_{i}
for every
i
, so, using the monotonicity of
F
,

F(\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}h_{i})\geq F(\sum_{i=1}^{n}b_{i}h_{i})>1
; this
holds for every
\vec{a}\in\Delta_{n}
.

(2)\subseteq(1)
. Let

F\in\bigcap_{\vec{a}\in\Delta_{n}}[\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}h_{i}>1]
.
Since
r_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}\circ s_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}
is the identity,

F(\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}h_{i})=\sup_{\nu\in s_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}(F)}\int_{x\in X}\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}h_{i}(x)\,d\nu
—and

F(\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}h_{i})>1
—for every
\vec{a}\in\Delta_{n}
.

Let
f\colon\Delta_{n}\times s_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}(F)\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
map

(\vec{a},\nu)
to
\int_{x\in X}\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}h_{i}(x)\,d\nu
.
We have just shown that

\sup_{\nu\in s_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}(F)}f(\vec{a},\nu)>1
for every

\vec{a}\in\Delta_{n}
.

We equip
\Delta_{n}
with the subspace topology induced by the
inclusion in
(\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+})^{n}
, where each copy of
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
has the Scott
topology: this makes
f
lower semicontinuous in its first argument,
since addition and scalar multiplication are Scott-continuous on

\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
. The topology on
\Delta_{n}
has a base of open subsets

\Delta_{n}\cap\prod_{i=1}^{n}]b_{i},\infty]
, where each

b_{i}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
and
\sum_{i=1}^{n}b_{i}<1
(otherwise the intersection
with
\Delta_{n}
is empty, and can therefore be disregarded). But

\Delta_{n}\cap\prod_{i=1}^{n}]b_{i},\infty]=\Delta_{n}\cap\prod_{i=1}^{n}]b_{i},1+\epsilon[
, where
\epsilon
is any strictly
positive real number; therefore
\Delta_{n}
also has the subspace
topology induced by the inclusion in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
with its usual metric
topology. We then know that
\Delta_{n}
is compact (and Hausdorff).
The map
f
preserves pairwise linear combinations (with
coefficients
a
and
1-a
,
a\in[0,1]
) of its first arguments,
and similarly with its second arguments, so by Remark 3.4 and the
Minimax Theorem 3.3 of
[
18
]
,

\sup_{\nu\in s_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}(F)}\inf_{\vec{a}\in\Delta_{n}}f(\vec{a},\nu)=\min_{\vec{a}\in\Delta_{n}}\sup_{\nu\in s_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}(F)}f(\vec{a},\nu)
.

But
\sup_{\nu\in s_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}(F)}f(\vec{a},\nu)>1
for every

\vec{a}\in\Delta_{n}
, in other words

\min_{\vec{a}\in\Delta_{n}}\allowbreak\sup_{\nu\in s_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}(F)}f(\vec{a},\nu)>1
, so

\sup_{\nu\in s_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}(F)}\inf_{\vec{a}\in\Delta_{n}}f(\vec{a},\nu)>1
. Hence there is a continuous valuation

\nu\in s_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}(F)
such that

\inf_{\vec{a}\in\Delta_{n}}f(\vec{a},\nu)>1
, in particular such
that
\int_{x\in X}\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}h_{i}(x)\,d\nu>1
for every

\vec{a}\in\Delta_{n}
, in particular such that

\int_{x\in X}h_{i}(x)\,d\nu>1
for every

i\in\{1,\cdots n\}
. Therefore

\nu\in\bigcap_{i=1}^{n}[h_{i}>1]
, showing that

s_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}(F)\in\Diamond{\bigcap_{i=1}^{n}[h_{i}>1]}
. ∎

The following is immediate.

Fact 6.4

For every topological space
X
, for every
h\in\mathcal{L}X
,

{(r_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}})}^{-1}([h>1])=\Diamond{[h>1]}
.

7
The weak distributive law

We will show that there is a weak distributive law of
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}
over

{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}
on
\mathbf{Top}^{\flat}
. This will be the collection of
maps
{\lambda^{\flat}_{X}}
as
s_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}\circ\Psi
,
where
\Psi
is given below.

Lemma 7.1

Let
X
be a topological space. For every
h\in\mathcal{L}X
, the
function
h_{*}\colon C\mapsto\sup_{x\in C}h(x)
is in

\mathcal{L}{{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}
, and for every
r\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
,

h_{*}^{-1}(]r,\infty])=\Diamond{h^{-1}(]r,\infty])}
. There
is a continuous map

\Psi\colon{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}\to\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X
such
that:

\displaystyle\Psi(\mu)(h)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\int_{C\in{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}h_{*}(C)\,d\mu

for every
h\in\mathcal{L}X
. For every subbasic open subset

[h>r]
of
\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X
,

\displaystyle\Psi^{-1}([h>r])

\displaystyle=[h_{*}>r].

Proof 13

For every
r\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
,
h_{*}^{-1}(]r,\infty])=\{C\in{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}X\mid\sup_{x\in C}h(x)>r\}=\{C\in{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}X\mid\exists x\in C,h(x)>r\}=\Diamond{h^{-1}(]r,\infty])}
. This entails that

h_{*}
is lower semicontinuous.

In particular,
\Psi(\mu)(h)
is well-defined for every

\mu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X
and for every
h\in\mathcal{L}X
. It is
clear that
\Psi
is monotonic in
h
. If
h
is the (pointwise)
supremum of a directed family
{(h_{i})}_{i\in I}
in
\mathcal{L}X
,
then for every
C\in{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}X
,

h_{*}(C)=\sup_{x\in C}\sup\nolimits^{\scriptstyle\uparrow}_{i\in I}h_{i}(x)=\sup\nolimits^{\scriptstyle\uparrow}_{i\in I}h_{i}^{*}(C)
. Since integration with respect to the continuous
valuation
\mu
is Scott-continuous,

\Psi(\mu)(h)=\sup\nolimits^{\scriptstyle\uparrow}_{i\in I}\Psi(\mu)(h_{i})
, showing that

\Psi(\mu)
is Scott-continuous. For all
a\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
,

h,h^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}X
, we have
(ah)_{*}=ah_{*}
and

(h+h^{\prime})_{*}\leq h_{*}+{h^{\prime}}_{*}
, so
\Psi(\mu)
is sublinear.
Additionally,
(\mathbf{1}+h)_{*}=\mathbf{1}+h_{*}
, so
\Psi(\mu)
is
(sub)normalized if
\mu
is a (sub)probability valuation. Hence

\Psi(\mu)\in\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X
.

Finally,
\Psi^{-1}([h>r])
is the collection of all continuous
valuations
\mu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X
such that

\int_{C\in{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}h_{*}(C)\,d\mu>r
, namely
[h^{*}>r]
. In
particular,
\Psi
is continuous. ∎

Proposition 7.2

Let
\bullet
be nothing, “
\leq 1
” or “
1
”, and
X
be an

{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}_{\bullet}
-friendly space. The continuous function

{\lambda^{\flat}_{X}}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}s_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}\circ\Psi\colon{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}\to{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}
maps every

\mu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}
to the collection of continuous
valuations
\nu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X
such that

\nu(U)\leq\mu(\Diamond U)
for every
U\in{\mathcal{O}}X
. On
subbasic open sets,

\displaystyle{\lambda^{\flat}_{X}}^{-1}(\Diamond{\bigcap_{i=1}^{n}[h_{i}>1]})

\displaystyle=\bigcap_{\vec{a}\in\Delta_{n}}\left[\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}h_{i}\right)_{*}>1\right]

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{N>n}\bigcap_{\vec{b}\in\Delta_{n}^{N}}\left[\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}b_{i}h_{i}\right)_{*}>1\right].

Proof 14

The formula for inverse images is a direct consequence of
Lemma
6.3
and of Lemma
7.1
.

Let
\mu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}
.
The elements
\nu
of
{\lambda^{\flat}_{X}}(\mu)
are those
\nu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X
such that
\int_{x\in X}h(x)\,d\nu\leq\int_{C\in{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}h_{*}(C)\,d\mu
for every
h\in\mathcal{L}X
.
In particular, for
h\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\chi_{U}
, where
U\in{\mathcal{O}}X
, it is
easy to
check that
h_{*}=\chi_{\Diamond U}
, so
\nu(U)\leq\mu(\Diamond U)
.
Conversely, if
\nu(U)\leq\mu(\Diamond U)
for every
U\in{\mathcal{O}}X
, for every
h\in\mathcal{L}X
,

\int_{x\in X}h(x)\,d\nu=\int_{0}^{\infty}\nu(h^{-1}(]t,\infty]))\,dt\leq\int_{0}^{\infty}\mu(\Diamond{h^{-1}(]t,\infty])})\,dt=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mu(h_{*}^{-1}(]t,\infty]))\,dt=\int_{x\in X}h_{*}(x)\,d\mu
. ∎

When
n=1
,
\Delta_{n}=\{(1)\}
, and
\Delta_{n}^{N}=\{(1)\}
for
every
N>n
. Lemma
6.3
and
Proposition
7.2
then specialize to the following.

Corollary 7.3

Let
\bullet
be nothing, “
\leq 1
” or “
1
”, and let
X
be an

{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}_{\bullet}
-friendly space. For every
h\in\mathcal{L}X
,

\displaystyle{(s_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet})}^{-1}(\Diamond{[h>1]})

\displaystyle=[h>1]

\displaystyle{\lambda^{\flat}_{X}}^{-1}(\Diamond{[h>1]})

\displaystyle=[h_{*}>1].

Remark 7.4

Let
X
be
{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}_{\bullet}
-friendly. For every

\mu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}
,
\lambda^{\flat}_{X}(\mu)
is also the
set

\{\nu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X\mid\forall h\in\mathcal{L}X,\int_{x\in X}h(x)\,d\nu\leq\int_{C\in{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}\sup_{x\in C}h(x)\,d\mu\}
.

In order to illustrate what
{\lambda^{\flat}_{X}}
does, let us look at
the special case where
\mu\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\delta_{C_{i}}
,
where
n\geq 1
,
C_{i}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\dc E_{i}
and where each set
E_{i}
is
non-empty and finite. For every
h\in\mathcal{L}X
,

\int_{C\in{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}\sup_{x\in C}h(x)\,d\mu=\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\sup_{x_{i}\in C_{i}}h(x_{i})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\max_{x_{i}\in E_{i}}h(x_{i})=\max_{x_{1}\in E_{1},\cdots,x_{n}\in E_{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}h(x_{i})
, which is equal to

\max_{x_{1}\in E_{1},\cdots,x_{n}\in E_{n}}\int_{x\in X}h(x)\,d\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\delta_{x_{i}}
, so

\Psi=r_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}(\dc\mathcal{E})
where
\mathcal{E}
is the finite set

\{\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\delta_{x_{i}}\mid x_{1}\in E_{1},\cdots,x_{n}\in E_{n}\}
. By Proposition 4.19 of
[
17
]
(see also the
errata
[
21
]
), which applies since
X
is

{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}_{\bullet}
-friendly,
s_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}\circ r_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}
maps every
closed subset
C
of
{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X
to its closed convex hull,
namely to
cl(conv(C)
, where
conv
denotes convex hull. Hence

{\lambda^{\flat}_{X}}(\mu)=cl(conv(\{\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\delta_{x_{i}}\mid x_{1}\in E_{1},\cdots,x_{n}\in E_{n}\}))
.

We will use the following easily proved fact.

Fact 7.5

For every topological space
X
, for every
h\in\mathcal{L}X
,
for every subset
A
of
X
,
\sup_{x\in cl(A)}h(x)=\sup_{x\in A}h(x)
.

Indeed, we use the fact that an open subset intersects
cl(A)
if and
only if it intersects
A
. Then, for every
t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
,

\sup_{x\in cl(A)}h(x)>t
if and only if the open set

h^{-1}(]t,\infty])
intersects
cl(A)
, if and only if it
intersects
A
, if and only if
\sup_{x\in A}h(x)>t
.

Lemma 7.6

\lambda^{\flat}
is a natural transformation on
\mathbf{Top}^{\flat}
.

Proof 15

Since
s_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}
is natural, it suffices to show that
\Psi
is
natural. Let
f\colon X\to Y
be any continuous map. We need to
show that

\Psi\circ{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}f}=\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}f\circ\Psi
.
Let
\mu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}
and
h\in\mathcal{L}Y
. Then

(\Psi\circ{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}f})(\mu)(h)=\Psi({\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}f[\mu])(h)=\int_{C\in{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}Y}\sup_{y\in C}h(y)\,d{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}f[\mu]
. By
the change of variable formula, this is equal to

\int_{C\in{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}\sup_{y\in{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}f(C)}h(y)\,d\mu
. Now

\sup_{y\in{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}f(C)}h(y)=\sup_{y\in cl(f[C])}h(y)=\sup_{y\in f[C]}h(y)
(by Fact
7.5
)

=\sup_{x\in C}h(f(x))
. We therefore obtain that

(\Psi\circ{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}f})(\mu)(h)=\int_{C\in{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}\sup_{x\in C}h(f(x))\,d\mu
. But

(\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}f\circ\Psi)(\mu)(h)=\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}f(\Psi(\mu))(h)=\Psi(\mu)(h\circ f)=\int_{C\in{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}\sup_{x\in C}h(f(x))\,d\mu
. ∎

We prove the first of the weak distributivity laws.

Lemma 7.7

For every space
X
in
\mathbf{Top}^{\flat}
,

{\lambda^{\flat}_{X}}\circ{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{\eta^{\mathcal{H}}_{X}}=\eta^{\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}
.

Proof 16

For every
\nu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X
, the elements of

({\lambda^{\flat}_{X}}\circ{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{\eta^{\mathcal{H}}_{X}})(\nu)
are
those
\nu^{\prime}\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X
such that

\nu^{\prime}(U)\leq{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{\eta^{\mathcal{H}}_{X}}(\nu)(\Diamond U)
for
every
U\in{\mathcal{O}}X
. Now

{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{\eta^{\mathcal{H}}_{X}}(\nu)(\Diamond U)=\nu({(\eta^{\mathcal{H}}_{X})}^{-1}(\Diamond U))=\nu(U)
, so

({\lambda^{\flat}_{X}}\circ{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{\eta^{\mathcal{H}}_{X}})(\nu)
is
simply the collection of
\nu^{\prime}\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X
such that

\nu^{\prime}\leq\nu
, namely
\eta^{\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}(\nu)
. ∎

For the remaining proofs, we will use Trick A, and the following
variant of Trick B.

Trick C.

In order to
show that
f=g
, where
f
and
g
are continuous map from a space

Y
to a space of the form
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}Z}
, where
Z
is an
object of
\mathbf{Top}^{\flat}
, show that
f(y)
and
g(y)
are convex
for every
y\in Y
, and then show that

r_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}\circ f=r_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}\circ g
.

Indeed, we recall that
r_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}
is a homeomorphism of

{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{cvx}({\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}Z)
onto
\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}Z
.

We also note the following. In a semitopological cone, the closure of
a convex subsets is convex
[
32
, Lemma 4.10 (a)]
,
and we obtain the following as an easy consequence.

Fact 7.8

Given any convex subspace
Z
of a semitopological cone, the closure
of any convex subset of
Z
in
Z
is convex.

Lemma 7.9

For every object
X
of
\mathbf{Top}^{\flat}
,

{\lambda^{\flat}_{X}}\circ{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{\mu^{\mathcal{H}}_{X}}=\mu^{\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}\circ{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\lambda^{\flat}_{X}}\circ\lambda^{\flat}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}
.

The presence of the term
\lambda^{\flat}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}
is why we require

\mathbf{Top}^{\flat}
to be closed under
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}
, see
Definition
5.6
.

Proof 17

Since
{\lambda^{\flat}_{X}}=s_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}\circ\Psi
and the image
of
s_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}
consists of convex subsets, the left-hand side
takes its values in convex sets. For the right-hand side, for every

\xi\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}{{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}}
,

(\mu^{\mathcal{Q}}_{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}\circ{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\lambda^{\flat}_{X}}\circ\lambda^{\flat}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}X})(\xi)
is the closure of

\bigcup({\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\lambda^{\flat}_{X}}\circ\lambda^{\flat}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}X})(\xi)=\bigcup cl(\{\lambda^{\flat}_{X}(\mu)\mid\mu\in\lambda^{\flat}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}(\xi)\})
, hence also of

\bigcup\{\lambda^{\flat}_{X}(\mu)\mid\mu\in\lambda^{\flat}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}(\xi)\}
. In order to show that

(\mu^{\mathcal{Q}}_{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}\circ{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\lambda^{\flat}_{X}}\circ\lambda^{\flat}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}X})(\xi)
is convex, it suffices to show that

A\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\bigcup\{\lambda^{\flat}_{X}(\mu)\mid\mu\in\lambda^{\flat}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}(\xi)\}
is, by Fact
7.8
.

Let
a\in[0,1]
, and
\nu_{1},\nu_{2}\in A
. there is a

\mu_{1}\in\lambda^{\flat}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}(\xi)
such that

\nu_{1}\in{\lambda^{\flat}_{X}}(\mu_{1})
and there is a

\mu_{2}\in\lambda^{\flat}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}(\xi)
such that

\nu_{2}\in{\lambda^{\flat}_{X}}(\mu_{2})
. We note that

\lambda^{\flat}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}(\xi)=(s_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}\circ\Psi)(\xi)
is
convex, so
a\mu_{1}+(1-a)\mu_{2}\in\lambda^{\flat}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}(\xi)
.
Additionally,

a\nu_{1}+(1-a)\nu_{2}\in{\lambda^{\flat}_{X}}(a\mu_{1}+(1-a)\mu_{2})
:
for every
U\in{\mathcal{O}}X
,
\nu_{1}(U)\leq\mu_{1}(\Diamond U)
and

\nu_{2}(U)\leq\mu_{2}(\Diamond U)
by definition of

{\lambda^{\flat}_{X}}
, so

(a\nu_{1}+(1-a)\nu_{2})(U)\leq(a\mu_{1}+(1-a)\mu_{2})(\Diamond U)
. Therefore there is a
\mu\in\lambda^{\flat}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}(\xi)
,
namely
\mu\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}a\mu_{1}+(1-a)\mu_{2}
, such that

a\nu_{1}+(1-a)\nu_{2}\in{\lambda^{\flat}_{X}}(\mu)
. Equivalently,

a\nu_{1}+(1-a)\nu_{2}
is in
A
. Hence
A
is convex, as
promised.

It follows that we can use Trick C, and so we prove the following
instead:

\displaystyle\Psi\circ{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{\mu^{\mathcal{H}}_{X}}

\displaystyle=r_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}\circ\mu^{\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}\circ{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\lambda^{\flat}_{X}}\circ\lambda^{\flat}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}.

Indeed,

r_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}\circ{\lambda^{\flat}_{X}}=r_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}\circ s_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}\circ\Psi=\Psi
, which allows us to simplify the left-hand side.

We now use Trick A:

\displaystyle[h>1]

\displaystyle{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\Longrightarrow}\limits^{{\Psi}}}}[h_{*}>1]

by Lemma
7.1

\displaystyle{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\Longrightarrow}\limits^{{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{\mu^{\mathcal{H}}_{X}}}}}}[h_{*}\circ\mu^{\mathcal{H}}_{X}>1]=[h_{**}>1],

where for every
\mathcal{C}\in{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}{{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}
,

h_{**}(\mathcal{C})=\sup_{C\in\mathcal{C}}h_{*}(C)=\sup_{C\in\mathcal{C}}\sup_{x\in C}h(x)
; indeed, the latter is equal to

\sup_{x\in\bigcup\mathcal{C}}h(x)=\sup_{x\in cl(\bigcup\mathcal{C})}h(x)
(by Fact
7.5
)

=h_{*}(\mu^{\mathcal{H}}_{X}(\mathcal{C}))
.

\displaystyle[h>1]

\displaystyle{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\Longrightarrow}\limits^{{r_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}}}}}\Diamond{[h>1]}

by Fact
6.4

\displaystyle{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\Longrightarrow}\limits^{{\mu^{\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}}}}}\Diamond{\Diamond{[h>1]}}

\displaystyle{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\Longrightarrow}\limits^{{{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}{{\lambda^{\flat}_{X}}}}}}}\Diamond{{\lambda^{\flat}_{X}}^{-1}(\Diamond{[h>1]})}=\Diamond{[h_{*}>1]}

by Corollary
7.3

\displaystyle{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\Longrightarrow}\limits^{{\lambda^{\flat}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}}}}}[h_{**}>1]

\displaystyle\text{by Corollary~\ref{corl:lambda:flat:inv:func:spec}}.

∎

Lemma 7.10

For every object
X
of
\mathbf{Top}^{\flat}
,

{\lambda^{\flat}_{X}}\circ\mu_{{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}={\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\mu_{X}}\circ\lambda^{\flat}_{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}\circ{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{\lambda^{\flat}_{X}}
.

The term
\lambda^{\flat}_{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}
is why we require

\mathbf{Top}^{\flat}
to be closed under
{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}
(see
Definition
5.6
).

Proof 18

The left-hand side takes its values in convex sets. We claim that
the right-hand side does, too. For every

\xi\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}}
,

({\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\mu_{X}}\circ\lambda^{\flat}_{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}\circ{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{\lambda^{\flat}_{X}})(\xi)
is the closure of

A\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\bigcup\{\mu_{X}(\mu)\mid\mu\in\lambda^{\flat}_{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}({\lambda^{\flat}_{X}}[\xi])\}
so it
suffices to show that
A
is convex, and to rely on
Fact
7.8
. For all
a\in[0,1]
,

\mu_{1},\mu_{2}\in\lambda^{\flat}_{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}({\lambda^{\flat}_{X}}[\xi])
,
\mu\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}a\mu_{1}+(1-a)\mu_{2}
is in

\lambda^{\flat}_{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}({\lambda^{\flat}_{X}}[\xi])
, since

\lambda^{\flat}_{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}
takes its values in convex sets.
Then

\mu_{X}(\mu)=(\mathcal{U}\in{\mathcal{O}}{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}\mapsto\int_{\nu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}\nu(\mathcal{U})\,d\mu)=a\mu_{X}(\mu_{1})+(1-a)\mu_{X}(\mu_{2})
is in
A
, showing that
A
is convex.

We can therefore use Trick C, and since
r_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}\circ{\lambda^{\flat}_{X}}=r_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}\circ s_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}\circ\Psi=\Psi
, it remains to show:

\displaystyle\Psi\circ\mu_{{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}

\displaystyle=r_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}\circ{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\mu_{X}}\circ\lambda^{\flat}_{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}\circ{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{\lambda^{\flat}_{X}}.

To this end, we use Trick A:

\displaystyle[h>1]

\displaystyle{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\Longrightarrow}\limits^{{\Psi}}}}[h_{*}>1]

by Lemma
7.1

\displaystyle{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\Longrightarrow}\limits^{{\mu_{{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}}}}}\left[\left(\mu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}\mapsto\int_{C\in{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}h_{*}(C)\,d\mu\right)>1\right]

by (
1
)

while:

\displaystyle[h>1]

\displaystyle{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\Longrightarrow}\limits^{{r_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}}}}}\Diamond{[h>1]}

by Fact
6.4

\displaystyle{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\Longrightarrow}\limits^{{{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\mu_{X}}}}}}\Diamond{\mu_{X}^{-1}([h>1])}=\Diamond{\left[\left(\nu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X\mapsto\int_{x\in X}h(x)\,d\nu\right)>1\right]}\mskip-80.0mu

by (
1
)

\displaystyle{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\Longrightarrow}\limits^{{\lambda^{\flat}_{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}}}}}\left[\left(\nu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X\mapsto\int_{x\in X}h(x)\,d\nu\right)_{*}>1\right]

by Corollary
7.3

\displaystyle{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\Longrightarrow}\limits^{{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{\lambda^{\flat}_{X}}}}}}\left[\left(\nu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X\mapsto\int_{x\in X}h(x)\,d\nu\right)_{*}\circ{\lambda^{\flat}_{X}}>1\right].

In order to conclude, it suffices to show that:

\displaystyle\left(\nu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X\mapsto\int_{x\in X}h(x)\,d\nu\right)_{*}\circ{\lambda^{\flat}_{X}}

\displaystyle=(\mu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}\mapsto\int_{C\in{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}h_{*}(C)\,d\mu).

The left-hand side maps every
\mu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}
to

\displaystyle\sup_{\nu\in s_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}(\Psi(\mu))}\int_{x\in X}h(x)\,d\nu

\displaystyle=r_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}(s_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}(\Psi(\mu)))(h)

\displaystyle=\Psi(\mu)(h)=\int_{C\in{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}h_{*}(C),

and this completes the proof. ∎

Putting Lemma
7.6
, Lemma
7.7
,
Lemma
7.9
and Lemma
7.10

together, we obtain the following.

Lemma 8.2

For every topological space

.

If

is an object of

, then a splitting of the

) where

\alpha\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\alpha_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}
is

\displaystyle\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 21.1958pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-21.1958pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 45.1958pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 45.1958pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{cvx}{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 119.2804pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 119.2804pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces,

where the arrow on the right is the inclusion map, and the arrow on

), and

, we have

, for

of

. Since

),

\mathbf{Top}^{\flat}
, too.

Proof 20

The map
F\mapsto F(h)
is lower semicontinuous, since the inverse
image of
]t,\infty]
is
[h>t]
, for all
h\in\mathcal{L}X
and

t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
. Hence the integral

\int_{F\in\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}F(h)\,d\xi
makes sense. The
map
{\alpha_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}}(\xi)
is Scott-continuous because each

F\in\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X
is and because integration with respect
to
\nu
is Scott-continuous; it is sublinear because
F
is and
integration with respect to
\xi
is linear. Additionally,

{(\alpha_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet})}^{-1}([h>r])=[(F\mapsto F(h))>r]
,
so
{\alpha_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}}
is continuous.

We claim that the map
\alpha_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}
is a

{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}
-algebra. We have

\alpha_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}(\eta_{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}(F))(h)=\int_{F^{\prime}\in\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}F^{\prime}(h)\,d\delta_{F}=F(h)
, so

\alpha_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}\circ\eta_{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}
is the
identity map. In order to show that

\alpha_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}\circ\mu_{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}=\alpha_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}\circ{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{\alpha_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}}
, we use
Trick A:

\displaystyle[h>1]

\displaystyle{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\Longrightarrow}\limits^{{\alpha_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}}}}}[(F\in\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X\mapsto F(h))>1]

\displaystyle{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\Longrightarrow}\limits^{{\mu_{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}}}}}[(\xi\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}\mapsto\int_{F\in\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}F(h)\,d\xi)>1]

by (
1
)

\displaystyle[h>1]

\displaystyle{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\Longrightarrow}\limits^{{\alpha_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}}}}}[(F\in\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X\mapsto F(h))>1]

\displaystyle{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\Longrightarrow}\limits^{{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{\alpha_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}}}}}}[(F\in\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X\mapsto F(h))\circ\alpha_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}>1]

\displaystyle=[(\xi\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}\mapsto\alpha_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}(\xi)(h))>1]

\displaystyle=[(\xi\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}\mapsto\int_{F\in\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}F(h)\,d\xi)>1].

In order to justify the announced splitting of
(
7
), we need to show that the image of
(
7
) is exactly
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{cvx}{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}
.
For every
C\in{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}X
, the image of
C
by (
7
)
is
cl(\alpha_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}[C^{\prime}])
where

C^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\lambda^{\flat}_{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}(\delta_{C})
is
convex, and
\alpha_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}
is linear (in its
\xi
argument),
so that image is convex, using Fact
7.8
.

In order to see that every element
C
of

{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{cvx}{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}
is reached as the image of some
element by (
7
), we simply show that
C
is its
own image by (
7
), or equivalently, that
(
7
) restricts to the identity on

{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{cvx}{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}
. We use Trick A:

\displaystyle\Diamond{\bigcap_{i=1}^{n}[h_{i}>1]}

\displaystyle{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\Longrightarrow}\limits^{{{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\alpha_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}}}}}}\Diamond{\bigcap_{i=1}^{n}{(\alpha_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet})}^{-1}([h_{i}>1])}

\displaystyle=\Diamond{\bigcap_{i=1}^{n}[(F\mapsto F(h_{i}))>1]}

\displaystyle{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\Longrightarrow}\limits^{{\lambda^{\flat}_{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}}}}}\bigcap_{\vec{a}\in\Delta_{n}}\left[\left(F\mapsto\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}F(h_{i})\right)_{*}>1\right]

by Proposition
7.2

\displaystyle{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\Longrightarrow}\limits^{{\eta_{{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}}}}}}\bigcap_{\vec{a}\in\Delta_{n}}\left(\left(F\mapsto\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}F(h_{i})\right)_{*}\right)^{-1}(]1,\infty]),\mskip-50.0mu

and we claim that the convex elements
C
of
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}
that are in the latter open set are exactly those of
\Diamond{\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}[h_{i}>1]}
, which will show the claim.

For every
C\in{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}
,

C\in\bigcap_{\vec{a}\in\Delta_{n}}\left(\left(F\mapsto\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}F(h_{i})\right)_{*}\right)^{-1}(]1,\infty])
if
and only for every
\vec{a}\in\Delta_{n}
,

\sup_{F\in C}\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}F(h_{i})>1
. Let

f\colon\Delta_{n}\times C\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
map
(\vec{a},F)
to

\int_{x\in X}\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}F(h_{i})
. This is a lower
semicontinuous map in its first argument,
\Delta_{n}
is compact (and
Hausdorff), as in the proof of Lemma
6.3
, and

f
preserves pairwise linear combinations (with coefficients
a

and
1-a
,
a\in[0,1]
) of its first arguments, and similarly
with its second arguments (which exist because
C
is convex), so by
Remark 3.4 and the Minimax Theorem 3.3 of
[
18
]
,

\sup_{F\in C}\inf_{\vec{a}\in\Delta_{n}}f(\vec{a},F)=\min_{\vec{a}\in\Delta_{n}}\sup_{F\in C}f(\vec{a},F)
. But the
elements
C\in{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}
that are in

\bigcap_{\vec{a}\in\Delta_{n}}\left(\left(F\mapsto\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}F(h_{i})\right)_{*}\right)^{-1}(]1,\infty])
are those such
that
\min_{\vec{a}\in\Delta_{n}}\sup_{F\in C}f(\vec{a},F)>1
,
hence such that

\sup_{F\in C}\inf_{\vec{a}\in\Delta_{n}}f(\vec{a},F)>1
, hence
such that for some
F\in C
,

\inf_{\vec{a}\in\Delta_{n}}f(\vec{a},F)>1
. Since

f(\vec{a},F)
is linear in
\vec{a}
(and lower semicontinuous on a
compact set), the latter inf is reached at one of the vertices of

\Delta_{n}
, so
\inf_{\vec{a}\in\Delta_{n}}f(\vec{a},F)>1
is
equivalent to
F(h_{i})>1
for every
i\in\{1,\cdots,n\}
.
Therefore, for every
C\in{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}
,

C\in\bigcap_{\vec{a}\in\Delta_{n}}\left(\left(F\mapsto\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}F(h_{i})\right)_{*}\right)^{-1}(]1,\infty])
if
and only if there is an
F\in C
such that
F(h_{i})>1
for every

i\in\{1,\cdots,n\}
, if and only if

C\in\Diamond{\bigcap_{i=1}^{n}[h_{i}>1]}
. ∎

Lemma 8.3

For
S\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}
,
T\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}
,

\lambda\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\lambda^{\flat}
, the monad

is

.

The multiplication
\mu^{\overline{S}}_{\mu_{X}}

{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}

\mu_{X}\colon{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}\to{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X
is
the function that maps every
C\in{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{cvx}{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}
to
(h\in\mathcal{L}X\mapsto\sup_{F\in C}F(h))\in\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X
; the inverse image of

by that
function is
\Diamond{[h>1]}\cap{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{cvx}{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}
,
for every
h\in\mathcal{L}X
.

\textstyle{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\textstyle{{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\alpha_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}}}

\scriptstyle{\mu^{\overline{S}}_{\mu_{X}}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}

For every
\mathcal{F}\in\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}
,

{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\alpha_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}}(s_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{F}))=cl(\{\alpha_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}(\xi)\mid\xi\in s_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{F})\})=cl(\{(h\in\mathcal{L}X\mapsto\int_{F\in\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}F(h)\,d\xi)\mid\xi\in s_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{F})\})
. It
is easy to see that this is a convex set, because

is convex, integration is linear in the
continuous valuation, and closure preserves convexity
(Fact

). Therefore

maps it to itself, and,

, item 3,

maps it to:

\displaystyle(h\in\mathcal{L}X\mapsto\sup_{F\in{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\alpha_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}}(s_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{F}))}F(h))

\displaystyle=(h\in\mathcal{L}X\mapsto\sup_{F\in cl(\alpha_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}[s_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{F})])}F(h))

\displaystyle=(h\in\mathcal{L}X\mapsto\sup_{F\in\alpha_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}[s_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{F})]}F(h))\quad\text{by Fact~\ref{fact:sup:cl}}

\displaystyle=(h\in\mathcal{L}X\mapsto\sup_{\xi\in s_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{F})}\alpha_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}(\xi)(h))

\displaystyle=(h\in\mathcal{L}X\mapsto\sup_{\xi\in s_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{F})}\int_{F\in\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}F(h)\,d\xi)

\displaystyle=(h\in\mathcal{L}X\mapsto\mathcal{F}(F\in\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X\mapsto F(h)),

and this is exactly what the multiplication of the

\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}
monad, evaluated at
X
and applied to

\mathcal{F}
, produces. ∎

Part III
A weak distributive law between
{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}
and
\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}
,
resp.
\mathcal{P}\ell_{\mathcal{V}}

Combining
{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}
and
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}
, there is a monad of lenses and a monad of
quasi-lenses, which we define below. We start with lenses, which are
probably more well-known. Quasi-lenses are easier to work with.

A
lens
is a non-empty set of the form
Q\cap C
where
Q
is
compact saturated and
C
is closed in
X
. The
Vietoris
topology
has subbasic open subsets of the form
\Box U
(the set of
lenses included in
U
) and
\Diamond U
(the set of lenses that
intersect
U
), for each open subset
U
of
X
. We write

\mathcal{P}\ell X
for the set of lenses on
X
, and we let
\mathcal{P}\ell_{\mathcal{V}}X

denote
\mathcal{P}\ell X
with the Vietoris topology. The specialization
ordering of
\mathcal{P}\ell_{\mathcal{V}}X
is the
topological Egli-Milner
ordering
:
L\sqsubseteq^{\text{TEM}}L^{\prime}
if and only if
\upc L\supseteq\upc L^{\prime}

and
cl(L)\subseteq cl(L^{\prime})

[
15
, Discussion before
Fact 4.1]
. This is an ordering, not just a preordering,
hence
\mathcal{P}\ell_{\mathcal{V}}X
is
T_{0}
.

A
quasi-lens
on a topological space
X
is a pair
(Q,C)
of a
compact saturated subset
Q
and a closed subset
C
of
X
such that:

1.

Q
intersects
C
;

2.

Q\subseteq\upc(Q\cap C)
;

3.

for every open neighborhood
U
of
Q
,
C\subseteq cl(U\cap C)
.

The notion originates from
[
27
, Theorem 9.6]
, and
was rediscovered in
[
15
, Section 5]
. We write
\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}X

for the space of quasi-lenses on
X
. The
Vietoris topology
on

\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}X
is generated by the subbasic open sets

\Box^{\mathrm{q}}U\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\{(Q,C)\in\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}X\mid Q\subseteq U\}
and

\Diamond^{\mathrm{q}}U\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\{(Q,C)\in\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}X\mid C\cap U\neq\emptyset\}
. We write
\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X
for
\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}X
with the Vietoris
topology. Its specialization preordering is

\supseteq\times\subseteq
, which is antisymmetric, so
\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X
is

T_{0}

[
23
, Lemma 8.2, item 2]
; also, the inclusion
of
\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X
into
{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X\times{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}X
is a topological embedding

[
23
, Lemma 8.2, item 1]
, and
\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}
extends to an
endofunctor on
\mathbf{Top}
, whose action on morphisms
f\colon X\to Y

is defined by

\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}f(Q,C)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}({\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}f(Q),{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}f(C))=(\upc f[Q],cl(f[C]))
, and is such that

{(\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}f)}^{-1}(\Box^{\mathrm{q}}V)=\Box^{\mathrm{q}}{f^{-1}(V)}
and

{(\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}f)}^{-1}(\Diamond^{\mathrm{q}}V)=\Diamond^{\mathrm{q}}{f^{-1}(V)}

for every
V\in{\mathcal{O}}Y

[
23
, Lemma 8.3]
.

The relationship between spaces of lenses and of quasi-lenses is as
follows. For every topological space
X
, there is a topological
embedding
\iota_{X}\colon\mathcal{P}\ell_{\mathcal{V}}X\to\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X
, defined by

\iota_{X}(L)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}(\upc L,cl(L))
. There is a map

\varrho_{X}\colon\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X\to\mathcal{P}\ell_{\mathcal{V}}X
defined by

\varrho_{X}(Q,C)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}Q\cap C
, and

\varrho_{X}\circ\iota_{X}=\mathrm{id}_{X}
. When
X
is weakly Hausdorff

[
20
, Lemma 6.1, Proposition 6.2, Theorem 6.3]
or
quasi-Polish
[
23
, Theorem 9.6]
, those define
inverse continuous maps, hence a homeomorphism between
\mathcal{P}\ell_{\mathcal{V}}X

and
\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X
. A space is
weakly Hausdorff
if and only if given
any two points
x
and
y
, every open neighborhood
W
of

\upc x\cap\upc y
contains an intersection
U\cap V
of an open
neighborhood
U
of
x
and an open neighborhood
V
of
y

[
33
, Lemma 6.6]
. Every Hausdorff space is weakly
Hausdorff, every stably locally compact space is weakly Hausdorff

[
33
, Lemma 8.1]
. The
quasi-Polish spaces
are
the topological spaces underlying a separable Smyth-complete
quasi-metric space
[
9
]
. We will not define them
any further, except than to notice that every
\omega
-continuous dcpo
is quasi-Polish in its Scott topology. (See Section 2.2 of

[
1
]
for continuous and
\omega
-continuous dcpos.)

We will write
\pi_{1}\colon\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X\to{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X
and

\pi_{2}\colon\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X\to{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}X
for the respective projections. For
every
U\in{\mathcal{O}}X
,
\pi_{1}^{-1}(\Box U)=\Box^{\mathrm{q}}U
and

\pi_{2}^{-1}(\Diamond U)=\Diamond^{\mathrm{q}}U
, so they are continuous.
Those define natural transformations
\pi_{1}\colon\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}\to{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}
and

\pi_{2}\colon\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}\to{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}
, as one can see easily using Trick A.

Proposition 8.5

There is a monad
(\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}},\eta^{\mathrm{q}},\mu^{\mathrm{q}})
on
\mathbf{Top}
,

•

whose unit
\eta^{\mathrm{q}}_{X}\colon X\to\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X
maps every

x\in X
to

(\eta^{\mathcal{Q}}_{X}(x),\eta^{\mathcal{H}}_{X}(x))=(\upc x,\dc x)
, and is
such that

{(\eta^{\mathrm{q}}_{X})}^{-1}(\Box^{\mathrm{q}}U)={(\eta^{\mathrm{q}}_{X})}^{-1}(\Diamond^{\mathrm{q}}U)=U
for every

\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

maps every

and

{(\mu^{\mathrm{q}}_{X})}^{-1}(\Diamond^{\mathrm{q}}U)=\Diamond^{\mathrm{q}}{\Diamond^{\mathrm{q}}U}
for every

, and is
characterized uniquely by the commutativity of the diagram:

\textstyle{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu^{\mathcal{Q}}_{X}}

\scriptstyle{\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{2}}

(11)

Proof 23

Unit. For every
x\in X
, we claim that

(Q,C)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}(\upc x,\dc x)
is a quasi-lens. First,

\upc x\cap\dc x
is non-empty, since it contains
x
. Second,

Q\subseteq\upc(Q\cap C)
, since every element of
Q
is larger
than or equal to
x
, which is in
Q\cap C
. Third, since

x\in Q\cap C
,
C=\dc x=cl(\{x\})\subseteq cl(Q\cap C)
,
from which is follows that for every open neighborhood
U
of
Q
,

C\subseteq(U\cap C)
. The commutativity of (
10
) is
an equivalent way of stating the definition of
\eta^{\mathrm{q}}_{X}

(modulo the fact that all arrows are indeed continuous maps, which
we show next).

For every
U\in{\mathcal{O}}X
, for every
x\in X
,

x\in{(\eta^{\mathrm{q}}_{X})}^{-1}(\Box^{\mathrm{q}}U)
if and only if

\eta^{\mathcal{Q}}_{X}(x)\in\Box U
, if and only if
x\in U
, and the
equality
{(\eta^{\mathrm{q}}_{X})}^{-1}(\Diamond^{\mathrm{q}}U)=U
is proved
similarly. In particular,
\eta^{\mathrm{q}}_{X}
is continuous.

Finally, the commutativity of (
10
) characterizes

\eta^{\mathrm{q}}_{X}
, since the pairing

\langle\pi_{1},\pi_{2}\rangle\colon\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X\to{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X\times{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}X

is the inclusion map, hence is injective.

Multiplication. For every

(\mathcal{Q},\mathcal{C})\in\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}
, let

(Q,C)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}(\bigcup\pi_{1}[\mathcal{Q}],\allowbreak cl(\bigcup\pi_{2}[\mathcal{C}]))
. We need to check that this is a quasi-lens.

We have

\mu^{\mathcal{Q}}_{X}({\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\pi_{1}}(\mathcal{Q}))=\bigcup\upc\pi_{1}[\mathcal{Q}]=\bigcup\pi_{1}[\mathcal{Q}]=Q
(because

\pi_{1}[\mathcal{Q}]
is upwards-closed, as one checks easily). In
particular,
Q
is in
{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X
. Also,

\mu^{\mathcal{H}}_{X}({\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\pi_{2}}(\mathcal{C}))=cl(\bigcup cl(\pi_{2}[\mathcal{C}]))
. The outer closure is taken in
X
, while the inner
closure is taken in
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}X
. We claim that this is equal to
C
.
Clearly,
C\subseteq cl(\bigcup cl(\pi_{2}[\mathcal{C}]))
.
Conversely, every open set
U
that intersects

cl(\bigcup cl(\pi_{2}[\mathcal{C}]))
intersects

\bigcup cl(\pi_{2}[\mathcal{C}])
, so there is an element of

cl(\pi_{2}[\mathcal{C}])
that intersects
U
; in other words,

cl(\pi_{2}[\mathcal{C}])
intersects
\Diamond U
, so

\pi_{2}[\mathcal{C}]
intersects
\Diamond U
, equivalently,

\bigcup\pi_{2}[\mathcal{C}]=C
intersects
U
. Since

C=\mu^{\mathcal{H}}_{X}({\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\pi_{2}}(\mathcal{C}))
, in particular

C\in{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}X
. Also, we have just shown that the diagram
(
11
) commutes (modulo the fact that all arrows are
continuous maps, which we will check later).

Since
(\mathcal{Q},\mathcal{C})
is a quasi-lens, there is an
element
(Q^{\prime},C^{\prime})
in
\mathcal{Q}\cap\mathcal{C}
. Then there is
an element
x
in
Q^{\prime}\cap C^{\prime}
, because
(Q^{\prime},C^{\prime})
is a quasi-lens.
Since
(Q^{\prime},C^{\prime})\in\mathcal{Q}
,
Q^{\prime}
is in
\pi_{1}[\mathcal{Q}]
, so

Q^{\prime}\subseteq\bigcup\pi_{1}[\mathcal{Q}]=Q
; then

x\in Q^{\prime}\subseteq Q
. Since
(Q^{\prime},C^{\prime})\in\mathcal{C}
,
C^{\prime}
is in

\pi_{2}[\mathcal{C}]
, so

C^{\prime}\subseteq cl(\bigcup\pi_{2}[\mathcal{C}])=C
; then

x\in C^{\prime}\subseteq C
. It follows that
Q\cap C\neq\emptyset
.

Let us show that
Q\subseteq\upc(Q\cap C)
. For every

x\in Q=\bigcup\pi_{1}[\mathcal{Q}]
, there is quasi-lens

(Q^{\prime},C^{\prime})\in\mathcal{Q}
such that
x\in Q^{\prime}
. Since

(\mathcal{Q},\mathcal{C})
is itself a quasi-lens,

\mathcal{Q}\subseteq\upc(\mathcal{Q}\cap\mathcal{C})
, so there
is a quasi-lens
(Q^{\prime\prime},C^{\prime\prime})\in\mathcal{Q}\cap\mathcal{C}
such
that
(Q^{\prime\prime},C^{\prime\prime})\mathrel{(\supseteq\times\subseteq)}(Q^{\prime},C^{\prime})
.
We have
Q^{\prime\prime}\supseteq Q^{\prime}
and
x\in Q^{\prime}
, so
x\in Q^{\prime\prime}
; also,

Q^{\prime\prime}\subseteq\upc(Q^{\prime\prime}\cap C^{\prime\prime})
, so there is a point

x^{\prime\prime}\in Q^{\prime\prime}\cap C^{\prime\prime}
such that
x^{\prime\prime}\leq x
. Since
x^{\prime\prime}\in Q^{\prime\prime}

and
(Q^{\prime\prime},C^{\prime\prime})\in\mathcal{Q}
,
x^{\prime\prime}
is in

\bigcup\pi_{1}[\mathcal{Q}]=Q
, and since
x^{\prime\prime}\in C^{\prime\prime}
and

(Q^{\prime\prime},C^{\prime\prime})\in\mathcal{C}
,
x^{\prime\prime}
is in

\bigcup\pi_{2}[\mathcal{C}]
, hence in its closure
C
. We have
obtained that
x
is larger than or equal to a point
x^{\prime\prime}
in

Q\cap C
, so
x\in\upc(Q\cap C)
.

We now show that for every open neighborhood
U
of
Q
,

C\subseteq cl(U\cap C)
. It suffices to show that for every
open neighborhood
U
of
Q
, every open set
V
that intersects
C

also intersects
U\cap C
. Since

Q=\bigcup\pi_{1}[\mathcal{Q}]\subseteq U
, every quasi-lens

(Q^{\prime},C^{\prime})\in\mathcal{Q}
is such that
Q^{\prime}\subseteq U
, namely is
in
\Box^{\mathrm{q}}U
. Therefore
\mathcal{Q}\subseteq\Box^{\mathrm{q}}U
.
Since
(\mathcal{Q},\mathcal{C})
is a quasi-lens,

\mathcal{C}\subseteq cl(\mathcal{C}\cap\mathcal{\Box}^{\mathrm{q}}U)
.
Hence every open subset
\mathcal{V}
of
\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X
that intersects

\mathcal{C}
also intersects

\mathcal{C}\cap\mathcal{\Box}^{\mathrm{q}}U
. Since

C=cl(\bigcup\pi_{2}[\mathcal{C}])
and
V
intersects
C
,
V

intersects
\bigcup\pi_{2}[\mathcal{C}]
, hence
V
intersects some
closed set
C^{\prime}
such that
(Q^{\prime},C^{\prime})\in\mathcal{C}
. In other
words,
\Diamond^{\mathrm{q}}V
intersects
\mathcal{C}
. Taking

\mathcal{V}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\Diamond^{\mathrm{q}}V
, we obtain that

\Diamond^{\mathrm{q}}V
intersects

\mathcal{C}\cap\mathcal{\Box}^{\mathrm{q}}U
. In other words, there is
a quasi-lens
(Q^{\prime},C^{\prime})
in
\mathcal{C}
such that
Q^{\prime}\subseteq U

and
C^{\prime}
intersects
V
. Since
(Q^{\prime},C^{\prime})
is a quasi-lens,

C^{\prime}\subseteq cl(C^{\prime}\cap U)
, so
V
intersects
C^{\prime}\cap U
. Hence

C^{\prime}
intersects
U\cap V
. But, since
(Q^{\prime},C^{\prime})\in\mathcal{C}
,

C^{\prime}
is in
\pi_{2}[\mathcal{C}]
, so

C^{\prime}\subseteq C=cl(\bigcup\pi_{2}[\mathcal{C}])
. Therefore
C

also intersects
U\cap V
, showing that
V
intersects
U\cap C
.

It remains to show that
\mu^{\mathrm{q}}_{X}
is continuous. For every
open subset
U
of
X
,

{(\mu^{\mathrm{q}}_{X})}^{-1}(\Box^{\mathrm{q}}U)=\{(\mathcal{Q},\mathcal{C})\in\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}\mid\bigcup\pi_{1}[\mathcal{Q}]\subseteq U\}=\{(\mathcal{Q},\mathcal{C})\in\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}\mid\forall(Q,C)\in\mathcal{Q},Q\subseteq U\}=\Box^{\mathrm{q}}{\Box^{\mathrm{q}}U}
, and

{(\mu^{\mathrm{q}}_{X})}^{-1}(\Diamond^{\mathrm{q}}U)=\{(\mathcal{Q},\mathcal{C})\in\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}\mid cl(\bigcup\pi_{2}[\mathcal{C}])\cap U\neq\emptyset\}=\{(\mathcal{Q},\mathcal{C})\in\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}\mid\bigcup\pi_{2}[\mathcal{C}]\cap U\neq\emptyset\}=\{(\mathcal{Q},\mathcal{C})\in\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}\mid\exists(Q,C)\in\mathcal{C},C\cap U\neq\emptyset\}=\Diamond^{\mathrm{q}}{\Diamond^{\mathrm{q}}U}
.

There are two easy ways to verify that
\eta^{\mathrm{q}}
and

\mu^{\mathrm{q}}
are natural, and the monad laws. One is by using
Trick A, and the other one is as follows. Given any two maps

f,g\colon Y\to\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}Z
(for some spaces
Y
and
Z
),
f=g
if
and only if
\pi_{1}\circ f=\pi_{1}\circ g
and

\pi_{2}\circ f=\pi_{2}\circ g
, since the pairing

\langle\pi_{1},\pi_{2}\rangle
is the inclusion map, hence is
injective. We use this new trick, as it will be clear that this
reduces the question for
\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}
to those for
{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}
and
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}
.

Naturality. Given any continuous map
f\colon X\to Y
, we check
that
\eta^{\mathrm{q}}_{Y}\circ f=\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}f\circ\eta^{\mathrm{q}}_{X}
:

\pi_{1}\circ\eta^{\mathrm{q}}_{Y}\circ f=\eta^{\mathcal{Q}}_{Y}\circ f
(by
(
10
)
={\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}f\circ\eta^{\mathcal{Q}}_{X}
(by naturality of

\eta^{\mathcal{Q}}
)
=\pi_{1}\circ\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}f\circ\eta^{\mathcal{Q}}_{X}
, and
similarly

\pi_{2}\circ\eta^{\mathrm{q}}_{Y}\circ f=\pi_{2}\circ\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}f\circ\eta^{\mathcal{Q}}_{X}
.

We also check that
\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}f\circ\mu^{\mathrm{q}}_{X}=\mu^{\mathrm{q}}_{Y}\circ\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}f}
by:

\displaystyle\pi_{1}\circ\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}f\circ\mu^{\mathrm{q}}_{X}

\displaystyle={\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}f\circ\pi_{1}\circ\mu^{\mathrm{q}}_{X}

def. of
\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}f

\displaystyle={\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}f\circ\mu^{\mathcal{Q}}_{X}\circ{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\pi_{1}}\circ\pi_{1}

by (
11
)

\displaystyle=\mu^{\mathcal{Q}}_{Y}\circ{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}f}\circ{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\pi_{1}}\circ\pi_{1}

nat. of
\mu^{\mathcal{Q}}

\displaystyle=\mu^{\mathcal{Q}}_{Y}\circ{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\pi_{1}}\circ{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}f}\circ\pi_{1}

nat. of
\pi_{1}

\displaystyle=\mu^{\mathcal{Q}}_{Y}\circ{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\pi_{1}}\circ\pi_{1}\circ\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}f}

nat. of
\pi_{1}

\displaystyle=\pi_{1}\circ\mu^{\mathrm{q}}_{Y}\circ\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}f}

\displaystyle\text{by (\ref{eq:muq})},

and similarly with
\pi_{2}
instead of
\pi_{1}
,
{\mathcal{H}}
in lieu of

{\mathcal{Q}}
, and
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}
instead of
{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}
.

The monad laws. We proceed similarly. We verify that
\mu^{\mathrm{q}}_{X}\circ\eta^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}=\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}
by:

\displaystyle\pi_{1}\circ\mu^{\mathrm{q}}_{X}\circ\eta^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}

\displaystyle=\mu^{\mathcal{Q}}_{X}\circ{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\pi_{1}}\circ\pi_{1}\circ\eta^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}

by (
11
)

\displaystyle=\mu^{\mathcal{Q}}_{X}\circ{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\pi_{1}}\circ\eta^{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}

by (

)

\displaystyle=\mu^{\mathcal{Q}}_{X}\circ\eta_{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}\circ\pi_{1}

nat. of
\pi_{1}

\displaystyle=\pi_{1},

by the monad law

\mu^{\mathcal{Q}}_{X}\circ\eta^{\mathcal{Q}}_{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}=\mathrm{id}_{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}
for

{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}
; similarly with
\pi_{2}
instead of
\pi_{1}
.

We verify that

\mu^{\mathrm{q}}_{X}\circ\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\eta^{\mathrm{q}}_{X}}=\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}
by:

\displaystyle\pi_{1}\circ\mu^{\mathrm{q}}_{X}\circ\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\eta^{\mathrm{q}}_{X}}

\displaystyle=\mu^{\mathcal{Q}}_{X}\circ{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\pi_{1}}\circ\pi_{1}\circ\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\eta^{\mathrm{q}}_{X}}

by (
11
)

\displaystyle=\mu^{\mathcal{Q}}_{X}\circ{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\pi_{1}}\circ{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\eta^{\mathrm{q}}_{X}}\circ\pi_{1}

def. of
\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}
on morphisms

\displaystyle=\mu^{\mathcal{Q}}_{X}\circ{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\eta^{\mathcal{Q}}_{X}}\circ\pi_{1}

by (
10
)

\displaystyle=\pi_{1},

by the monad law

\mu^{\mathcal{Q}}_{X}\circ{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\eta^{\mathcal{Q}}_{X}}=\mathrm{id}_{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}
for

{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}
; similarly with
\pi_{2}
instead of
\pi_{1}
.

Finally, we verify that
\mu^{\mathrm{q}}_{X}\circ\mu^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}=\mu^{\mathrm{q}}_{X}\circ\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\mu^{\mathrm{q}}_{X}}
. Once again, we do this for

\pi_{1}
only:

\displaystyle\pi_{1}\circ\mu^{\mathrm{q}}_{X}\circ\mu^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}

\displaystyle=\mu^{\mathcal{Q}}_{X}\circ{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\pi_{1}}\circ\pi_{1}\circ\mu^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}

by (

)

\displaystyle=\mu^{\mathcal{Q}}_{X}\circ{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\pi_{1}}\circ\mu^{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}\circ{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\pi_{1}}\circ\pi_{1}

by (

)

\displaystyle=\mu^{\mathcal{Q}}_{X}\circ\mu^{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}\circ{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\pi_{1}}}\circ{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\pi_{1}}\circ\pi_{1}

nat. of
\mu^{\mathcal{Q}}

while:

\displaystyle\pi_{1}\circ\mu^{\mathrm{q}}_{X}\circ\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\mu^{\mathrm{q}}_{X}}

\displaystyle=\mu^{\mathcal{Q}}_{X}\circ{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\pi_{1}}\circ\pi_{1}\circ\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\mu^{\mathrm{q}}_{X}}

by (
