)

\displaystyle=\mu^{\mathcal{Q}}_{X}\circ{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\pi_{1}}\circ{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\mu^{\mathrm{q}}_{X}}\circ\pi_{1}

def. of
\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}
on morphisms

\displaystyle=\mu^{\mathcal{Q}}_{X}\circ{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\mu^{\mathcal{Q}}_{X}}\circ{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\pi_{1}}}\circ{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\pi_{1}}\circ\pi_{1}\mskip-150.0mu

\displaystyle\text{by (\ref{eq:muq})},

and we conclude since

\mu^{\mathcal{Q}}_{X}\circ\mu^{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}=\mu^{\mathcal{Q}}_{X}\circ{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\mu^{\mathcal{Q}}_{X}}
, by the final monad law on
{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}
. ∎

Remark 8.6

There is also a
\mathcal{P}\ell_{\mathcal{V}}
monad (of lenses) on
\mathbf{Top}
, but we
will not care about it except on full subcategories of weakly
Hausdorff of quasi-Polish spaces, where we can transport the monad
structure of
\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}
over to
\mathcal{P}\ell_{\mathcal{V}}
. We obtain that for every
continuous map
f\colon X\to Y
,

\mathcal{P}\ell_{\mathcal{V}}f=\varrho_{Y}\circ\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}f\circ\iota_{X}
maps every lens

L
on
X
to
\upc f[\upc L]\cap cl(f[cl(L)])
(which we may
simplify as
\upc f[L]\cap cl(f[L])
), the unit

\eta^{\mathcal{P}\ell}_{X}
maps every
x\in X
to

\varrho_{X}(\upc x,\dc x)=\upc x\cap\dc x
(which simplifies to

\{x\}
if
X
is
T_{0}
), and the multiplication

\mu^{\mathcal{P}\ell}_{X}=\varrho_{X}\circ\mu^{\mathrm{q}}_{X}\circ\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\iota_{X}}\circ\iota_{\mathcal{P}\ell X}
maps every lens

\mathcal{L}\in\mathcal{P}\ell_{\mathcal{V}}{\mathcal{P}\ell_{\mathcal{V}}X}
to

\upc\bigcup\mathcal{L}\cap cl(\bigcup\mathcal{L})
.

In order to obtain a distributive law over
{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}
, we need to
further restrict the category of topological spaces we can work on.
Not only do we need our spaces
X
to be such that
\mathcal{L}X
is
locally convex, but addition on
\mathcal{L}X
needs to be
almost
open
, meaning that given any two open subsets
\mathcal{U}
and

\mathcal{V}
of
\mathcal{L}X
,
\upc(\mathcal{U}+\mathcal{V})
is open.
(We will also need
X
to be compact when
\bullet
is “
1
”.) This
is the case if
X
is stably locally compact
[
17
, Lemma
3.24]
. (We have already stated that every stably locally
compact space
X
is weakly Hausdorff
[
33
, Lemma 8.1]
,
so that
\mathcal{P}\ell_{\mathcal{V}}X\cong\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X
in this case.)

We also need our category of spaces to be closed under
{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}
,

\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}
, and under retracts. The category of stably compact spaces is
closed under retracts
[
37
, Proposition, bottom of p.153, and
subsequent discussion]
, see also

[
31
, Proposition 2.17]
.

As far as
{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X
is concerned, it was shown in

[
3
, Theorem 39]
that
{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1}X
is stably
compact for every stably compact space
X
. The same proof shows that

{\mathbf{V}}X
is stably compact under the same assumption. Additionally,

{\mathbf{V}}_{1}X
occurs as the subspace
\langle X\geq 1\rangle
, where

\langle Q\geq r\rangle
denotes

\{\nu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\leq 1}X\mid\forall U\in{\mathcal{O}}X,Q\subseteq U\mathrel{\Rightarrow}\nu(U)\geq 1\}
, and is compact saturated for every compact
saturated subset
Q
of the stably compact space
X
and for every

r\in\mathbb{R}_{+}

[
15
, Lemma 6.6]
. But any compact saturated
subset of a stably compact space is stably compact; more generally,
any patch-closed subset of a stably compact space, namely, any subset
that we can obtain as an intersection of finite unions of closed
subsets and of compact saturated subsets, is stably compact

[
31
, Proposition 2.16]
.

As for
\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X
, it was noted in
[
15
, Fact 5.2]
that for
every sober space
X
,
\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X
is homeomorphic to Heckmann’s space of

\mathbf{A}
-valuations on
X

[
28
]
. If
X
is stably
compact, then, both spaces are stably compact

[
15
, Proposition 5.13]
.

Definition 8.7

Let
\mathbf{Top}^{\natural}
be any full subcategory of
\mathbf{Top}

consisting of spaces
X
such that
\mathcal{L}X
is locally convex and
has an almost open addition map (and such that
X
is compact if

\bullet
is “
1
”), and which is closed under
\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}
,

{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}
and under retracts—for example the category of
stably compact spaces.

We note that every object of
\mathbf{Top}^{\natural}
is an

{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}_{\bullet}
-friendly space. In particular, if
\bullet
is “
1
”,
every compact space
X
such that
\mathcal{L}X
is locally convex is

{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}_{1}
-friendly, by definition.

9
The monad of forks

A
fork
on a space
X
is any pair

(F^{-},F^{+})
of a superlinear prevision
F^{-}
on
X
and of a
sublinear prevision
F^{+}
on
X
satisfying
Walley’s condition
:

F^{-}(h+h^{\prime})\leq F^{-}(h)+F^{+}(h^{\prime})\leq F^{+}(h+h^{\prime})

for all
h,h^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}X

[
14
,
34
]
. A
fork is
subnormalized
, resp.
normalized
if and only if
both
F^{-}
and
F^{+}
are.

We write
\mathbb{P}_{{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}}X
for the set of all forks on
X
, and

\mathbb{P}^{\leq 1}_{{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}}X
,
\mathbb{P}^{1}_{{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}}X
for their subsets of
subnormalized, resp. normalized, forks. The
weak topology
on
each is the subspace topology induced by the inclusion into the larger
space
\mathbb{P}_{{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}}X\times\mathbb{P}_{{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}}X
. A subbase of the weak
topology is composed of two kinds of open subsets:
[h>r]^{-}
,
defined as
\{(F^{-},F^{+})\mid F^{-}(h)>r\}
, and
[h>r]^{+}
, defined
as
\{(F^{-},F^{+})\mid F^{+}(h)>r\}
, where
h\in\mathcal{L}X
,

r\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
. The specialization ordering of spaces of forks is
the product ordering
\leq\times\leq
, where
\leq
denotes the
pointwise ordering on previsions. In particular, all those spaces of
forks are
T_{0}
.

Whether
\bullet
is nothing, “
\leq 1
”, or
“
1
”,
\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}
defines an endofunctor on
\mathbf{Top}
,
whose action on morphisms is given by

\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}f\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}(\mathbb{P}f,\mathbb{P}f)
.

Proposition 9.1

Let
\bullet
be nothing, “
\leq 1
”, or “
1
”. For every
topological space
X
such that
\mathcal{L}X
is locally convex and has
an almost open addition map (and such that
X
is compact, in case

\bullet
is “
1
”), there are continuous maps

r_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}\colon\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}\to\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X
and

s_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}\colon\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X\to\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}
defined by:

\displaystyle r_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}(Q,C)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}(r_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}(Q),r_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}(C))

\displaystyle s_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}(F^{-},F^{+})

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}(s_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}(F^{-}),s_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}(F^{+})).

Additionally,
r_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}
and
s_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}
are natural in
X
.

Proof 24

For every quasi-lens
(Q,C)
on
{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X
, we check that

(F^{-},F^{+})\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}(r_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}(Q),r_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}(C))
is a fork. For all

h,h^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}X
,

\displaystyle F^{-}(h+h^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\min_{\nu\in Q}\int_{x\in X}(h(x)+h^{\prime}(x))\,d\nu

\displaystyle\leq\min_{\nu\in Q\cap C}\int_{x\in X}(h(x)+h^{\prime}(x))\,d\nu

\displaystyle\leq\min_{\nu\in Q\cap C}\left(\int_{x\in X}h(x)\,d\nu+F^{+}(h^{\prime})\right)

since
\int_{x\in X}h^{\prime}(x)\,d\nu\leq F^{+}(h^{\prime})
for every
\nu\in Q\cap C

\displaystyle=\min_{\nu\in Q\cap C}\int_{x\in X}h(x)\,d\nu+F^{+}(h^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\min_{\nu\in\upc(Q\cap C)}\int_{x\in X}h(x)\,d\nu+F^{+}(h^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq\min_{\nu\in Q}\int_{x\in X}h(x)\,d\nu+F^{+}(h^{\prime})\qquad\text{since $Q\subseteq\upc(Q\cap C)$}

\displaystyle=F^{-}(h)+F^{+}(h^{\prime}).

For Walley’s other inequality, we observe that:

\displaystyle F^{-}(h)+F^{+}(h^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\sup\nolimits^{\scriptstyle\uparrow}_{r\in\mathbb{R}_{+},r<F^{-}(h)}(r+F^{+}(h^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=\sup_{r\in\mathbb{R}_{+},r<F^{-}(h),\nu\in C}\left(r+\int_{x\in X}h^{\prime}(x)\,d\nu\right).

Then, for every
r\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that
r<F^{-}(h)
, we have

r<\min_{\nu\in Q}\int_{x\in X}h(x)\,d\nu
, so

Q\subseteq[h>r]
, by definition of
F^{-}=r_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}(Q)
. Since

(Q,C)
is a quasi-lens,
C\subseteq cl([h>r]\cap C)
, so:

\displaystyle F^{-}(h)+F^{+}(h^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{r\in\mathbb{R}_{+},r<F^{-}(h),\nu\in cl([h>r]\cap C)}\left(r+\int_{x\in X}h^{\prime}(x)\,d\nu\right)

\displaystyle=\sup_{r\in\mathbb{R}_{+},r<F^{-}(h),\nu\in[h>r]\cap C}\left(r+\int_{x\in X}h^{\prime}(x)\,d\nu\right)

by Fact
7.5
.

Fact
7.5
applies, since

\nu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X\mapsto\int_{x\in X}h^{\prime}(x)\,d\nu
is
lower semicontinuous, as the inverse image of any basic open set

]t,\infty]
is
[h^{\prime}>t]
; and therefore

\nu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X\mapsto r+\int_{x\in X}h^{\prime}(x)\,d\nu
is
lower semicontinuous, too. Now for every
r\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that

r<F^{-}(h)
, for every
\nu\in[h>r]\cap C
, we have

\int_{x\in X}h(x)\,d\nu>r
by definition of
[h>r]
, so:

\displaystyle F^{-}(h)+F^{+}(h^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{r\in\mathbb{R}_{+},r<F^{-}(h),\nu\in[h>r]\cap C}\left(\int_{x\in X}h(x)\,d\nu+\int_{x\in X}h^{\prime}(x)\,d\nu\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{\nu\in C}\left(\int_{x\in X}h(x)\,d\nu+\int_{x\in X}h^{\prime}(x)\,d\nu\right)

\displaystyle=F^{+}(h+h^{\prime}).

Additionally, if
\bullet
is “
\leq 1
” (resp., “
1
”), then

F^{-}=r_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}(Q)
and
F^{+}=r_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}(C)
are subnormalized (resp.,
normalized), so
r_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}
takes its values in
\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X
.
The fact that
r_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}
is continuous follows from the fact that

r_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}
and
r_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}
are, and similarly for naturality.

Turning to
s_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}
, we must show that for every

(F^{-},F^{+})\in\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X
,

(Q,C)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}(s_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}(F^{-}),s_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}(F^{+}))
is a
quasi-lens. That
s_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}
is continuous and natural will
then follow from the same properties for
s_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}
and

s_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}
.

Lemma 3.27 of
[
17
]
states that there is a map (called
s_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}
there; although
this is not the same map as our
s_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}
, the two are
definitely related) from
\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X
to the space of
lenses on
\mathbb{P}_{\mathtt{P}}^{\bullet}X
, and which sends
(F^{-},F^{+})
to

s_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}(F^{-})\cap s_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}(F^{+})
. In particular,
Q\cap C=s_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}(F^{-})\cap s_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}(F^{+})
is
a lens, and is therefore non-empty.

Up to the isomorphism

\mathbb{P}_{\mathtt{P}}^{\bullet}X\cong{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X
, Lemma 3.28 of

[
17
]
states that for every

\nu^{\prime}\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X
, if
\nu^{\prime}\in s_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}(F^{-})
, then
there is a
\nu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X
such that
\nu\leq\nu^{\prime}
and
for every
h\in\mathcal{L}X
,

F^{-}(h)\leq\int_{x\in X}h(x)\,d\nu\leq F^{+}(h)
. In other
words,
Q\subseteq\upc(Q\cap C)
.

Similarly, Lemma 3.29 of
[
17
]
states that for every

\nu^{\prime}\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X
such that
\nu^{\prime}\in s_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}(F^{+})
,
there is a
\nu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X
such that
\nu^{\prime}\leq\nu
and
for every
h\in\mathcal{L}X
,

F^{-}(h)\leq\int_{x\in X}h(x)\,d\nu\leq F^{+}(h)
. This
requires
\mathcal{L}X
to be locally convex and to have an almost open
addition map, and also
X
to be compact if
\bullet
is “
1
”.
In other words,
C\subseteq\dc(Q\cap C)
. Since the
downwards-closure of a set is always included in its closure,

C\subseteq cl(Q\cap C)
. In particular, for every open
neighborhood
U
of
Q
,
C\subseteq cl(U\cap C)
. ∎

Proposition 9.2

Let
\bullet
be nothing, “
\leq 1
”, or “
1
”. For every
topological space
X
such that
\mathcal{L}X
is locally convex and has
an almost open addition map (and such that
X
is compact, in case

\bullet
is “
1
”),
r_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}
and
s_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}
restrict to a
homeomorphism between the subspace
\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q},cvx}_{\mathsf{V}}{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}
of

\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}
consisting of quasi-lenses
(Q,C)
such
that both
Q
and
C
are convex, and
\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X
.

Proof 25

Immediate consequence of Proposition
9.1
, of the fact
that
r_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}
and
s_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}
form a homeomorphism between

{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{cvx}{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}
and
\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X
, and of the
fact that
r_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}
and
s_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}
form a homeomorphism between

{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{cvx}{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}
and
\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X
. Note that
this holds because
X
is
{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}_{\bullet}
-friendly; if
\bullet
is
“
1
”, notably, then not only is
\mathcal{L}X
locally convex, but we
have also assumed that
X
is compact.
∎

Much like there are projection maps
\pi_{1}\colon\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X\to{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X

and
\pi_{2}\colon\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X\to{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}X
, there are projection maps, which
we write with the same notation,

\pi_{1}\colon\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X\to\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X
and

\pi_{2}\colon\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X\to\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X
. The
pairing
\langle\pi_{1},\pi_{2}\rangle
is then the inclusion map

\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X\to\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X\times\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X
. It is easy to see that this is a topological embedding.

It was shown in
[
14
, Proposition 3]
that there is a monad
of forks on the category of dcpos (resp., pointed dcpos) and
Scott-continuous maps, except that spaces of previsions were given the
Scott topology of the pointwise ordering. As for previsions, we check
that there is a corresponding monad of (subnormalized, normalized)
forks on
\mathbf{Top}
.

10
The weak distributive law

Proposition 10.1

Let
X
be a topological space. There is a unique continuous map

\Theta\colon{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}\to\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X
such
that:

\textstyle{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Phi}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{\pi_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\Theta}
\scriptstyle{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{\pi_{2}}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Psi}
\textstyle{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}
\textstyle{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{2}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X.}

Proof 26

In other words, for every
\xi\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}
,

\Theta(\xi)
must be equal to

(\Phi(\pi_{1}[\xi]),\Psi(\pi_{2}[\xi]))
. It remains to show that
the latter is a fork; continuity will follow from the continuity of

\Phi
and of
\Psi
(for the latter, see Lemma
7.1
).
Let
(F^{-},F^{+})\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}(\Phi(\pi_{1}[\xi]),\Psi(\pi_{2}[\xi]))
.
We know that
F^{-}\in\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X
, that

F^{+}\in\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}
, and it remains to show Walley’s
conditions.

For every
h\in\mathcal{L}X
, we have:

\displaystyle F^{-}(h)

\displaystyle=\int_{Q\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}\min_{x\in Q}h(x)\,d\pi_{1}[\xi]

\displaystyle=\int_{(Q,C)\in\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}\min_{x\in Q}h(x)\,d\xi

(15)

\displaystyle F^{+}(h)

\displaystyle=\int_{C\in{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}\sup_{x\in C}h(x)\,d\pi_{2}[\xi]

\displaystyle=\int_{(Q,C)\in\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}\sup_{x\in C}h(x)\,d\xi,

(16)

using the change of variable formula.

For every
h\in\mathcal{L}X
, we have:

\displaystyle\min_{x\in Q}h(x)

\displaystyle\geq\min_{x\in\upc(Q\cap C)}h(x)

since
Q\subseteq\upc(Q\cap C)

\displaystyle=\min_{x\in Q\cap C}h(x)\geq\min_{x\in Q}h(x),

since
Q\cap C\subseteq C
. This is a circular list of
inequalities, hence all its terms are equal.

For every
h,h^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}X
, for all
(Q,C)\in\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X
, we then
have:

\displaystyle\min_{x\in Q}(h(x)+h^{\prime}(x))

\displaystyle=\min_{x\in Q\cap C}(h(x)+h^{\prime}(x))

\displaystyle\leq\min_{x\in Q\cap C}(h(x)+\sup_{x\in C}h^{\prime}(x))

\displaystyle=\min_{x\in Q\cap C}h(x)+\sup_{x\in C}h^{\prime}(x)

\displaystyle=\min_{x\in Q}h(x)+\sup_{x\in C}h^{\prime}(x).

(17)

Using (
15
) and (
16
), it follows that
F^{-}(h+h^{\prime})\leq F^{-}(h)+F^{+}(h^{\prime})
.

We claim that:

\displaystyle\min_{x\in Q}h(x)+\sup_{x\in C}h^{\prime}(x)

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{x\in C}(h(x)+h^{\prime}(x)).

(18)

It suffices to show that for every
t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that

t<\min_{x\in Q}h(x)
,

t+\sup_{x\in C}h^{\prime}(x)\leq\sup_{x\in C}(h(x)+h^{\prime}(x))
.
Since
t<\min_{x\in Q}h(x)
,

Q\subseteq h^{-1}(]t,\infty])
. Since
(Q,C)
is a quasi-lens,

C\subseteq cl(h^{-1}(]t,\infty])\cap C)
. Hence

\sup_{x\in C}h^{\prime}(x)\leq\sup_{x\in cl(h^{-1}(]t,\infty])\cap C)}h^{\prime}(x)=\sup_{x\in h^{-1}(]t,\infty])\cap C}h^{\prime}(x)

(using Fact
7.5
), and since
h(x)\geq t
for every

x\in h^{-1}(]t,\infty])\cap C
, we obtain that

t+\sup_{x\in C}h^{\prime}(x)\leq\sup_{x\in h^{-1}(]t,\infty])\cap C}(h(x)+h^{\prime}(x))\leq\sup_{x\in C}(h(x)+h^{\prime}(x))
.

Using (
15
) and (
16
), it follows from
(
18
) that
F^{-}(h)+F^{+}(h^{\prime})\leq F^{+}(h+h^{\prime})
. ∎

Remark 10.3

Using Remark
4.3
and Remark
7.4
, in the special
case where
\mu\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\delta_{(Q_{i},C_{i})}
, where

n\geq 1
, and where
Q_{i}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\upc E_{i}
,
C_{i}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\dc E_{i}
,
where each set
E_{i}
is finite and non-empty, then

\lambda^{\natural}_{X}(\mu)=(\upc conv(\mathcal{E}),cl(conv(\mathcal{E})))
, where

\mathcal{E}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\{\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\delta_{x_{i}}\mid x_{1}\in E_{1},\cdots,x_{n}\in E_{n}\}
.

Remark 10.4

We recall that if
X
is weakly Hausdorff or quasi-Polish, then

\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X\cong\mathcal{P}\ell_{\mathcal{V}}X
, and that those isomorphisms allow us to
transport the
\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}
monad structure over to any full subcategory of

\mathbf{Top}
consisting of weakly Hausdorff or quasi-Polish spaces.
Theorem
10.2
then allows us to obtain a weak
distributive law of
\mathcal{P}\ell_{\mathcal{V}}
over
{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}
on any full
subcategory of
\mathbf{Top}^{\natural}
consisting of weakly Hausdorff or
quasi-Polish spaces—in particular on the category of stably
compact spaces, which are all weakly Hausdorff.

Remark 10.5

Combining Remark
10.3
and Remark
10.4
,
in the special case where

\mu\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\delta_{L_{i}}
, where
n\geq 1
, and
where
L_{i}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\upc E_{i}\cap\dc E_{i}
, where each set
E_{i}
is
finite and non-empty, then

\lambda^{\natural}_{X}(\mu)=\upc conv(\mathcal{E})\cap cl(conv(\mathcal{E}))
, where

\mathcal{E}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\{\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\delta_{x_{i}}\mid x_{1}\in E_{1},\cdots,x_{n}\in E_{n}\}
.

11
The associated weak composite monad

We proceed as with
\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}
and
\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}
, and in
fact we reduce to the cases of those monads. We start with the
idempotent:

\textstyle{\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\lambda^{\natural}_{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\mu_{X}}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}}

(20)

whose splitting will define the action of the functor part of the
associated weak composite monad on
X
.

Lemma 11.1

For every object
X
of
\mathbf{Top}^{\natural}
, a splitting of
(
20
) is

\textstyle{\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{r_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}}

\scriptstyle{s_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}}
.

Proof 28

Composing (
20
) with
\pi_{1}
, resp. with
\pi_{2}
on
the right (of the diagram—namely computing
\pi_{1}\circ f
resp.
\pi_{2}\circ f
where
f
is (
20
)), and simplifying
using the naturality of
\pi_{1}
(resp.,
\pi_{2}
),
(
19
) (see Theorem
10.2
) and
(
12
) (see Proposition
9.3
), we obtain
(
2
), resp. (
6
)—the corresponding
idempotents in the
{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}
and
{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}
cases—composed with
\pi_{1}
,
resp.
\pi_{2}
.

By Lemma
5.1
(resp.,
Lemma
8.1
), this is equal to

s_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}\circ r_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}\circ\pi_{1}
(resp.,

s_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}\circ r_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}\circ\pi_{2}
), and that, in turn, is
equal to
\pi_{1}\circ s_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}\circ r_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}
(resp.,

\pi_{2}\circ s_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}\circ r_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}
), by
Proposition
9.1
. We conclude since

\langle\pi_{1},\pi_{2}\rangle
is an inclusion map, hence is
monotonic. ∎

Generalizing (
20
), there is an idempotent:

\textstyle{\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}}

\scriptstyle{\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}\alpha}
\textstyle{\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}

(21)

for every
{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}
-algebra
\alpha
. As before, we will only
need to compute this splitting for one additional

{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}
-algebra. We write

for the subspace of

consisting of

such that

and

are convex.

Lemma 11.2

For every topological space

, there is a

for every

.

is an object of

, then a splitting of the

) where

), and

Proof 29

For every
\xi\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}
, let

(F_{0}^{-},F_{0}^{+})\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\alpha_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}(\xi)
. For every

h\in\mathcal{L}X
,

F_{0}^{-}(h)=\alpha_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}(\pi_{1}[\xi])(h)=\int_{F\in\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}F(h)\,d\pi_{1}[\xi]=\int_{(F^{-},F^{+})\in\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}F^{-}(h)\,d\xi
, by the change of variable
formula, and similarly

F_{0}^{+}(h)=\int_{(F^{-},F^{+})\in\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}F^{+}(h)\,d\xi
. Hence, for all
h,h^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}X
,

\displaystyle F_{0}^{-}(h+h^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\int_{(F^{-},F^{+})\in\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}F^{-}(h+h^{\prime})\,d\xi

\displaystyle\leq\int_{(F^{-},F^{+})\in\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}(F^{-}(h)+F^{+}(h^{\prime}))\,d\xi

\displaystyle=\int_{(F^{-},F^{+})\in\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}F^{-}(h)\,d\xi+\int_{(F^{-},F^{+})\in\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}F^{+}(h^{\prime})\,d\xi

\displaystyle=F_{0}^{-}(h)+F_{0}^{+}(h^{\prime}),

and:

\displaystyle F_{0}^{-}(h)+F_{0}^{+}(h^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\int_{(F^{-},F^{+})\in\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}(F^{-}(h)+F^{+}(h^{\prime}))\,d\xi

\displaystyle\leq\int_{(F^{-},F^{+})\in\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}F^{+}(h+h^{\prime})\,d\xi

\displaystyle=F_{0}^{+}(h+h^{\prime}),

so
(F_{0}^{-},F_{0}^{+})\in\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X
. The map

\alpha_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}
is continuous, since the inverse image of the
subbasic open set
[h>r]^{-}
is

[((F^{+},F^{-})\mapsto F^{-}(h))>r]
, and the inverse image of the
subbasic open set
[h>r]^{+}
is

[((F^{+},F^{-})\mapsto F^{+}(h))>r]
.

The map
\alpha_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}
is defined so that

\pi_{1}\circ\alpha_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}=\alpha_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}\circ{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{\pi_{1}}
and

\pi_{2}\circ\alpha_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}=\alpha_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}\circ{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{\pi_{2}}
. The fact that
\alpha_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}
is a

{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}
-algebra then follows from the fact that

\alpha_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}
and
\alpha_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}
are.

) with

\alpha\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\alpha_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}

\scriptstyle{\eta_{\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}X}}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{1}}
nat.

\scriptstyle{{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{\pi_{2}}}}
def.
\alpha_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\pi_{2}}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\lambda^{\flat}_{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\alpha_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}}

The bottom row is the idempotent (
7
) of
Lemma
8.2
(with

\alpha\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\alpha_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}
), which splits as

\textstyle{{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{r^{\flat}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{cvx}{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{s^{\flat}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}}
, where
s^{\flat}
is the inclusion map and
r^{\flat}
maps
every
C\in{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{cvx}{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}
to itself.

Let
r^{\natural}
map every

(\mathcal{Q},\mathcal{C})\in\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}
to

(r^{\sharp}({\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\pi_{1}}(\mathcal{Q})),r^{\flat}({\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\pi_{2}}(\mathcal{C})))
. This is a pair in

{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{cvx}{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}\times{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{cvx}{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}
. We need to show that this is also a
quasi-lens, and that
r^{\natural}
is continuous. We observe that

\pi_{1}\circ r^{\natural}=r^{\sharp}\circ{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\pi_{1}}\circ\pi_{1}
is
the corestriction of the idempotent (
3
) to

{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{cvx}{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}
, and

\pi_{2}\circ r^{\natural}=r^{\flat}\circ{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\pi_{1}}\circ\pi_{1}
is
the corestriction of the idempotent (
7
) to

{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{cvx}{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}
, so
r^{\sharp}
is the
corestriction of the idempotent (
21
) to

{\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}}^{cvx}{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}
. In particular,
r^{\natural}

takes its values in (convex) quasi-lenses and is continuous.

Since
r^{\sharp}
maps convex elements to themselves, and similarly
for
r^{\flat}
,
r^{\natural}
maps convex quasi-lenses to themselves.

We also define

s^{\natural}\colon{\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}}^{cvx}{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}\to\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}
as the inclusion map. Since
r^{\natural}

maps convex quasi-lenses to themselves,

r^{\natural}\circ s^{\natural}=\mathrm{id}_{{\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}}^{cvx}{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}}
. Also, by definition of
r^{\natural}
as
the corestriction of the idempotent (
21
) to

{\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}}^{cvx}{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}
, (
21
) is
equal to
s^{\natural}\circ r^{\natural}
. ∎

Lemma 11.3

For
S\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}
,
T\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}
,

\lambda\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\lambda^{\natural}
, the monad
\overline{S}
on the
category of
{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}
-algebras over
\mathbf{Top}^{\natural}
has the
following properties:

1.

For every free
{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}
-algebra

\mu_{X}\colon{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}\to{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X
,

\overline{S}\mu_{X}\colon{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}\to\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X
is
\alpha_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}
(see
Lemma
11.2
).

2.

The unit
\eta^{\overline{S}}_{\mu_{X}}
evaluated at the free

{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}
-algebra

\mu_{X}\colon{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}\to{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X
is

r_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}\circ\eta^{\mathrm{q}}_{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}
.

3.

The multiplication
\mu^{\overline{S}}_{\mu_{X}}
evaluated at
the free
{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}
-algebra

\mu_{X}\colon{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}\to{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X
is
the function that maps every

(Q,C)\in{\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}}^{cvx}{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}
to

(h\in\mathcal{L}X\mapsto\min_{(F^{-},F^{+})\in Q}F^{-}(h),h\in\mathcal{L}X\mapsto\sup_{(F^{+},F^{-})\in C}F^{+}(h))\in\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X
.

4.

The counit
\epsilon^{T}
of the adjunction
F^{T}\dashv U^{T}

between
\mathbf{Top}
and the category of
T
-algebras
(
{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}
-algebras) is given at each algebra
\beta\colon{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X\to X
as
\beta
itself.

Proof 30

1. We know that
\overline{S}\mu_{X}
must be a

{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}
-algebra on
\overline{S}{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}
, and the
latter can be taken as
\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X
, by
Lemma
11.1
. We know that

\overline{S}\mu_{X}=\pi_{\mu_{X}}\circ S\mu_{X}\circ\lambda_{X}\circ T\iota_{\mu_{X}}=r_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}\circ\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}\mu_{X}\circ{\lambda^{\natural}_{X}}\circ{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{s_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}}
. Hence

\pi_{1}\circ\overline{S}\mu_{X}=r_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}\circ{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}\mu_{X}\circ\lambda^{\sharp}_{X}\circ{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{s_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}}\circ{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{\pi_{1}}
using the definition of
r_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}

(Proposition
9.1
), the naturality of
\pi_{1}
, the
definition of
\lambda^{\natural}
(Theorem
10.2
), and
the definition of
s_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}
(Proposition
9.1
).
Hence, by Lemma
5.3
, item 1,

\pi_{1}\circ\overline{S}\mu_{X}=\alpha_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}\circ{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{\pi_{1}}
. Similarly,

\pi_{2}\circ\overline{S}\mu_{X}=r_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}\circ{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}\mu_{X}\circ\lambda^{\flat}_{X}\circ{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{s_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}}\circ{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{\pi_{2}}
, so

\pi_{2}\circ\overline{S}\mu_{X}=\alpha_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}\circ{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{\pi_{2}}
, using Lemma
8.3
. We also
have

\pi_{1}\circ\alpha_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}=\alpha_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}\circ{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{\pi_{1}}
and

\pi_{2}\circ\alpha_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}=\alpha_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}\circ{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{\pi_{2}}
, by definition of
\alpha_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}

(Lemma
11.2
). Since

\langle\pi_{1},\pi_{2}\rangle
is injective,

\overline{S}\mu_{X}=\alpha_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}
.

2.

\eta^{\overline{S}}_{\mu_{X}}=\pi_{\mu_{X}}\circ\eta^{S}_{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}=r_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}\circ\eta^{\mathrm{q}}_{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}
.

3. In general,
\mu^{\overline{S}}_{\alpha}
is a morphism from

\overline{S}{\overline{S}\alpha}
to
\overline{S}\alpha
in the
category of
{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}
-algebras. When

\alpha=\mu_{X}\colon{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}\to{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X
, it is therefore one from

\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}
to

\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X
. We have

\mu^{\overline{S}}_{\mu_{X}}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\pi_{\mu_{X}}\circ\mu^{S}_{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}\circ S\iota_{\mu_{X}}\circ\iota_{\overline{S}\mu_{X}}
. We may take

\iota_{\overline{S}\mu_{X}}=\iota_{\alpha_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}}
to be the
right arrow, from
{\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}}^{cvx}{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}
to

\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}
in
Lemma
11.2
—just subspace inclusion. Let

m^{\natural}
be that subspace inclusion. Writing
m^{\sharp}
for the
subspace inclusion of
{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{cvx}{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}
into

{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}
and
m^{\flat}
for the subspace inclusion
of
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}^{cvx}{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}
into

, we have

and

{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\pi_{2}}\circ\pi_{2}\circ m^{\natural}=m^{\flat}\circ{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\pi_{2}}\circ\pi_{2}
.

Then

S\iota_{\mu_{X}}=\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{s_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}}\colon\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}\to\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}}
, by

. We compose that with

, then with

. The
resulting composition is

f\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}r_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}\circ\mu^{\mathrm{q}}_{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}\circ\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{s_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}}\circ m^{\natural}
.

Then

\pi_{1}\circ f=r_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}\circ\mu^{\mathcal{Q}}_{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}\circ{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\pi_{1}}\circ\pi_{1}\circ\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{s_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}}\circ m^{\natural}
by

(Proposition

) and by

11
) (see Proposition

). Now

(by naturality of

)

\pi_{1}\circ f=r_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}\circ\mu^{\mathcal{Q}}_{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X}\circ{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{s_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}}\circ m^{\sharp}\circ{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\pi_{1}}\circ\pi_{1}
. The

was elucidated in Lemma

, item 3, as
mapping every

to

. Hence

maps every convex quasi-lens

(Q,C)\in{\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}}^{cvx}{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}

.

Similarly, and using Lemma
8.3
, item 3,

\pi_{2}\circ f
maps every convex quasi-lens

(Q,C)\in{\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}}^{cvx}{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}
to

(h\in\mathcal{L}X\mapsto\sup_{(F^{-},F^{+})\in Q}F^{+}(h))
.

4. Standard category theory
[

, Chapter VI, Section 2,
Theorem 1]
. ∎

Theorem 11.4

The weak composite monad associated with

\lambda^{\natural}\colon{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}}\to\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}}
on
\mathbf{Top}^{\natural}
is

(\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet},\eta^{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}},\mu^{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}})
.

Proof 31

We take
T\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}
,
S\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}
,

\lambda\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\lambda^{\natural}
. The functor
U^{T}
takes every

{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}
-algebra
\alpha\colon{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X\to X
to
X

and every
{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}
-algebra morphism

to the underlying continuous map

. The functor

takes every space
X
to

{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}
-algebras.

Then

to the splitting

. We also obtain

-algebra)

, namely

\pi_{2}
.

By definition of

(Proposition

), naturality

and definition of

namely to

.
Similarly with
\pi_{2}
.

The unit of the weak composite monad is

. At object
X
, this maps
every
x\in X
to

, item 2. In order to show that this is

\pi_{2}
. This follows from the

The multiplication is

,

.

\textstyle{TT\overline{S}TX\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

(22)

), and

\pi_{\beta}\colon\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}\to{\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}}^{cvx}{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}

) (call it
f
), which maps every convex
element of
\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}
to itself.

Hence the multiplication of the weak composite monad, evaluated at

X
, is the composition of (
22
) with

\mu^{\overline{S}}_{\mu_{X}}
(given in Lemma
11.3
,
item 3), namely:

\textstyle{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{s_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\alpha_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}}^{cvx}{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu^{\overline{S}}_{\mu_{X}}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}

Let us call
g
for that composition. Using the explicit expression
for
\mu^{\overline{S}}_{\mu_{X}}
, and the appropriate naturality
equations, we recognize
\pi_{1}\circ g
as being equal to the
composition
(\ref{eq:Sbarmu})\circ{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\pi_{1}}\circ\pi_{1}
;
(
5
) is the expression of the weak composite monad we
obtained from
\lambda^{\sharp}
in the proof of
Theorem
5.4
, which we elucidated as mapping every

\mathcal{F}\in\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}
to

(h\in\mathcal{L}X\mapsto\mathcal{F}(F\in\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X\mapsto F(h))
.

Similarly,
\pi_{2}\circ g
is equal to

(\ref{eq:Sbarmu:flat})\circ{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\pi_{2}}\circ\pi_{2}
, where
(
9
) was elucidated in the proof of
Theorem
8.4
as mapping every

\mathcal{F}\in\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}
to

(h\in\mathcal{L}X\mapsto\mathcal{F}(F\in\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X\mapsto F(h))
. Hence
g
maps every

(\mathcal{F}^{-},\mathcal{F}^{+})\in\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}{\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X}
to the pair of maps

(h\in\mathcal{L}X\mapsto{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\pi_{1}}(\mathcal{F}^{-})(F\in\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X\mapsto F(h))
and

(h\in\mathcal{L}X\mapsto{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{V}}{\pi_{2}}(\mathcal{F}^{+})(F\in\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet}X\mapsto F(h))
, namely to

\mu^{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}_{X}(\mathcal{F}^{+},\mathcal{F}^{-})
(see
Proposition
9.3
). ∎

Remark 11.5

The isomorphic weak distributive law of Remark
10.4

on the category of stably compact spaces must induce the same weak
composite monad, namely

(\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet},\eta^{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}},\mu^{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}})
—or rather, its
restriction to the category of stably compact spaces.

Remark 11.6

A space is
T_{1}
if and only if its specialization preordering is
the equality relation. Every Hausdorff space is
T_{1}
. On a
T_{1}

space
X
, a lens is the same thing as a non-empty compact subset.
Hence
\mathcal{P}\ell_{\mathcal{V}}X
is the usual hyperspace of non-empty compact
subsets, with the (usual) Vietoris topology, and the
\mathcal{P}\ell_{\mathcal{V}}

monad specializes to what has sometimes been called the Vietoris
monad on the category
\mathbf{KHaus}
of compact Hausdorff spaces
(although Vietoris had no knowledge of monads, and while he defined
the Vietoris topology on the space of closed subsets of a space

[
46
]
, spaces of subsets were further explored by
Hausdorff
[
26
, §28]
then by Michael

[
39
]
, among others). In other words, relying on
Remark
8.6
, removing the now useless signs
\upc
and
simplifying,
\mathcal{P}\ell_{\mathcal{V}}f\colon\mathcal{P}\ell_{\mathcal{V}}X\to\mathcal{P}\ell_{\mathcal{V}}Y
maps
every non-empty compact subset
L
to its image
f[L]
under
f
,
the unit
\eta^{\mathcal{P}\ell}_{X}
maps every
x\in X
to
\{x\}
, and
the multiplication
\mu^{\mathcal{P}\ell}_{X}
maps every

\mathcal{L}\in\mathcal{P}\ell_{\mathcal{V}}{\mathcal{P}\ell_{\mathcal{V}}X}
to
\bigcup\mathcal{L}
.

Specializing Remark
11.5
to the appropriate
subcategory, we obtain a weak distributive law of the Vietoris monad
over
{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}
on
\mathbf{KHaus}
, and its associated weak
composite monad is the appropriate restriction of

(\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet},\eta^{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}},\mu^{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}})
, by
Theorem
11.4
.

Remark 11.7

We refine Remark
10.5
in the case of regular
spaces. A space is
regular
if and only if for every point

x
, every open neighborhood
U
of
x
contains a closed
neighborhood
C
of
x
; equivalently, if every open set is the
union of the directed family of interiors
\text{int}({C})
of closed
subsets of
U
. For every regular space
X
,
{\mathbf{V}}_{1}X
is
Hausdorff: given any two distinct elements

\mu,\nu\in{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}
, there must be an open subset
U
of
X

such that
\mu(U)\neq\nu(U)
, say
\mu(U)<\nu(U)
. Let
r

be such that
\mu(U)<r<\nu(U)
. Since

r<\nu(U)=\sup\nolimits^{\scriptstyle\uparrow}_{C}\nu(\text{int}({C}))
, where
C
ranges over the
closed subsets of
U
, we have
r<\nu(\text{int}({C}))
for some
closed subset
C
of
U
. Then
\nu
is in
[\text{int}({C})>r]
,

\mu
is in
[X\smallsetminus C>1-r]
(since

1=\mu(X)=\mu((X\smallsetminus C)\cup U)\leq\mu(X\smallsetminus C)+\mu(U)<\mu(X\smallsetminus C)+r
, hence
\mu(X\smallsetminus C)>1-r
), and

[\text{int}({C})>r]
and
[X\smallsetminus C>1-r]
are disjoint; for the
latter, any probability valuation in the intersection must map the
disjoint union
\text{int}({C})\cup(X\smallsetminus C)
to a number strictly
larger than
r+(1-r)=1
, which is impossible. (One could show
that
{\mathbf{V}}_{1}X
is in fact regular Hausdorff, namely
T_{3}
, but we
will not need that.)

Every compact Hausdorff space
X
is regular, so the specialization
ordering on
{\mathbf{V}}_{1}X
is equality. Specializing
Remark
10.5
to this case, the weak distributive law

\lambda^{\natural}
on
\mathbf{KHaus}
maps every

\mu\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\delta_{E_{i}}
, where
n\geq 1
, and
each set
E_{i}
is finite and non-empty, to

\lambda^{\natural}_{X}(\mu)=\upc conv(\mathcal{E})\cap cl(conv(\mathcal{E}))
(where

\mathcal{E}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\{\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\delta_{x_{i}}\mid x_{1}\in E_{1},\cdots,x_{n}\in E_{n}\}
)

=conv(\mathcal{E})\cap cl(conv(\mathcal{E}))=conv(\mathcal{E})
. This is equivalent to the formula given by Goy and Petrişan
for their weak distributive law of the powerset monad over the
finite distribution monad
[
25
, Lemma 3.1]
. (Note that
their powerset includes the empty set, while our monad is one of
non-empty compact sets, but that seems to be a minor difference
here.)

12
Radon measures

We finish with a discussion of Radon measures. A
Radon measure

on a Hausdorff topological space
X
is a Borel measure
\mu
such
that for every Borel subset
B
of
X
, for every
\epsilon>0
,
there is a compact set
K_{\epsilon}
included in
B
such that

\mu(B\smallsetminus K_{\epsilon})\leq\epsilon

[
7
, Definition 7.1.1]
. One needs to take care to
modify this definition slightly on non-Hausdorff spaces

[
33
, Definition 7.2]
, if only because
K_{\epsilon}
may
fail to be Borel: a
Radon measure

\mu
on a coherent sober
space
X
is a Borel measure on a
\sigma
-algebra that contains the
patch topology on
X
, whose restriction to
{\mathcal{O}}{(X^{\text{{patch}}})}
is
locally finite (every point has a patch-open neighborhood of finite

\mu
-measure) and
inner regular
, meaning that

\mu(B)=\sup\nolimits^{\scriptstyle\uparrow}_{K\in\mathcal{C}X,K\subseteq B}\mu(K)
for
every Borel subset
B
; the collection
\mathcal{C}X
consists of the
intersections of non-empty families of finite unions of lenses. (See

A
for Keimel and Lawson’s definition, and why we changed it
to the current one.)
The
patch topology
on
X
is the coarsest topology that
contains the original topology on
X
, as well as the complements of
all compact saturated subsets of
X
. The patch topology coincides
with the original topology on
X
if
X
is Hausdorff, since in that
case every compact set is closed.

In the following, a
well-filtered
space
X
is a topological
space such that for every filtered family
{(Q_{i})}_{i\in I}
of
compact saturated subsets, for every open subset
U
of
Z
, if

\bigcap\nolimits^{\scriptstyle\downarrow}_{i\in I}Q_{i}\subseteq U
then
Q_{i}\subseteq U
for some

i\in I
. A family
{(Q_{i})}_{i\in I}
is
filtered
if and
only if it is non-empty, and for any two elements
Q_{i}
and
Q_{i^{\prime}}
,
there is an element
Q_{i^{\prime\prime}}
included in both. In a well-filtered
space
X
, for every filtered family
{(Q_{i})}_{i\in I}
,

\bigcap\nolimits^{\scriptstyle\downarrow}_{i\in I}Q_{i}
is compact saturated

[
16
, Proposition 8.3.6]
. Every sober space is
well-filtered
[
16
, Proposition 8.3.5]
.

Lemma 12.1

For every coherent well-filtered space
X
, every element of

\mathcal{C}X
is compact in the patch topology, hence in the
original topology on
X
.

In the sequel, we would only need to show that every element of

\mathcal{C}X
is compact in
X
, not in
X^{\text{{patch}}}
. But
Lemma
12.1
fixes a flaw. Let me explain.

Lemma 7.1 (5) of
[
33
]
states that, in a coherent sober
space
X
, every element of
\mathcal{C}X
is compact in the patch
topology, and the proof really only uses well-filteredness, not
sobriety. However, the result is obtained from the statement that
every patch-closed subset of
X
is the union of an element of

\mathcal{C}X
with a closed subset of
X

[
33
, Lemma 7.1 (4)]
, and that is wrong. For a
counterexample (to item (4), not (5), of that lemma), consider
\mathbb{R}

with its Scott topology for
X
. Then
X^{\text{{patch}}}
is
\mathbb{R}
with its
usual metric topology, while it is an easy exercise to show that the
elements of
\mathcal{C}X
are the compact subsets of
\mathbb{R}
(in
accordance with Lemma
12.1
; the easiest elementary
proof consists in verifying that every element of
\mathcal{C}X
is
closed and bounded in
\mathbb{R}
). Then any union of an element of

\mathcal{C}X
with a closed subset of
X
must be bounded from above.
Hence, for example,
\mathbb{Z}
, which is closed in

X^{\text{{patch}}}=\mathbb{R}
, is not of that form. The fixed proof below, which
is in the spirit of the original proof of

[
33
, Lemma 7.1 (5)]
, was communicated to me by Jimmie
Lawson on August 22nd, 2024.

Proof 32

Just as in
[
33
, Lemma 7.1]
, let the
lens
topology
be the topology on
X
whose closed subsets are the
intersections of finite unions of lenses. We will write
X^{\ell}

for
X
with the lens topology. The closed subsets of
X^{\ell}
are
the elements of
\mathcal{C}X
, plus possibly
X
itself (the empty
intersection) if
X
is not compact. It is clear that the lens
topology is coarser than the patch topology. It is a consequence of
Alexander’s subbase lemma that
X^{\ell}
is compact (see

[
33
, Lemma 7.1 (2)]
).

We now claim that:
(*)
for every
K\in\mathcal{C}X
, the lens
and the patch topologies on
X
induce the same subspace topology on

K
. Since the lens topology is coarser than the patch topology on

X
, the same inclusion holds for their subspace topologies on
K
.
Conversely, we must show that given any patch-closed subset
D
of

X
,
D\cap K
is closed in
K
seen as a subspace of
X^{\ell}
.
Since patch-closed subsets are intersections of finite unions of
closed subsets and of compact saturated subsets of
X
, it suffices
to show this when
D
is either closed or compact saturated in
X
.

Let us write
K
as

\bigcap_{i\in I}\bigcup_{j=1}^{n_{i}}(Q_{ij}\cap C_{ij})
where

I\neq\emptyset
, each
Q_{ij}
is compact saturated and each

C_{ij}
is closed in
X
.

If
D
is a closed subset
C
of
X
, then

C\cap K=\bigcap_{i\in I}\bigcup_{j=1}^{n_{i}}(Q_{ij}\cap C\cap C_{ij})
. Every set
Q_{ij}\cap C\cap C_{ij}
is a lens (or
empty), so
C\cap K
is in
\mathcal{C}X
, hence closed in

X^{\ell}
. Then
C\cap K
is also equal to the intersection of
itself with
K
, hence is closed in
K
as a subspace of
X^{\ell}
.

If
D
is a compact saturated subset
Q
of
X
, then similarly

Q\cap K=\bigcap_{i\in I}\bigcup_{j=1}^{n_{i}}(Q\cap Q_{ij}\cap C_{ij})
. Since
X
is coherent, each set
Q\cap Q_{ij}
is
compact saturated in
X
, so
Q\cap Q_{ij}\cap C_{ij}
is a lens
(or is empty). Therefore
Q\cap K
is in
\mathcal{C}X
, hence is
closed in
X^{\ell}
. Then
Q\cap K
is also equal to the
intersection of that set
Q\cap K
with
K
, hence is closed in
K

as a subspace of
X^{\ell}
. This ends the proof of claim
(*)
.

Using
(*)
, we can now show that every
K\in\mathcal{C}X
is
compact in
X^{\text{{patch}}}
.
K
is closed in
X^{\ell}
, hence it is also
closed in
K
, seen as a subspace of
X^{\ell}
. Since
X^{\ell}
is
compact,
K
is compact in
X^{\ell}
, hence also in
K
, seen as a
subspace of
X^{\ell}
. But, by
(*)
, the latter is also equal to

K
, see as a subspace of
X^{\text{{patch}}}
. Any subset of a subspace is
compact in the surrounding space, so
K
is compact in
X^{\text{{patch}}}
.

Finally, every compact subset of
X^{\text{{patch}}}
is compact in
X
: every
cover by open subsets from
X
is also an open cover by open subsets
from
X^{\text{{patch}}}
. ∎

Given any topological space
X
, the collection
\blacksquare Q
of
open neighborhoods of an arbitrary compact saturated subset
Q
is a
Scott-open subset of
{\mathcal{O}}X
. It follows that any union

\bigcup_{i\in I}\blacksquare{Q_{i}}
, where each
Q_{i}
is compact
saturated in
X
, is Scott-open in
{\mathcal{O}}X
. A space is

consonant
if and only if all the Scott-open subsets of

{\mathcal{O}}X
are of this form. The notion arises from

[
11
]
, where it was proved that every regular
Čech-complete space is consonant; every locally compact space is
consonant, too, as well as every LCS-complete space
[
10
, Proposition
12.1]
.

Proposition 12.2

For
\bullet
equal to “
\leq 1
”, resp. “
1
”, for every
coherent sober space
X
, let
\mathbf{R}_{\bullet}X
be the set of
Radon subprobability (resp. probability) measures on
X
, with the

weak topology
, defined by subbasic open sets

\{\mu\in\mathbf{R}_{\bullet}X\mid\mu(U)>r\}
,
U\in{\mathcal{O}}X
,

r\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
.

If
X
is consonant, weakly Hausdorff, coherent and sober (e.g., if

X
is stably locally compact), then restriction to
{\mathcal{O}}X

defines a homeomorphism between
\mathbf{R}_{\bullet}X
and

{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X
.

Proof 33

Every Radon measure
\mu
on the
\sigma
-algebra generated by the
patch topology on a coherent sober space
X
has a restriction
\nu

to
{\mathcal{O}}X
that is clearly strict and modular. We claim that it
is
tight
, namely that for every open subset
U
, for every

r<\nu(U)
, there is a compact saturated subset
Q
of
U
such
that
r<\nu(V)
for every open neighborhood of
V
. Let

U\in{\mathcal{O}}X
and let
r<\nu(U)
; equivalently,
r<\mu(U)
.
By definition of Radon measures, there is a proper closed subset
K

of
X
in the lens topology such that
K\subseteq U
and

r<\mu(K)
. By Lemma
12.1
,
K
is compact in

X
, so
Q\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\upc K
is compact saturated in
X
. Since
U
is
upwards-closed,
Q\subseteq U
, and we have

r<\mu(K)\leq\mu(Q)\leq\mu(V)=\nu(V)
for every open
neighborhood
V
of
Q
. We now use the fact that every tight map
from
{\mathcal{O}}X
to
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
is Scott-continuous

[
19
, Lemma 6.2]
, so
\nu
is a continuous
valuation.

In the converse direction, by
[
33
, Theorem 7.3]
,
every locally finite, tight valuation
\nu
on a weakly Hausdorff
coherent sober space
X
extends to a unique Radon measure on the

\sigma
-algebra generated by the patch topology. In this sentence,
that
\nu
is locally finite means that every point
x
has an open
neighborhood
U
such that
\nu(U)<\infty
; this is certainly the
case for all (sub)probability valuations. Every continuous
valuation
\nu
on a consonant space is tight

[
19
, Lemma 6.2]
. Hence every (sub)probability
valuation on a consonant, weakly Hausdorff, coherent sober space
extends to a unique Radon measure on the
\sigma
-algebra generated
by the patch topology.

In conclusion, restriction to
{\mathcal{O}}X
defines a bijection of

\mathbf{R}_{\bullet}X
onto
{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}X
. It is clear that it is
a homeomorphism. ∎

It follows that there is a monad of Radon (sub)probability measures

\mathbf{R}_{\bullet}
on the category of stably compact spaces, which is
isomorphic to the monad
{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}
of (sub)probability valuations.
(We recall that
{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}
preserves stable compactness;
otherwise, the previous sentence makes no sense.)

Remark
11.5
then entails the following.

Proposition 12.3

Let
\bullet
be “
\leq 1
” or “
1
”. There is a weak
distributive law of
\mathcal{P}\ell^{\mathrm{q}}_{\mathsf{V}}
(equivalently,
\mathcal{P}\ell_{\mathcal{V}}
) over

\mathbf{R}_{\bullet}
(equivalently,
{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}
) on the category
of stably compact spaces, and it induces the composite monad

(\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet},\eta^{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}},\mu^{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}})
.

Similarly, Remark
11.6
entails the following.

Proposition 12.4

Let
\bullet
be “
\leq 1
” or “
1
”. There is a weak
distributive law of the Vietoris monad over
\mathbf{R}_{\bullet}

(equivalently,
{\mathbf{V}}_{\bullet}
) on
\mathbf{KHaus}
, and it induces
the composite monad
(\mathbb{P}_{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}}^{\bullet},\eta^{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}},\mu^{{\mathtt{A}}{\mathtt{D}}{\mathtt{P}}})
.

Competing interests

The author declares none.

Appendix A
Uniqueness of Radon measures

There is a gap in the proof of
[
33
, Theorem 7.3]
. It
is claimed that, for a weakly Hausdorff coherent sober space
X
,
every tight, locally finite valuation
\nu
on
X
extends to a unique
Radon measure on a
\sigma
-algebra
\Sigma
containing the patch
topology of
X
. While existence—the difficult part—holds,
uniqueness is dealt rather briefly in the final sentence of the proof
(“Clearly a Radon measure is uniquely determined by its values on

\mathcal{C}
”; the set
\mathcal{C}
is what we call
\mathcal{C}X
).
But that only says that the extension is determined uniquely from its
values on sets in
\mathcal{C}X
, and no reason is given as to why the
latter values would be determined uniquely from
\nu
.

In trying to find a fix, we needed to change the definition of Radon
measures slightly. We define Radon measures on coherent sober spaces
as measures on a
\sigma
-algebra
\Sigma
containing the patch
topology, whose restrictions to
{\mathcal{O}}{(X^{\text{{patch}}})}
are locally
finite, and which are inner regular. Keimel and Lawson’s definition

[
33
, Definition 7.2]
is a measure
\mu
on
\Sigma

that is inner regular and satisfying the following property, instead
of local finiteness:

(Fin)
\mu(K)<\infty
for every
K\in\mathcal{C}X
.

Every measure
\mu
whose restriction to
{\mathcal{O}}{(X^{\text{{patch}}})}
is
locally finite satisfies (Fin): for each
x\in K
, find a patch-open
neighborhood
O_{x}
of
x
such that
\mu(O_{x})<\infty
; finitely
many cover
K
, since
K
is patch-compact
(Lemma
12.1
), and their union, which contains
K
,
has finite
\mu
-measure. The converse fails, even for inner regular
measures. Consider
Appert space

[
4
, Chapter VIII, § II,
p.84]
, see also

[
43
, Counterexample 98]
. This is

\mathbb{N}\smallsetminus\{0\}
with the following topology: its open subsets are
all the subsets that do not contain
1
, plus all the subsets
U

containing
1
whose asymptotic density

\liminf_{n\to\infty}\mathrm{card}\;(U\cap\{1,\cdots,n\})/n

equals
1
. This is a Hausdorff, hence certainly weakly Hausdorff,
coherent and sober space, whose compact subsets are exactly the finite
subsets of
\mathbb{N}
. Then the counting measure, which maps every subset
of
\mathbb{N}
to its cardinality, is inner regular and satisfies property
(Fin), but is not locally finite since every open neighborhood of
1

is infinite.

Now, the extension of
\nu
built by Keimel and Lawson—call it

\mu
—does not just satisfy property (Fin), but satisfies the
stronger property of being locally finite on
{\mathcal{O}}{(X^{\text{{patch}}})}
.
Indeed, for every
x\in X
, since
\nu
is locally finite, there is
an open neighborhood
U
of
x
in
X
such that
\nu(U)<\infty
;
then
U
is also patch-open, and
\nu(U)=\mu(U)
.

We now claim that there can be only one Radon measure
\mu
(in our
sense) on the patch space that extends
\nu
, provided that
X
is
weakly Hausdorff, coherent, and well-filtered. As Keimel and Lawson
argue, the value of
\mu
on Borel sets is entirely determined by its
values on elements
K
of
\mathcal{C}X
, by inner regularity. In
proving that local finiteness implies (Fin), we have actually shown a
bit more: there is a patch-open neighborhood
O
of
K
such that

\mu(O)<\infty
. Then
\mu(K)=\mu(O)-\mu(O\smallsetminus K)
; the
difference makes sense because

\mu(O\smallsetminus K)\leq\mu(O)<\infty
. We use inner regularity a
second time, and we express
\mu(O\smallsetminus K)
as

\sup\nolimits^{\scriptstyle\uparrow}_{K^{\prime}}\mu(K^{\prime})
, where
K^{\prime}
ranges over the elements of

\mathcal{C}X
included in
O\smallsetminus K
. Then

\mu(K)=\inf\nolimits^{\scriptstyle\downarrow}_{K^{\prime}}(\mu(O)-\mu(K^{\prime}))=\inf\nolimits^{\scriptstyle\downarrow}_{K^{\prime}}\mu(O\smallsetminus K^{\prime})
. Each set
K^{\prime}
is patch-closed, so
O\smallsetminus K^{\prime}
is patch-open.
Hence
\mu(K)
is entirely determined by the values of
\mu
on the
patch-open sets.

We argue that the restriction
\underline{\smash{\mu}}
of
\mu
to the
patch-open subsets of
X
is a continuous valuation on
X^{\text{{patch}}}
. It
is clearly strict and modular, and Scott-continuity is proved as
follows. Let
{(O_{i})}_{i\in I}
be a directed family of patch-open
subsets of
X
, and let
O
be its union. Then

\underline{\smash{\mu}}(O)=\mu(O)=\sup\nolimits^{\scriptstyle\uparrow}_{K\subseteq O}\mu(K)
,
where
K
(implicitly) ranges over
\mathcal{C}X
. Since
K
is
patch-compact (Lemma
12.1
),

K\subseteq O=\bigcup\nolimits^{\scriptstyle\uparrow}_{i\in I}O_{i}
is equivalent to the existence
of an
i\in I
such that
K\subseteq O_{i}
. Therefore

\underline{\smash{\mu}}(O)=\sup\nolimits^{\scriptstyle\uparrow}_{i\in I,K\subseteq O_{i}}\mu(K)=\sup\nolimits^{\scriptstyle\uparrow}_{i\in I}\mu(O_{i})=\sup\nolimits^{\scriptstyle\uparrow}_{i\in I}\underline{\smash{\mu}}(O_{i})
. In the previous paragraph, we have argued that
\mu
is
determined uniquely as a function of
\underline{\smash{\mu}}
, and we
will show that
\underline{\smash{\mu}}
is determined uniquely from

\nu
.

The family
\mathcal{F}_{\nu}
of open subsets
U
(in the original
topology on
X
) such that
\nu(U)<\infty
is directed, because it
contains the empty set and is closed under binary unions, using
modularity. The fact that
\nu
is locally finite means that

\bigcup\mathcal{F}_{\nu}=X
.

Every patch-open set is a union of sets of the form
U\smallsetminus Q
with

U
open and
Q
compact saturated in
X
(with the original
topology). Since
\nu
is locally finite, we can write
U\smallsetminus Q
as
the union of the sets
U\cap U_{0}\smallsetminus Q
,
U_{0}\in\mathcal{F}_{\nu}
.
We note that
U\cap U_{0}
is itself in
\mathcal{F}_{\nu}
. Hence every
patch-open set is also a union of sets from the collection

\mathcal{B}
of sets of the form
U\smallsetminus Q
with

U\in\mathcal{F}_{\nu}
(not just
U\in{\mathcal{O}}X
) and
Q
compact
saturated in
X
. Namely,
\mathcal{B}
forms a subbase of the patch
topology. Additionally,
\mathcal{B}
is closed under finite
intersections, and in particular forms a base.

With these observations, we claim that
\underline{\smash{\mu}}
is
uniquely determined by its values on elements of
\mathcal{B}
. For
every patch-open set
O
, written as a union
\bigcup_{i\in I}B_{i}

of elements of
\mathcal{B}
, hence as a directed union

\bigcup\nolimits^{\scriptstyle\uparrow}_{J\in\mathbb{P}_{\mathrm{fin}}(I)}\bigcup_{j\in J}B_{j}
,

\underline{\smash{\mu}}(O)
is equal to

\sup\nolimits^{\scriptstyle\uparrow}_{J\in\mathbb{P}_{\mathrm{fin}}(I)}\underline{\smash{\mu}}(\bigcup_{j\in J}B_{j})
, hence is uniquely determined from the values that

\underline{\smash{\mu}}
takes on finite unions
\bigcup_{j\in J}B_{j}

of elements of
\mathcal{B}
. If

\underline{\smash{\mu}}(B_{j})=\infty
for some
j\in J
, then we
must have
\underline{\smash{\mu}}(\bigcup_{j\in J}B_{j})=\infty
,
since
\underline{\smash{\mu}}
is monotonic. Otherwise, we can rewrite

\underline{\smash{\mu}}(\bigcup_{j\in J}B_{j})
as

\sum_{K\subseteq J,K\neq\emptyset}{(-1)}^{|K|+1}\underline{\smash{\mu}}(\bigcap_{j\in K}B_{j})
(this is the
inclusion-exclusion formula, a consequence of modularity), and we
recall that
\bigcap_{j\in K}B_{j}\in\mathcal{B}
. Hence in both
cases, the value of
\underline{\smash{\mu}}(\bigcup_{j\in J}B_{j})
is
uniquely determined by the values that
\underline{\smash{\mu}}
takes
on elements of
\mathcal{B}
.

It remains to see that the value
\underline{\smash{\mu}}(U\smallsetminus Q)
is entirely
determined by
\nu
for every set
U\smallsetminus Q
in
\mathcal{B}
. We
will show that

\underline{\smash{\mu}}(U\smallsetminus Q)=\sup\nolimits^{\scriptstyle\uparrow}_{V\supseteq Q}(\nu(U)-\nu(U\cap V))
, where
V
ranges over
{\mathcal{O}}X
. We note that the difference

\nu(U)-\nu(U\cap V)
makes sense because

\nu(U\cap V)\leq\nu(U)<\infty
.

To this end, it is enough to show that for every
r\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that

r<\underline{\smash{\mu}}(U\smallsetminus Q)
, there is an open neighborhood

V
of
Q
such that
r\leq\nu(U)-\nu(U\cap V)
. This will
show that

\underline{\smash{\mu}}(U\smallsetminus Q)\leq\sup\nolimits^{\scriptstyle\uparrow}_{V\supseteq Q}(\nu(U)-\nu(U\cap V))
, and the reverse inequality is clear.

Since
\mu
is inner regular and
\underline{\smash{\mu}}
is a restriction of
\mu

(hence, in particular,
r<\mu(U\smallsetminus Q)
) there is an element

K\in\mathcal{C}X
such that
K\subseteq U\smallsetminus Q
and

r\leq\mu(K)
. Now
K
is an intersection

\bigcap_{i\in I}\bigcup_{j=1}^{n_{i}}L_{ij}
where
I
is non-empty
and each
L_{ij}
is a lens, and we can therefore rewrite
K
as a
filtered intersection
\bigcap\nolimits^{\scriptstyle\downarrow}_{J\in\mathbb{P}_{\mathrm{fin}}^{*}(I)}K_{J}
, where

\mathbb{P}_{\mathrm{fin}}^{*}(I)
denotes the collection of non-empty finite subsets of

I
, and
K_{J}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\bigcap_{i\in J}\bigcup_{j=1}^{n_{i}}L_{ij}
.
By distributing intersections of unions, and realizing that any finite
non-empty intersection of lenses is a lens (or the empty set), we see
that
K_{J}
is a finite union of lenses. From
K\subseteq U\smallsetminus Q
,
we deduce that
\bigcap\nolimits^{\scriptstyle\downarrow}_{J\in\mathbb{P}_{\mathrm{fin}}^{*}(I)}K_{J}\smallsetminus(U\smallsetminus Q)
is
empty. But
K_{J}\smallsetminus(U\smallsetminus Q)=(K_{J}\smallsetminus U)\cup(K_{J}\cap Q)

is also a finite union of lenses, because
L\smallsetminus U
and
L\cap Q

are lenses (or empty) for every lens
L
. (The second claim relies on
the coherence of
X
.) By Lemma
12.1
, each set

K_{J}\smallsetminus(U\smallsetminus Q)
is then patch-compact, and is patch-closed.
Now any filtered family of compact closed sets whose intersection is
empty contains the empty set; this is an easy exercise using the
definition of compactness. Therefore
K_{J}\smallsetminus(U\smallsetminus Q)
is empty
for some
J\in\mathbb{P}_{\mathrm{fin}}^{*}(I)
. In other words,

K_{J}\subseteq U\smallsetminus Q
. We have
K\subseteq K_{J}
, so

r\leq\mu(K_{J})
. Therefore, replacing
K
by
K_{J}
if necessary,
we may assume that
K
itself is a finite union of lenses, say

\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}(Q_{i}\cap C_{i})
, where each
Q_{i}
is compact
saturated and
C_{i}
is closed in
X
, in such a way that

r\leq\mu(K)
and
K\subseteq U\smallsetminus Q
.

From
K\subseteq U\smallsetminus Q
, we obtain that

Q_{i}\cap C_{i}\subseteq U\smallsetminus Q
for every
i\in\{1,\cdots,n\}
,
namely that
Q_{i}\subseteq U\cup U_{i}
, where
U_{i}
is the complement
of
C_{i}
, and
Q\cap Q_{i}\subseteq U
. Since
X
is weakly
Hausdorff,
Q
has an open neighborhood
V_{i}
and
Q_{i}
has an open
neighborhood
W_{i}
such that
V_{i}\cap W_{i}\subseteq U_{i}
, for every

i
. Then

Q_{i}\cap C_{i}\subseteq W_{i}\cap C_{i}\subseteq X\smallsetminus V_{i}
, for every

i\in\{1,\cdots,n\}
. We let
V\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\bigcap_{i=1}^{n}V_{i}
.
Then
K\subseteq X\smallsetminus V
. We also have
K\subseteq U
, so

K\subseteq U\smallsetminus V
. From
r\leq\mu(K)
, we deduce that

r\leq\mu(U\smallsetminus V)
. Now

\mu(U\smallsetminus V)=\mu(U)-\mu(U\cap V)
, an equality that makes
sense because all the sets involved have finite
\nu
-measure,
equivalently finitely
\mu
-measure, since
\mu
extends
\nu
. Then

\mu(U\smallsetminus V)=\nu(U)-\nu(U\cap V)
, and this completes the
proof.
